# Upgrade Batch 7 — Infrastructure and delivery: Phase 1 analysis

**Written 2026-07-29.** This file is the working input Phase 2 executes from. It is not a report to
be read once; it is the specification a writer works from without re-reading the originals.

**Membership.** `alaa-docker-production`, `alaa-k8s-helm`, `alaa-gitlab-ci-cd`, `alaa-haproxy`,
`alaa-makefile`, `caas-arvan-kuber`, `ansible-generator`, `ansible-validator`. Eight skills,
808 KB on disk, 59 KB of always-loaded body.

**Method.** Every file of every skill was read in full — 154 files across the eight directories,
including all twenty-seven bundled scripts read as code rather than skimmed as prose. Two live
repositories were read as ground truth for what these skills actually govern: `service-ci-kit`
(the fleet's GitLab platform template, its eleven CI scripts, and RFC 0001, its own statement of
gate policy) and `service-runtime-kit` (the 77 KB generator that emits every Laravel service's
Dockerfile, Compose file and Swarm stack). Nineteen HAProxy configs, three Makefiles and six Ansible
playbooks were written as probes and the shipped validators run against them; where a checker could
be executed, it was, and its actual output is quoted rather than its documentation. Every
version-sensitive claim was checked against the vendor's own current documentation on 2026-07-29.

---

## 1. The batch-level survey, before any individual skill

Three mechanical measurements were taken across all eight directories before analysis began, because
the previous four waves each found that the batch-level pattern mattered more than any single skill.

### 1.1 The doctrine-owner silence, for the fifth wave running

The programme maintains twenty doctrine and platform owners that a stack skill is supposed to route
to rather than restate. Counting how many of the eight skills name each one, anywhere, across 808 KB:

| Owner | Skills naming it |
|---|---|
| `alaa-reliability-sla` | **0** |
| `alaa-testing-strategy` | **0** |
| `alaa-system-design` | **0** |
| `alaa-project-constitution` | **0** |
| `alaa-controlled-ops` | **0** |
| `alaa-async-messaging` | **0** |
| `alaa-data-layer` | **0** |
| `alaa-low-noise` | **0** |
| `alaa-keyset-pagination` | **0** |
| `alaa-algorithms-data-structures` | **0** |
| `alaa-workflow` | **0** |
| `service-runtime-kit-governance` | **0** |
| **`alaa-frontend-devops`** | **0** |
| `alaa-observability-soc` | 1 |
| `alaa-services-contract` | 1 |
| `alaa-trust-gateway-auth` | 1 |
| `alaa-arvan-object-storage` | 1 |
| `alaa-security-review` | 2 |
| `alaa-prompting-guide` | 3 |
| `alaa-minio-object-storage` | 3 |

The last row of zeroes is the one that decides this batch's shape. **`alaa-frontend-devops` is named
by none of the eight**, which means decision D8 — the stack-versus-platform boundary the frontend
batch wrote and declared binding — exists in exactly one direction. Batch 6 wrote its half. The three
platform skills named in that ruling have not written theirs, and until they do the boundary is an
assertion rather than an agreement.

`service-runtime-kit-governance` at zero is the second finding of the same class:
`alaa-docker-production` governs the container layer of a fleet whose containers are entirely
generated by a kit it never mentions.

### 1.2 Trigger syntax — the batch fails it in both directions

| Skill | `$name` (Codex) | `/name` (Claude Code) | Visible from |
|---|---|---|---|
| `alaa-docker-production` | 5 | 1 | Codex mostly |
| `alaa-k8s-helm` | 2 | 2 | both |
| `alaa-gitlab-ci-cd` | 1 | 1 | both, but only one call site exists |
| `alaa-haproxy` | 6 | **0** | **Codex only** |
| `alaa-makefile` | 5 | 8 | both |
| `caas-arvan-kuber` | 3 | 12 | both |
| `ansible-generator` | 12 | 3 | Codex mostly |
| `ansible-validator` | **0** | 17 | **Claude Code only** |

Counts exclude `agents/openai.yaml`, which is Codex-only by design and correctly uses the bare `$`.
Batch 6 found this defect in one direction only — 197 `$` against 2 `/`. This batch fails it in
*both* directions, which is worse, because a reader who learns the rule from one skill will apply it
backwards in another. `alaa-haproxy` is unreachable from Claude Code at all six of its call sites;
`ansible-validator` is unreachable from Codex at all seventeen of its.

### 1.3 Shape

| Skill | Body | Pack | References | `00-topic-map.md` | Scripts |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `alaa-docker-production` | 3,989 | 16 KB | 3 | yes | **0** |
| `alaa-k8s-helm` | 9,207 | 83 KB | 9 | yes | 7 |
| `alaa-gitlab-ci-cd` | 10,623 | 72 KB | 8 | no | 2 |
| `alaa-haproxy` | 6,653 | 53 KB | 5 | yes | **0** |
| `alaa-makefile` | 7,359 | 231 KB | 13 | yes | 3 |
| `caas-arvan-kuber` | 16,078 | 62 KB | 5 | no | 3 |
| `ansible-generator` | 2,568 | 109 KB | 4 | no | **0** |
| `ansible-validator` | 2,636 | 182 KB | 6 | no | 10 |

The router convention from Batch 1 — eight or fewer references listed in the body, nine or more in a
separate topic map — is satisfied by `alaa-makefile` and `alaa-k8s-helm` and by
`ansible-generator`/`ansible-validator` and `alaa-gitlab-ci-cd`, which correctly have none. It is
violated in the harmless direction by `alaa-docker-production` (three references and a topic map) and
`alaa-haproxy` (five and a topic map). `caas-arvan-kuber` has five references, no topic map, and a
16 KB body — the largest body in the batch — which is the shape the convention exists to prevent.

No `__pycache__` and no `.fuse_hidden*` residue exists anywhere in the batch. Defect class 8 is clean.

## 2. The ten-criteria table for the whole batch

Verdicts follow the brief's vocabulary. `NOT-OWNED` was granted only where the skill names the other
owner at a call site in its own text; silence was scored `FAIL`. Per-cell evidence with line
citations is in the appendices.

| # | Criterion | docker-production | k8s-helm | gitlab-ci-cd | haproxy | makefile | caas-arvan | ansible-gen | ansible-val |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Correctness and testability | FAIL | FAIL | FAIL | FAIL | FAIL | FAIL | FAIL | FAIL |
| 2 | Failure behaviour | FAIL | FAIL | FAIL | FAIL | FAIL | FAIL | FAIL | FAIL |
| 3 | Security | FAIL | FAIL | **SAT** | FAIL | **SAT** | FAIL | FAIL | FAIL |
| 4 | Observability | FAIL | FAIL | FAIL | FAIL | FAIL | FAIL | FAIL | FAIL |
| 5 | Concurrency and load | FAIL | FAIL | FAIL | FAIL | **SAT** | FAIL | FAIL | FAIL |
| 6 | Clean code, SOLID, patterns | **SAT** | **SAT** | **SAT** | FAIL | **SAT** | FAIL | **SAT** | FAIL |
| 7 | Algorithm and data-structure choice | FAIL | FAIL | FAIL | FAIL | *NOT-OWNED: `alaa-bash-shell`* | *NOT-OWNED: `alaa-k8s-helm`* | FAIL | FAIL |
| 8 | Configurability | **SAT** | **SAT** | **SAT** | FAIL | **SAT** | **SAT** | **SAT** | FAIL |
| 9 | Speed and debuggability | FAIL | **SAT** | **SAT** | **SAT** | FAIL | **SAT** | FAIL | FAIL |
| 10 | Documentation | FAIL | FAIL | **SAT** | FAIL | FAIL | FAIL | **SAT** | **SAT** |
| | **Tally** | 2/8/0 | 3/7/0 | 5/5/0 | 1/9/0 | 4/5/1 | 2/7/1 | 3/7/0 | 1/9/0 |

**Eighty cells: 21 SATISFIED, 57 FAIL, 2 legitimately NOT-OWNED.**

Four rows fail in all eight skills, and they are the same four the frontend batch found failing in all
nine of its members: **correctness and testability, failure behaviour, observability**, and — one row
different from Batch 6 — **algorithm and data-structure choice** fails in six of eight with two
legitimate delegations. This is now a five-wave pattern, and the reading is no longer that individual
authors forgot: the quality bar's four hardest criteria are the four that require the author to state
a number, a budget, or a machine-checkable predicate, and a skill assembled from vendor documentation
never contains one.

**The two legitimate delegations are the model for the other seventy-eight cells.**
`alaa-makefile/SKILL.md:137` — "Pair with `$alaa-bash-shell` when recipe complexity moves into shell
logic" — and `caas-arvan-kuber/SKILL.md:168` — "Build or refine templates/manifests with
`alaa-k8s-helm`". Each names a skill, states the condition under which the other owns the subject, and
stops. That is what a `NOT-OWNED` cell looks like; everything else in this batch is silence.

## 3. Defect classes from section 3, as actually found

Only classes actually observed are listed. Each entry gives the count and the sharpest instance;
full evidence is in the appendices.

**Class 1 — stale model pins: NOT FOUND, and the carry-over is wrong about it.**
`UPGRADE-CARRYOVER.md:188` states that three stale hardcoded model names live in this batch, in
`alaa-gitlab-ci-cd`, `alaa-k8s-helm` and `caas-arvan-kuber`. Verified false for all three. Every
directory was searched exhaustively — bodies, references, scripts, assets, emitted templates and
`agents/openai.yaml` — and no model or effort key survives anywhere in the batch. All three skills
already carry the corrected pointer (`alaa-k8s-helm/SKILL.md:153`, `caas-arvan-kuber/SKILL.md:217`,
`alaa-gitlab-ci-cd/SKILL.md:175`). The pins were removed by an earlier sweep; the carry-over was not
updated. **Recorded here so a later batch does not go looking again.**

**Class 2 — wrong trigger syntax: found in eight of eight**, and in both directions. See §1.2.

**Class 3 — duplication between body and references: found in five of eight.**
Sharpest instance is not inside a skill but between two: `ansible-generator/references/best-practices.md`
and `ansible-validator/references/best_practices.md` teach the same sixteen subjects, and **on variable
precedence they contradict each other** — the generator's nine-item list places `set_fact` below task,
block and role vars; the validator's correct twenty-one-item list has it outranking all three. An agent
reading both gets contradictory guidance on the single question most likely to produce a wrong-value bug.

**Class 4 — project-specific content in an always-loaded body: found in `caas-arvan-kuber`**, whose
16 KB body is the largest in the batch and carries runner expression (`SKILL.md:387-390`) that belongs
to another skill entirely.

**Class 6 — descriptions that only say when to use: found in two of eight.** `alaa-haproxy` and
`caas-arvan-kuber` have no "do not use for" clause. The other six do, and
`ansible-generator`/`ansible-validator` carry the best pair of reciprocal negative clauses in the
repository — each names the other as its exclusion.

**Class 7 — fragile tooling: found across the executable surface.** Twenty-seven scripts ship in this
batch. Details in §4 below; the summary is that the two largest validators return a clean verdict on
input they cannot parse.

**Class 8 — shipped `__pycache__`: not found.** Clean across the batch.

**Class 11 — companion boundary: found in eight of eight.** No skill in this batch states what it does
not own in a form another skill could rely on. Five carry "pair with" lists, which name a companion
without transferring a subject; three carry nothing.

**Class 12 — two routers: not found.** No skill duplicates its topic map into its body. The convention
violations are the reverse — three skills with nine-or-fewer references correctly have no topic map,
and `caas-arvan-kuber` has neither a topic map nor a body small enough to serve as one.

**Class 13 — a rule with no tool that reports a violation.** This is the batch's defining defect and it
has two halves.

*Half one: three skills ship no executable check at all.* `alaa-docker-production` states roughly
seventy imperative sentences and ships zero checkers, against a live kit that ships two working ones it
does not know exist. `alaa-haproxy` mandates `haproxy -c -f` and ships nothing that runs it.
`ansible-generator` ships a scaffold and nothing that validates the scaffold — and six of its eight
template YAML files do not parse.

*Half two, and this is worse: the checkers that do ship report clean on input they cannot handle.*

- `alaa-gitlab-ci-cd/scripts/validate_gitlab_ci.py` returns `No issues found`, exit 0, on the fleet's
  entire production pipeline — the same file RFC 0001:13 opens by calling "no code gate of any kind".
  It reports `!reference` as a YAML syntax error and abandons the file; it cannot see `default:`, so
  the image, cache, `set -x` and secret checks never run; a registry-with-port image counts as pinned.
- `alaa-makefile/scripts/validate_makefile.sh` has six of nineteen checks dead from a single bug —
  `grep -E "^\t"` in double quotes matches the letter `t`, not a tab — and because every dead check
  inspects a recipe line, the validator cannot see what a target *does*. A Makefile whose deploy recipe
  fails at `cd /nonexistent` prints "still ran" and exits 0. Its sibling
  `add_standard_targets.sh` fails on 100% of invocations from a `set -e` plus `((n++))`-from-zero abort:
  it has never once worked, while `SKILL.md:109` advertises it.
- `ansible-validator/scripts/validate_role.sh:208` inverts its own YAML result — the `if !` reads
  `grep`'s status, not `yamllint`'s — so since every conventional Ansible file starts with `---`, the
  YAML stage of role validation **can never report a failure**. It printed five `syntax error` lines
  and then `✓ YAML syntax check passed`.
- `ansible-validator/assets/.ansible-lint:53` silently disables the two rules it explicitly enables:
  `task_name_prefix: "{path}:"` is an invalid key, ansible-lint swallows the `KeyError`, and `NameRule`
  dies. Measured: shipped config 19 failures and zero `name[missing]`; the same config with that one
  line removed, 21 failures plus both `name[missing]` warnings.
- `ansible-validator/scripts/scan_secrets.sh:129` uses ERE `{16}` under BRE `grep`, so an
  `AKIA…`-shaped key is not detected, while correctly vaulted playbooks are red-lighted.

**A fourteenth class this batch adds: the exit-code contract.** Across the batch, "could not run" and
"clean" are indistinguishable in every shipped checker but one. `alaa-makefile`'s validator returns 1
for both "passed with warnings" and "could not run"; every `ansible-validator` script returns 1 for "no
argument", "target not found", "tool unavailable" and "findings present" alike. A CI gate built on any
of them treats a missing tool as a pass. Batch 6 established that a checker must distinguish the two;
this batch is the evidence for why it is not a stylistic rule.

---

## 4. The five section-4 candidate skills, judged against what was read

**`alaa-design-patterns` — must not be built, and this batch adds evidence.** Nothing in eight
infrastructure skills wants a general pattern catalogue. What the batch actually lacks on criterion 6
is domain-shaped structural judgment — chart design in `alaa-k8s-helm`, config structure in
`alaa-haproxy`, the reuse ladder in `alaa-gitlab-ci-cd` — and each of those already sits inside its own
skill's boundary. Where criterion 6 passes in this batch, it passes because the skill stated its own
domain's structural rules, not because it cited a pattern.

**`alaa-algorithms-data-structures` — exists, and the finding is that nobody routes to it.** Not one of
the eight names it. Criterion 7 fails in six of eight, and in every one of those six the domain analogue
is concrete and stateable: a stick-table memory formula in `alaa-haproxy`, a critical-path and
fan-out budget for the job graph in `alaa-gitlab-ci-cd`, an image-size and layer-cache budget in
`alaa-docker-production`, `check_fqcn.sh`'s 94-name linear scan in `ansible-validator`. The skill is not
missing; the call sites are.

**`alaa-system-design` — exists, named by none of the eight.** The clearest place it belongs is
`alaa-k8s-helm`, whose failure-behaviour and concurrency gaps are design questions the skill already
owns. Again a routing gap, not a coverage gap.

**`alaa-reliability-sla` — exists, named by none of the eight, and its absence is the largest single
cause of the criterion-2 sweep.** Every one of the eight fails failure behaviour, and in most cases the
skill does have the surface — Swarm `update_config` and `rollback_config`, GitLab `retry:` and
`timeout:`, HAProxy peers behaviour when a peer drops, Ansible `until`/`retries` — but states it as a
YAML fragment with no doctrine behind it. The single highest-value edit available to this batch is a
call site to `alaa-reliability-sla` in each of the eight, followed by the concrete values that
doctrine's shape implies.

**`alaa-testing-strategy` — exists, named by none of the eight.** Relevant to the three skills that
ship checkers and to the two that ship none, because the question "what would a test that fails against
a plausible broken implementation look like here" is exactly what every one of the six broken checkers
above would have failed.

**Conclusion on section 4: all five candidates are resolved. Four exist and are unrouted; one must not
be built.** The correct action in this batch is call sites, not construction.

---

## 5. Is any new skill genuinely required?

**No. All six analysis lanes reached this independently, and the batch-level answer is the same.**

Four candidate gaps were examined seriously and each was rejected for a stated reason.

**The fleet safety-control variable register** — the list of variable names for which a `:-` default is
a defect regardless of syntax — genuinely has no owner today. But it has no owner because nobody has
written it, not because writing it would violate a boundary. The ratification names
`alaa-docker-production` as the invariant's owner for Compose authorship, and the register is a
statement about interpolation form, which is precisely that skill's ground against
`service-runtime-kit-governance`'s ownership of variable naming. It becomes
`alaa-docker-production/references/25-fail-closed-interpolation.md`. Splitting it into its own skill
would reproduce the exact failure this batch found live in the kit: an invariant enforced syntactically
by one party and semantically by nobody.

**An executable checker for rendered Kubernetes manifests** does not exist in either
`alaa-k8s-helm` or `caas-arvan-kuber`, and nothing today can fail a Deployment that ships with no
resources, no probes, `privileged: true` and a mutated selector. But that is a script, and it belongs in
`alaa-k8s-helm/scripts/` beside the two already there, with `caas-arvan-kuber` contributing Arvan-only
predicates. A skill whose entire content is one script is not a skill.

**Ansible Vault mechanics** — vault IDs, `encrypt_string`, rekeying, `no_log` interaction with
`--diff` — are substantially uncovered (`ansible-validator/references/security_checklist.md:64-85` is
five bullets). They fit inside `ansible-validator` without straining it, because verifying that a
secret is vaulted is a validation act, and the fail-closed *decision* already has an owner in
`alaa-security-review`.

**The D8 ruling's silence on configuration management** — the boundary names CI, containers and the
proxy, and Ansible is a fourth delivery mechanism it does not cover — is a ruling gap, not a skill gap.
It closes with seam sentences that four existing owners each write from their own side.

**One structural recommendation stands in place of a new skill.** Two of the eight skills ship no
executable check and four ship broken ones. The batch's largest deliverable is not text; it is that
every skill in it leaves Phase 2 with at least one checker that has `--help`, a `--self-test`, an exit
code distinguishing "could not run" from "clean", and a demonstrated finding against a real artifact in
one of the two live kits. That is a property of the eight existing skills, not a ninth.

---

## 6. Two boundary questions this batch settles

### 6.1 `alaa-k8s-helm` versus `caas-arvan-kuber`: two skills, with a deciding test

Keep two. Three sibling skills already route to `caas-arvan-kuber` by name
(`alaa-docker-production/SKILL.md:56`, `alaa-makefile/SKILL.md:141`, `alaa-haproxy/SKILL.md:88`), and
`alaa-makefile:141-142` discriminates between the two on consecutive lines. The deciding test both
skills state, in the same words:

> **Load `/caas-arvan-kuber` (`$caas-arvan-kuber`) only when the answer depends on a fact that is true
> of Arvan CaaS and false of stock Kubernetes at the same minor version. Otherwise load
> `/alaa-k8s-helm` (`$alaa-k8s-helm`) — including when the target cluster happens to be Arvan.**

`caas-arvan-kuber`'s runner material at `SKILL.md:387-390` is runner expression and moves to
`alaa-gitlab-ci-cd`, which claims that surface in its own description and covers none of it today. Its
gate material at `:399-403` becomes part of the Kubernetes and Helm gate register, which is
`alaa-k8s-helm/references/validation-workflows.md` — a file that already contains the register in
everything but the name.

### 6.2 `ansible-generator` versus `ansible-validator`: two skills, with a deciding test

Keep two, and move the shared ground into the validator. The merge case is real — `alaa-k8s-helm` is
this repository's own precedent for merging a generator/validator/debugger triple — and it fails on
three counts: the trigger discrimination here works and not one of the forty-odd findings is a
mis-triggering finding; the two halves have different consumers (a human scaffolding a role, and a
headless CI job that cares only about an exit code); and the merge's only real benefit, deduplicating
`best_practices`, is achieved by assigning ownership rather than by merging. The deciding test both
skills state, in the same words:

> **Does a file that does not yet exist have to exist when you are done? If yes,
> `/ansible-generator` (`$ansible-generator`). If every file you need already exists and your job is to
> produce a verdict about it, `/ansible-validator` (`$ansible-validator`).**

The per-subject ownership split for the eleven duplicated subjects is in Appendix G/H's shared closing
and is binding on both sides.

---

## 7. The D8 reciprocal half — the three sentences Phase 2 writes

Decision D8 is binding and one-directional today. Each of the three platform skills states its own half
in its own words. The exact sentences, with their line-by-line dispositions, are in Appendix A §10,
Appendix B §10 and Appendix E §10; in summary:

- **`alaa-gitlab-ci-cd`** absorbs cache-key expression (`cache: key: files:`, `policy:`,
  `fallback_keys`), the job graph, `rules:`/`needs:`, artifact retention and `expire_in`, and the runner
  image reference — four subjects on which it currently has **zero occurrences of the relevant keyword
  anywhere in the skill** — and states that it decides no gate. Its `SKILL.md:63` currently duplicates
  frontend gate 2 and must be restated as expression.
- **`alaa-docker-production`** absorbs the five Dockerfile rules moving in from the frontend batch —
  layer ordering by manifest and lockfile, layer-invalidation avoidance, multi-stage separation, image
  minimisation, and not copying the full repo before dependency install — states that it decides no
  gate, and additionally states its seam with `service-runtime-kit-governance`: the governance skill
  owns which generator variable expresses a thing, this skill owns how the image or Compose file is
  expressed.
- **`alaa-haproxy`** states that it owns how a cache or routing decision is expressed as a directive and
  decides no policy. This is a genuinely new capability for it: the skill has **no caching content
  whatsoever** — zero matches for cache, `max-age`, compression or rewrite across all thirty-seven
  files — so its half of the ruling is written from nothing rather than restated. Its two CI files
  (`examples/github-actions/haproxy-validate.yml`, `examples/gitlab-ci/gitlab-ci-snippet.yml`) are
  provider YAML that now belongs to `alaa-gitlab-ci-cd` and retire.

**`alaa-makefile` adds a fourth half the ruling does not name**, and it should: a Make target is a
*local invocation* of a command whose definition is owned elsewhere, and this skill's job is to make the
local verdict identical to the runner's verdict. `alaa-gitlab-ci-cd` owns how that command is expressed
on a runner; `alaa-docker-production` owns the Compose file the target fronts.

---

## 8. Findings outside the eight skills

### 8.1 In the live repositories — RFC candidates, not skill edits

Thirty-one defects were found in `service-ci-kit` and `service-runtime-kit`, recorded in full in the
appendices. The five most serious:

1. **`service-runtime-kit`: the fail-closed interpolation RFC was acted on syntactically and not
   semantically.** Every `APP_KEY` is now `${APP_KEY:?…}` (`render-runtime.sh:1218,1312,1379,1655,1712`),
   `up-local.sh:85-87` aborts before Docker runs, and a 179-line regression test enforces the form with
   correct `$$`-escape and comment handling. But rendering the kit's own fixture still produces
   `DB_PASSWORD: ${DB_PASSWORD:-}`, `RABBITMQ_PASSWORD: ${RABBITMQ_PASSWORD:-app}` and
   `DB_PROVISION_ADMIN_PASSWORD: ${DB_PROVISION_ADMIN_PASSWORD:-postgres}` in the production and Swarm
   files, and `validate-runtime.sh` exits 0 on all three. **The half of the invariant that requires a
   judgment about which variable is a safety control was implemented nowhere.**
2. **`service-runtime-kit`: the generated Swarm stack has no rollout control** — no `update_config`,
   `rollback_config`, `replicas` or healthcheck on any long-lived service — so `stack deploy` defaults
   to `order: stop-first` on one replica. That is a full outage per service per deploy, against a
   99.99% target.
3. **`service-ci-kit`: `interruptible: true` is inherited onto `migrate_db`.** A newer pipeline on the
   same ref cancels a running database migration mid-flight.
4. **`service-ci-kit`: `pg_dump` output is exfiltrated through `kubectl logs`** into a week-retained
   artifact, and can be silently truncated.
5. **`service-ci-kit`: a personal access token is written into `.git/config`** on a persistent shell
   runner.

### 8.2 In skills outside the batch

- **`alaa-haproxy-lua` is assigned to no batch in the carry-over plan** and is named at no call site in
  `alaa-haproxy`. A grep for `haproxy-lua` across all thirty-seven files of `alaa-haproxy` returns
  nothing, while `SKILL.md:12` declares that skill "the HAProxy source of truth for this pack" — so the
  silence reads as authority, and an agent would conclude that HAProxy Lua is `alaa-haproxy`'s own
  subject. The in-batch half of the fix is a row in `alaa-haproxy`'s boundary table; the reciprocal half
  and the batch assignment are the owner's.
- **`UPGRADE-CARRYOVER.md:188` is wrong about the three model pins** (see §3, class 1).
- **`ansible-validator/references/playbook.md:1107` names `terraform-validator`, which does not exist**
  anywhere in the repository. In batch; deleting the pointer is in scope.
- No edit to `alaa-frontend-devops` is required. Its half of D8 is stated correctly at `SKILL.md:16` and
  `references/90-companion-boundary.md:15-16`, with both trigger forms, and it already routes Dockerfile
  authorship and cache-key syntax away. Once the three reciprocal sentences are written the boundary is
  agreed from both sides.

## 9. Phase 2 lane plan

Six writing lanes, each on the escalated implementer, none overlapping another's files.

| Lane | Skills | Principal deliverable |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | `alaa-docker-production` | Fourteen references from three; the D8 and governance seams; the fleet safety-control register; four checkers. The one skill in the batch that must grow several-fold. |
| 2 | `alaa-gitlab-ci-cd` | The four D8-routed subjects it owns and has no text on; repair the validator's four parser defects; re-baseline to the current GitLab line. |
| 3 | `alaa-k8s-helm` + `caas-arvan-kuber` | The deciding test in both; move runner expression out; name the gate register; the manifest checker; re-baseline the Arvan capability matrix. |
| 4 | `alaa-haproxy` | The `defaults` association rule; the peers failure semantics; the caching-directive half of D8; re-baseline to the current LTS; the first checkers this skill has ever had. |
| 5 | `alaa-makefile` | Repair seven dead checks and a script that has never run; state what a Makefile is for on this fleet; redistribute the C/C++ material. |
| 6 | `ansible-generator` + `ansible-validator` | The deciding test in both; the eleven-subject ownership split; repair the inverted grep, the invalid lint key and the BRE/ERE bug; make the scaffold parse. |

Lanes are independent and dispatch together. The only cross-lane dependency is that lanes 1, 2 and 4
each write one sentence of the same ruling, and those three sentences are already fixed in this
document, so no lane waits on another.

---

The appendices follow, one per skill, in the order A–H.

---

## Appendix A — `alaa-docker-production`

### 1. What this skill is today

Five files, 16 KB total, at `/mnt/user-data/uploads/skills/skills/sohrab/alaa-docker-production/`:

| File | Lines | Bytes | What it actually is |
|---|---|---|---|
| `SKILL.md` | 82 | 3,989 | Frontmatter plus five separate routing blocks and one companion list |
| `references/full-guide.md` | 138 | ~8 KB | The entire body of rules: sixteen `#` sections, **zero code blocks** |
| `references/00-topic-map.md` | 28 | ~1 KB | A flat list of the sixteen `#` headings in `full-guide.md` |
| `references/SOURCES.md` | 44 | ~2 KB | A genuine provenance ledger: twelve official URLs plus a conflict-resolution order |
| `agents/openai.yaml` | 7 | ~0.4 KB | Codex interface block, correct bare `$` form |

There are **no scripts, no assets, no templates, no `__pycache__`, no example Dockerfile, and no example Compose file**. `grep -c '```'` returns `0` for both `SKILL.md` and `references/full-guide.md`.

The skill's own scope claim, `SKILL.md:3`, is:

> "production Dockerfile hardening, Docker Compose or Docker Swarm delivery patterns, registry mirror or private-registry strategy, shared-network or shared-infra container runtime design, secret and healthcheck handling, image size, or deterministic production container guidance"

Against that claim, a term-coverage sweep across all three reference files and `SKILL.md` returns zero occurrences of: `.dockerignore`, `layer`, `BuildKit`, `buildx`, `# syntax=`, `--mount=type=`, cache mount, `LABEL`, `STOPSIGNAL`, PID 1, `stop_grace_period`, `update_config`, `rollback_config`, `replicas`, `start_period`, `read_only`, `env_file`, `interpolation`, `:?`, `profiles`, `depends_on`, `service_healthy`, `logging`, `provenance`, `attestation`, `cosign`. The only Dockerfile *instruction* named anywhere in the skill is `ENTRYPOINT`, and that occurrence (`full-guide.md:34`) is the phrase "wrapper entrypoint" describing a shell script, not the instruction.

`SKILL.md:11-15` is a Purpose section whose entire content is "Use this skill when the task needs the architectural or policy guidance owned by Alaa Docker Production" — a self-referential sentence carrying no information.

#### What the fleet's Docker and Compose artifacts actually look like

The live expression of everything this skill claims to govern is `service-runtime-kit` `v2.3.0` (`VERSION:1`), whose `scripts/render-runtime.sh` (1,799 lines) emits every Laravel service's Compose and Swarm files. I reconstructed the missing `scripts/lib/common.sh` and rendered the kit's own fixture (`contracts/service.runtime.env.example` plus a minimal `.env`) to read the real output rather than infer it. Findings, with generator line citations:

**The kit does not emit a Dockerfile.** `render-runtime.sh:1295-1306` writes a `build:` stanza that *references* one — `dockerfile: Dockerfile`, `target: runtime`, with build args `IMAGE_PROXY_PREFIX`, `OCTANE_BASE_IMAGE`, `COMPOSER_VERSION`, `WWWUSER`, `WWWGROUP` — but there is no Dockerfile template anywhere in the kit. `README.md:182` confirms this is deliberate: *"Direct manual Docker builds that bypass `up-local.sh` still depend on Dockerfile-side normalization. Documented for later Dockerfile work; do not patch Dockerfiles here unless that change is explicitly requested."* The Dockerfile for every service in the fleet is therefore governed by **nothing except this skill**, and this skill contains no Dockerfile guidance beyond seven bullets of preference verbs at `full-guide.md:17-30`.

**Base images.** The app image derives from `${OCTANE_BASE_IMAGE:-registry.takhtenegar.ir/docker/octane-base:v1.3.1}` (`render-runtime.sh:302`, default at `contracts/service.runtime.env.example:25`) — a private first-party base whose own Dockerfile is likewise outside the kit. Infra images are hardcoded tag-pins in the generator: `postgres:18.3` (`render-runtime.sh:1417,1485`), `rabbitmq:4.2.4-management` (`:1438,1535`), `redis:8.6.1-alpine` (`:1518`), `adminer:5.4.2` (`:1506`), `edoburu/pgbouncer:v1.24.1-p1` (`:1556,1747`). None is digest-pinned; none is routed through the mirror prefix.

**Multi-stage structure and user.** Only visible as `target: runtime` (`:1298`). No generated Compose or Swarm service sets `user:` for `platform-app-php`, `worker`, or `scheduler` — the only `user:` keys in the whole generator are on the throwaway `composer` and `vendor-sync` tool services (`:1464,1470`). `WWWUSER`/`WWWGROUP` are passed as build args and as *environment variables* (`:1305-1306,1316-1317`) but never as the runtime `user:` key, so whether the container runs as UID 1000 depends entirely on the unmanaged Dockerfile.

**Healthcheck shape.** In the rendered production Compose, `platform-app-php` has **no `healthcheck:` key at all**. `worker` and each `worker-<queue>` carry `healthcheck: test: ["NONE"]` (`:1264-1265`), which disables any inherited image healthcheck. `scheduler` has none. Only the three shared-infra services get real probes (`:1497-1501` pg_isready, `:1526-1530` redis-cli ping, `:1547-1551` rabbitmq-diagnostics). **The generated Swarm file contains exactly one `healthcheck:` key across the whole stack, and it is the worker's `["NONE"]`.** A `docker/octane/healthcheck.sh` template does exist (`templates/generated/docker/octane/healthcheck.sh`), invoked presumably by a `HEALTHCHECK` instruction in the unmanaged Dockerfile; it runs `php artisan octane:status` for the app role (`:13`) and `php artisan schedule:list` for the scheduler (`:16`) — both full Laravel framework boots per probe, which is the opposite of `full-guide.md:80` ("Healthchecks should be lightweight").

**Secret delivery.** Two mutually exclusive modes, switched by `RUNTIME_SECRET_FILES_ENABLED` (`render-runtime.sh:1173`). With secret-files **on**, the app receives `APP_KEY_FILE: /run/secrets/app_key` plus Passport key paths (`:1174-1176`), Compose mounts them from `./docker/.local-secrets/*` (`:1178`) and Swarm mounts `external: true` named secrets (`:1179`). The container-side `_FILE` convention is implemented generically in `templates/generated/docker/octane/entrypoint.sh:33-36`, which walks every env var ending `_FILE` and exports the base name from the file contents. With secret-files **off** — the default, `contracts/service.runtime.env.example:112` — the app, worker and scheduler each get `APP_KEY: ${APP_KEY:?APP_KEY is missing from the service .env; pass it with --env-file}` and every other credential arrives as a plain `environment:` entry.

**Swarm versus Compose divergence.** The Swarm renderer (`render-runtime.sh:1642-1787`) drops `build:`, `restart:`, `volumes:`, the `storage-data` volume, and all seven bootstrap/tool/infra services; it converts short-form port publishing to long form with `mode: ingress` (`:1686-1692`); it swaps bind-mounted files for top-level `configs:` (`:1761-1765,1776-1781`); it swaps `file:`-backed secrets for `external: true` ones (`:1179`); and it serialises worker `command` as a JSON array rather than a YAML list (`:1243-1254`). Rendered service inventory: Compose 13 services, Swarm 4. What it does **not** carry: no `update_config`, no `rollback_config`, no `replicas`, no `placement`, no `reservations`, no `stop_grace_period`, no healthcheck on any long-lived role.

**How much of this the 8 KB full-guide covers.** By subject: registry strategy (`:61-67`) and shared-network/DNS (`:47-59`) are covered and correspond closely to what the kit does. Secret handling (`:69-74`) is covered at the level of intent but not mechanism. Runtime mode split (`:31-36`) and the delivery wrapper (`:38-45`) describe `up-local.sh` accurately and are the strongest part of the file. Everything else — Dockerfile authorship, layer economics, healthcheck shape, Swarm rollout, resource limits, interpolation form, logging, lifecycle signals — is either one preference-verb bullet or absent. My estimate: the guide covers roughly a fifth of the surface the kit actually generates, and none of the Dockerfile surface the kit deliberately leaves ungoverned.

#### The fail-closed interpolation invariant: current state

**The RFC was acted on, in its syntactic half only.**

The memory record cites `render-runtime.sh:911,975` emitting bare `${APP_KEY}` and `up-local.sh:72-76` omitting `--env-file`. Both call sites have moved and both are fixed:

- Every APP_KEY emission is now fail-closed. `render-runtime.sh:1218` (worker), `:1312` (prod app), `:1379` (prod scheduler), `:1655` (swarm app), `:1712` (swarm scheduler) all read:
  ```
  if [[ "${RUNTIME_SECRET_FILES_ENABLED:-false}" == "true" ]]; then printf '%s\n' "${worker_secret_env}"; else echo '      APP_KEY: ${APP_KEY:?APP_KEY is missing from the service .env; pass it with --env-file}'; fi
  ```
  My render confirms it in the output: `docker-compose.yml:25,138,240` and `docker-compose.swarm.yml:12,115,206`.
- `scripts/up-local.sh:85-87` now aborts before touching Docker:
  ```
  if [[ ! -f "${env_file}" && "${ALLOW_NO_ENV_FILE:-}" != "true" ]]; then
    die "Required service environment file is missing: ${env_file}. Without it, Docker Compose can render required values as empty. ..."
  ```
  The same guard is emitted into the generated wrapper at `render-runtime.sh:772-775`. Only the exact string `true` bypasses it.
- A mechanical checker now exists: `scripts/validate-runtime.sh:73-118` rejects any `$VAR` or `${VAR}` in `docker-compose*.yml` that lacks a `:?` or `:-` modifier, skipping comment lines (`:82`) and counting preceding `$` characters to honour `$$` escapes (`:88-96`). Those are precisely the two false-positive traps the ratification names.
- A regression test now exists: `scripts/test-fail-closed-interpolation.sh` (179 lines). It proves the validator accepts the clean fixture (`:56-68`), rejects eight distinct violation shapes by exact `file:line:variable` (`:70-93`), proves both the kit wrapper and the generated wrapper refuse a missing `.env` without invoking Docker (`:139-161`, using a fake `docker` binary and a call log), proves `ALLOW_NO_ENV_FILE=TRUE` does *not* bypass the guard (`:152-156`), and proves the rendered Compose carries the fail-closed APP_KEY form in env mode and `APP_KEY_FILE` with no `APP_KEY` in secret-files mode (`:113-137`).

**The semantic half has not landed, and the checker cannot see it.** The ratified rule says a variable whose default would silently disable a safety control — explicitly including *"a credential in a production-shaped file"* — takes `:?` with **no default permitted**. The generator does the opposite for every credential except `APP_KEY`:

- `render-runtime.sh:318` — `db_password_expr="\${DB_PASSWORD:-${DB_PASSWORD_DEFAULT:-}}"`, and `contracts/service.runtime.env.example:52-55` sets `DB_PASSWORD_DEFAULT=` deliberately blank ("Keep database names and passwords in the service `.env`. Leave these defaults blank"). The rendered result is `DB_PASSWORD: ${DB_PASSWORD:-}` at `docker-compose.yml:44,146,249,337,461` and `docker-compose.swarm.yml:28,123,215,292`.
- `render-runtime.sh:358` with `RABBITMQ_PASSWORD_DEFAULT=app` (`service.runtime.env.example:79`) renders `RABBITMQ_PASSWORD: ${RABBITMQ_PASSWORD:-app}` at `docker-compose.yml:59-60,155-156,258-259,353,442`.
- `render-runtime.sh:325` with `DB_PROVISION_ADMIN_PASSWORD_DEFAULT=postgres` (`service.runtime.env.example:62`) renders `DB_PROVISION_ADMIN_PASSWORD: ${DB_PROVISION_ADMIN_PASSWORD:-postgres}` at `docker-compose.yml:52,333,463,399` — including as `POSTGRES_PASSWORD` on the shared-infra Postgres container.
- `render-runtime.sh:289` renders the service's own image as `${COMMENT_DOCKER_IMAGE:-comment-service:latest}` (`docker-compose.yml:19,132,234`, `docker-compose.swarm.yml`) — a floating `:latest` default in the production-shaped file and the Swarm stack file, contradicting `full-guide.md:21` and `:134`.

I ran `validate-runtime.sh` against this exact output: **`Runtime validation passed`, exit 0.** The checker enforces the *form* of every interpolation and has no concept of which variables are safety controls, so an empty database password and a hardcoded `postgres` admin password in a Swarm stack file are clean by its lights. That register — the list of variables for which `:-` is a defect regardless of syntax — does not exist in the kit and does not exist in this skill, which is the skill named as the invariant's owner.

Plainly: the RFC's two named defects were fixed, a real checker and a real regression test shipped with them, and the half of the invariant that needs a judgment about *which* variable is a safety control was not implemented anywhere.

#### Where the skill and the kit disagree

The dividing line is: `service-runtime-kit-governance` owns which generator variable expresses a thing — its name, its tracked default, which contract file holds it, whether changing it forces a re-render. `alaa-docker-production` owns the Docker or Compose construct that variable is interpolated into, and the interpolation form it is written in.

On that line, these belong to `alaa-docker-production` and the kit currently decides them alone, against the skill's own text:

| Subject | Skill says | Kit does | Citations |
|---|---|---|---|
| Interpolation form of a credential | invariant owner (per ratification); skill text silent | `${DB_PASSWORD:-}`, `${RABBITMQ_PASSWORD:-app}`, `${DB_PROVISION_ADMIN_PASSWORD:-postgres}` | `render-runtime.sh:318,358,325` |
| Image floating | "Do not 'float' critical service images in production"; digest for releases | `${..._DOCKER_IMAGE:-comment-service:latest}`; five tag-pinned infra images, zero digests | `full-guide.md:20-21,134` vs `render-runtime.sh:289,1417,1438,1485,1506,1518,1556` |
| Healthcheck | "Healthcheck for every service" | app: none; worker: `["NONE"]`; scheduler: none; Swarm: none | `full-guide.md:28` vs `render-runtime.sh:1264-1265` and rendered output |
| Healthcheck weight | "lightweight and must not depend on heavy DB queries" | `php artisan octane:status` — full framework boot per probe | `full-guide.md:80` vs `templates/generated/docker/octane/healthcheck.sh:13` |
| Non-root runtime user | "Non-root runtime user; least privilege"; "Run as non-root" | no `user:` on any app/worker/scheduler service in any of three files | `full-guide.md:23,89` vs generator (grep `user:` returns only `:1464,1470`) |
| Capability drop, no-new-privileges, read-only rootfs | `cap_drop: [ALL]`, `no-new-privileges`, read-only fs | zero occurrences of `cap_drop`, `security_opt`, `read_only`, `tmpfs`, `pids_limit` in the generator | `full-guide.md:91-93` vs grep over `render-runtime.sh` |
| Swarm secret ownership and mode | "prefer external secrets with explicit ownership and file mode" | secret mounts carry `source`/`target` only, no `uid`/`gid`/`mode` | `full-guide.md:72` vs `render-runtime.sh:1177` |
| Swarm rollout | "zero-downtime rollout guidance"; "Any rollout notes (restart strategy, ...)" | no `update_config`, no `rollback_config`, no `replicas` → Swarm defaults: parallelism 1, `order: stop-first`, `failure_action: pause` | `full-guide.md:13,127` vs `render-runtime.sh:1693-1697` |
| Exposure | "Do not publish DB/broker ports publicly in production Compose" | Postgres/Redis/RabbitMQ/Adminer/PgBouncer published on `127.0.0.1` with a documented owner-standard port table | `full-guide.md:86` vs `render-runtime.sh:1493-1495,1522-1524,1543-1545,1510-1512` and `README.md:149-166` |

The last row is not a defect — a loopback publish is not a public exposure — but the skill's sentence has no scope, so an agent reading it would flag the kit's deliberate, documented, owner-ratified port table as a violation. That is a wording failure in the skill, not a defect in the kit.

**Does the skill name `service-runtime-kit-governance`? No.** The companion list at `SKILL.md:52-63` names exactly five skills: `alaa-services-contract`, `caas-arvan-kuber`, `alaa-cicd-laravel-postgres`, `alaa-security-review`, `alaa-minio-object-storage`. The kit that generates every Compose file, every Swarm stack file, every runtime helper script and every secret bootstrap in the fleet is not mentioned in any file of this skill. `full-guide.md:32` gestures at the seam — "Keep primary orchestrator-specific production guidance in the platform-owned skill for that environment" — and names no skill. Under the batch rule, silence is not delegation.

---

### 2. Ten-criteria verdict

| # | Criterion | Verdict | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Correctness and testability | **FAIL** | Zero shipped checks, zero code blocks, zero fixtures. The nearest thing is `full-guide.md:113-127` "Output contract", which is a six-heading report template for prose, not an assertion. `full-guide.md:41` says "Validate the rendered Compose model" and names no command; the kit's `validate-runtime.sh` exists and the skill does not know about it. |
| 2 | Failure behaviour | **FAIL** | No rollout ordering, no `update_config`/`rollback_config`, no `stop_grace_period`, no SIGTERM/PID-1 handling, no `depends_on: condition: service_healthy`, no `start_period`, no restart-policy semantics. The whole subject is `full-guide.md:127`: "Any rollout notes (restart strategy, zero-downtime considerations, Compose-versus-Swarm differences)" — a report heading, and "Any" makes it optional. `alaa-reliability-sla` is not named anywhere in the skill. |
| 3 | Security | **FAIL** | `full-guide.md:88-96` is eight bullets, six of which carry an escape clause ("unless explicitly required", "where supported", "where possible", "where feasible", "only if policy requires"). The fail-closed interpolation invariant this skill is the named owner of appears zero times. `full-guide.md:29` "Secrets via env/secret manager" endorses environment delivery, which `docker inspect` exposes to anyone in the `docker` group, and contradicts `:72`. `SKILL.md:60` names `$alaa-security-review` but as "Pair when the task also touches runtime hardening and secret handling" — a pairing note, not a delegation of a named subject, and Codex-only syntax. |
| 4 | Observability | **FAIL** | Zero mention of logging driver, log rotation, `json-file` size limits, structured log format, a metrics endpoint, or container-level telemetry. `full-guide.md:85` names `X-Forwarded-*`, `X-Request-Id` and `traceparent` and asks only that trust boundaries be "documented". `alaa-observability-soc` and `alaa-services-contract`'s log-field and metric catalogs are not routed to. The kit meanwhile renders a full block of `OTEL_*` and `PROMETHEUS_*` defaults (`render-runtime.sh:1100-1159`) that the skill has no opinion about. |
| 5 | Concurrency and load | **FAIL** | `full-guide.md:109-111` is the whole treatment: "Surface `nofile` ulimit and relevant sysctls when high-load is required. Keep changes minimal and documented." No value, no formula, no trigger. Nothing on `deploy.resources.limits`/`reservations`, replica count, the Octane-workers-versus-CPU-quota problem (`README.md:113`: "Docker CPU quotas do not reliably change the value returned by `nproc`"), PgBouncer pool sizing against container CPU, or backpressure. The kit ships all of this; the skill governs none of it. |
| 6 | Clean code, SOLID, design patterns | **SATISFIED** | `full-guide.md:33-36` and `:38-45` are the one genuinely well-formed rule set in the skill: one wrapper entrypoint rather than scattered commands (`:34,39`), idempotent shared-primitive creation (`:41,50`), validate-before-deploy (`:41-42`), and an explicit fail-fast clause — "Fail fast when a requested runtime mode is unsupported instead of silently falling back to another mode" (`:36`). `:45` states an observable output contract for the wrapper (mode, project/stack name, image tag, prechecks). The kit's `up-local.sh` implements all of it. |
| 7 | Algorithm and data-structure choice | **FAIL** | The domain's form of this is a stated image-size budget, a build-time budget, and a layer-cache-hit predicate. `full-guide.md:22` states "Keep images minimal; only runtime deps in final stage" with no number and no test. Layer economics — the single largest determinant of build wall-clock in a fleet of Laravel services — is absent entirely (`layer` returns zero occurrences). No owner is named, so this is FAIL and not NOT-OWNED. |
| 8 | Configurability | **SATISFIED** | `full-guide.md:63-66`: keep the mirror configurable through env or build args rather than hardcoded in many files; "Distinguish public-mirror variables from private-registry variables so operators can rotate them independently." `:34-35` allows aliases only when they resolve to an explicit documented mode. Validation at the boundary is stated twice: `:41` (validate the rendered Compose model before deploy) and `:74` (validate secret presence and permissions before starting the main workload). The kit's `PUBLIC_DOCKER_REGISTRY` / `OCTANE_BASE_IMAGE` split matches. |
| 9 | Speed of development and debuggability | **FAIL** | No copy-pasteable artifact of any kind. The troubleshooting map (`SKILL.md:43-50`) is the skill's only debugging aid and four of its six rows route to section names that do not exist in `full-guide.md`: "image-build and deterministic-runtime sections", "runtime contract and container-user sections", "non-root user and filesystem guidance", "healthcheck and release-evidence sections". An agent following any of those four lands nowhere. |
| 10 | Documentation | **FAIL** | `full-guide.md:113-127` names six report headings and no destination — not which file, not which repository, not whether it goes in a merge request or an `AGENTS.md`. It covers "what shipped" and partially "how it is operated" and does not cover "how it fails": there is no failure catalogue, no runbook, no statement of what an operator does when a healthcheck flaps or a Swarm rollout stalls. Item 6 begins "Any rollout notes", which makes the operability half optional. |

**Tally: 2 SATISFIED, 8 FAIL, 0 NOT-OWNED.**

Zero `NOT-OWNED` verdicts is itself the finding. The skill routes to five companions in a "pair with" register that delegates no specific subject, so no criterion can legitimately be scored `NOT-OWNED`; every gap is silence.

---

### 3. Defect classes actually found

**1. Stale model pins — not found.** `grep -riE "gpt-|claude-|opus|sonnet|haiku|o3|o4"` across the skill returns nothing. The skill also never routes to `alaa-prompting-guide`, which is a routing gap rather than a pin defect.

**2. Wrong trigger syntax — found, four of five call sites.** `SKILL.md:54` `$alaa-services-contract`, `:56` `$caas-arvan-kuber`, `:58` `$alaa-cicd-laravel-postgres`, `:60` `$alaa-security-review` are bare Codex form and are invisible from Claude Code. Only `:62` gives both: "`$alaa-minio-object-storage` (`/alaa-minio-object-storage` in Claude Code)" — and even that inverts the settled ordering, which puts `/name` first. `agents/openai.yaml:4` is correctly bare-`$` and needs no change.

**3. Duplication between body and references — found, extensive.** See §5.

**4. Project-specific content in an always-loaded body — found, minor; the larger instance is in the reference.** `SKILL.md:35` hardcodes the three-way Ala/Arvan routing decision into the always-loaded body. In the reference, `full-guide.md:52` names `alaa-shared-network` and `alaa-shared-infra`, `:58` names the exact Compose service key `platform-app-php` and the alias pattern `<service>-platform-app-php`, and `:64` names `mirror.cdn.ir`. These are correct and load-bearing values — they match `render-runtime.sh:1294,1369` and `service.runtime.env.example:24,30-31` exactly — but they sit in the one reference file that must be opened for *any* Docker question, so a Kubernetes-only or a non-Ala task pays for them.

**5. Long numbered procedures nobody reads in order — found, mild.** `SKILL.md:32-39` "Quick start" is a six-step ordered list whose steps 3, 4 and 5 are all "read a reference", and `full-guide.md:113-127` is a six-item numbered output contract. Neither is recovery content, so the failure-class restructuring rule does not strictly apply; but the troubleshooting map at `SKILL.md:43-50`, which *is* recovery-shaped, has no diagnosis, no smallest retry and no escalation — only a symptom column and a broken destination column.

**6. Description that only says when to use — not found.** `SKILL.md:3` ends "Do not use it when pure app logic changes." It is 359 characters, well under 900, and contains no angle brackets, so it passes plugin validation. The "do not use" clause is however far narrower than the body's own version (`SKILL.md:27-30` adds "non-containerized local-only tasks") and names no other owner, which is the real weakness: it excludes by task shape, not by owner.

**7. Fragile tooling — not applicable.** No scripts ship.

**8. Shipped `__pycache__` — not found.**

**9. Measured against section 2 — see §2.** Eight FAILs.

**10. Shrink where possible — inverted here.** The always-loaded body has five routing structures (`SKILL.md:32-39`, `:41-50`, `:52-63`, `:65-72`, `:74-81`) totalling 50 of its 82 lines. Those can shrink substantially. The references must grow.

**11. Companion boundary — found, severe.** See §4.

**12. Two routers — found, in an unusual form.** There is no verbatim duplicate of `references/00-topic-map.md` in the body, but the body carries *three* independent routing tables: "Quick start" (`:32-39`), "Troubleshooting map" (`:41-50`), and "Reference navigation" (`:65-72`). Separately, `references/00-topic-map.md` is not a router over reference files at all — it is a table of contents for the sixteen `#` headings inside the single file `full-guide.md`. With only three reference files, the settled convention (eight or fewer ⇒ list them in `SKILL.md` with a trigger condition each) says a separate topic map should not exist yet. `references/SOURCES.md` is a legitimate provenance ledger and must be kept.

**13. Rules with no tool that reports a violation.** The skill ships **zero** executable checks. Every one of the roughly seventy imperative sentences in `full-guide.md` is therefore a preference. This is the single most consequential defect in the skill, and it is thrown into relief by the fact that the kit it governs ships two working checkers (`validate-runtime.sh`, `test-fail-closed-interpolation.sh`) that the skill neither knows about nor requires.

---

### 4. Boundary map

**Named today** (`SKILL.md:52-63`), all in a "Pair when…" register that transfers no subject:

| Named | Trigger given | Assessment |
|---|---|---|
| `$alaa-services-contract` | "Ala-specific deploy expectations, canonical service aliases, key ownership, service bootstrap rules" | Correct owner, wrong syntax, and contradicted by `full-guide.md:52,58` which states the alias values in this skill anyway. |
| `$caas-arvan-kuber` | "primary Arvan Kubernetes production path, Helm values, OCI charts, GitLab rollout mechanics" | Correct, wrong syntax. Overlaps `alaa-k8s-helm`, which is not named. |
| `$alaa-cicd-laravel-postgres` | "pipeline alignment for image builds" | Wrong owner under D8. Pipeline expression is `alaa-gitlab-ci-cd`'s. |
| `$alaa-security-review` | "runtime hardening and secret handling" | Correct owner for threat classification, but the skill states its own hardening rules at `:88-96`, so this reads as advisory, not as delegation. |
| `$alaa-minio-object-storage` | "what a MinIO container's bucket, policy, lifecycle rules, or credentials must be, as opposed to how the container is expressed" | **The one well-formed boundary sentence in the skill.** It states both halves and gives both trigger forms. Use it as the template for the rest. |

**Not named, and needed:**

| Missing owner | Where the silence bites |
|---|---|
| `service-runtime-kit-governance` | The kit generates every Compose file, Swarm stack file, wrapper script and secret bootstrap this skill claims to govern. Zero mentions anywhere in the skill. |
| `alaa-frontend-devops` | D8's counterparty. `alaa-frontend-devops/references/90-companion-boundary.md:16` and `SKILL.md:16` name this skill by both trigger forms; this skill names it not at all. The boundary is currently asserted from one side only. |
| `alaa-gitlab-ci-cd` | `full-guide.md:96` ("Prefer OSS scanners in CI"), `:101-102` ("storing it as a CI artifact"), `:124` — the skill repeatedly instructs an agent to do things *in CI* and never says whose CI. |
| `alaa-haproxy` | `full-guide.md:59` prohibits coupling HAProxy to task IDs and `:136` repeats it as an anti-pattern; `:83` states an edge-exposure rule. The directive owner is never named. |
| `alaa-reliability-sla` | Restart strategy, zero-downtime, degradation, timeouts — `full-guide.md:127` raises all four and owns none. |
| `alaa-observability-soc` + `alaa-services-contract` | Log fields, metric catalog, `OTEL_*` names and defaults. Criterion 4 has no owner at all. |
| `alaa-octane-performance` | `full-guide.md:76-80` writes Octane/Swoole extension, OPcache and worker rules. A dedicated skill for this exists. |
| `alaa-k8s-helm` | `full-guide.md:32` says "the platform-owned skill for that environment" and names no skill. |
| `alaa-project-constitution` | The quality bar itself is restated in fragments rather than routed to. |
| `alaa-prompting-guide` | No model/effort routing line exists. |

**The reciprocal half of D8 that Phase 2 must write.** The exact sentence, to sit in `SKILL.md` under an `## Ownership: platform versus stack` heading:

> `alaa-docker-production` owns how the build and runtime images and any Compose or Swarm stack file are expressed — the Dockerfile and its stages, the order and contents of its layers, what survives into the final image, and every key in a Compose or stack file including the interpolation form of every variable written into it — and it decides no gate: `/alaa-frontend-devops` (`$alaa-frontend-devops`) owns the frontend delivery gate register and states the obligations an image must satisfy, `/alaa-gitlab-ci-cd` (`$alaa-gitlab-ci-cd`) owns how a gate is expressed on a runner, and `/alaa-haproxy` (`$alaa-haproxy`) owns how a cache or routing decision is expressed as a directive. When a gate names a property of an image or a Compose file, this skill writes the instruction or key that satisfies it and does not change the gate.

**The runtime-kit seam sentence**, to sit immediately after it:

> `/service-runtime-kit-governance` (`$service-runtime-kit-governance`) owns which generator variable expresses a thing — its name, its tracked default, which contract file holds it, and whether changing it forces a re-render. This skill owns the Docker or Compose construct that variable is interpolated into and the interpolation form it is written in. A request to add a knob goes there; a request to change what the generated file looks like comes here. Where a variable's default would silently disable a safety control, the interpolation form is this skill's call, and it is `${VAR:?message}` with no default, whatever tracked default the generator carries for it.

### 5. Duplication

**Body against reference — two whole sections.**

- `SKILL.md:17-25` "When to use" (seven bullets) against `full-guide.md:8-14` "# When to use" (six bullets). Same subject list in different words; `SKILL.md:3`'s description states it a third time. Three statements of one thing.
- `SKILL.md:11-15` "Purpose" against `full-guide.md:1-6` "# Purpose". The body's version carries no content ("guidance owned by Alaa Docker Production"); the reference's version is the real one.

**Inside `full-guide.md` — the `# Anti-patterns` block is a restatement of the file.** Eight of its nine lines duplicate a rule stated earlier:

| Anti-pattern | Already stated at |
|---|---|
| `:130` "Running everything as root" | `:23` "Non-root runtime user"; `:89` "Run as non-root" — *three* statements |
| `:131` "Publishing DB/broker ports publicly" | `:86` |
| `:132` "Baking secrets into images" | `:29`; `:94` — three statements |
| `:133` "Huge base images with unnecessary build tooling" | `:22`; `:24` |
| `:134` "Using floating tags without pinning for releases" | `:19-21` |
| `:135` "Healthchecks that perform expensive DB queries or mutate state" | `:80` |
| `:136` "Proxying to container instance names when stable service DNS is available" | `:59` |
| `:137` "Building Swarm images ad hoc during deploy" | `:44` |

Only `:135`'s "or mutate state" clause adds anything not stated elsewhere.

**Other internal duplication.**

- Read-only filesystem: `:27` ("Read-only filesystem where possible; explicit writable volumes only") and `:93` ("Prefer read-only filesystem; allow writable tmp/cache dirs explicitly"). Two different escape clauses for one rule.
- Release evidence: `:4-6` states it, `:14` states it again as a "when to use" bullet, `:98-107` states it in full, `:124-125` states it a fourth time as an output-contract item.
- Secrets: `:29`, `:71-72`, `:94` state the same prohibition with three different delivery recommendations, one of which (`:29`, env) contradicts the other two.
- Determinism: `:18-21`, `:24-26`, `:99-104`, `:117-119` all state pinning obligations.

**Retirement nomination: `full-guide.md:129-138` in its current form.** Fold `:135`'s "or mutate state" into the healthcheck reference and move the block to `_to_delete/2026-07-29-batch-7/`. A "wrong shape" register is worth keeping in principle, but it must carry the *diagnostic* the positive rule cannot — how to spot the anti-pattern in an existing file — not a paraphrase of the rule.

### 6. Wording-test failures

Every one of these is a sentence a competent agent could follow exactly and still ship the wrong artifact.

1. **`full-guide.md:19`** — "Pin runtime base images by major/minor at minimum." No scope (which images: app only, or infra too?) and "at minimum" sets a floor with no ceiling. An agent writes `FROM php:8.4` and complies. *Replacement:* every `FROM` in a production Dockerfile and every `image:` in a production-shaped Compose or stack file names a tag that is not `latest` and not a bare major, and a production release additionally pins by digest.
2. **`full-guide.md:20`** — "**Prefer** digest pinning for **production releases** (not necessarily for local dev)." Preference verb where the sentence means a constraint, and "production release" is undefined. The kit ships `${..._DOCKER_IMAGE:-...:latest}` and violates nothing this sentence says.
3. **`full-guide.md:22`** — "Keep images minimal; only runtime deps in final stage." Abstract noun with no observable test. Contrast the testable form the frontend skill already wrote for the same obligation (`alaa-frontend-devops/references/20-ci-gates-and-predicates.md:44`): "The runtime image contains no package manager, no compiler, and no devDependencies."
4. **`full-guide.md:26`** — "avoid network calls during runtime image build except dependency installs." Every install is a network call; the sentence excepts the thing it prohibits and leaves nothing observable.
5. **`full-guide.md:27` / `:93`** — "Read-only filesystem where possible" / "Prefer read-only filesystem". "Where possible" is not a condition an agent can evaluate and there is no positive replacement stating which paths get `tmpfs`. Result in the field: `read_only` appears nowhere in the kit.
6. **`full-guide.md:28`** — "Healthcheck for every service." No shape, no probe target, no `interval`/`timeout`/`retries`/`start_period`, no exemption class. The kit's `test: ["NONE"]` on workers is arguably compliant (a healthcheck key is present) and the app service, which has no key at all, is the one that actually violates it — and nothing in the sentence would tell an agent which is which.
7. **`full-guide.md:29`** — "Secrets via env/secret manager; never committed and never baked into images." Endorses environment delivery, which `docker inspect` and `/proc/<pid>/environ` expose to any local process with the right privileges, and contradicts `:72`. *Replacement:* a runtime secret is delivered as a file mount with mode `0400` and read by the `_FILE` convention; an `environment:` entry is permitted only for a value that is not a secret.
8. **`full-guide.md:32`** — "Keep primary orchestrator-specific production guidance in the platform-owned skill for that environment." Prohibition with no owner named and no positive replacement.
9. **`full-guide.md:56`** — "prefer service DNS plus VIP semantics **such as** `endpoint_mode: vip`". "Such as" turns the one concrete key into an example, and `vip` is already the Swarm default, so the sentence asks for nothing.
10. **`full-guide.md:72`** — "prefer external secrets with explicit ownership and file mode **when the runtime supports it**." Swarm supports `uid`, `gid` and `mode` unconditionally; the escape clause has no true case, and the kit ships secrets with none of the three.
11. **`full-guide.md:74`** — "Validate secret presence and permissions before starting the main workload." "Permissions" is not defined. The kit's implementation is `chmod 600 "${dst}" || true` (`ensure-local-secrets.sh:51,118`), which swallows the failure it was meant to catch.
12. **`full-guide.md:79`** — "Use OPcache settings appropriate for long-lived workers." Abstract noun standing in for four specific values (`opcache.validate_timestamps`, `opcache.memory_consumption`, `opcache.max_accelerated_files`, `opcache.jit`).
13. **`full-guide.md:83`/`:86`** — "Only edge proxies are publicly exposed" / "Do not publish DB/broker ports publicly in production Compose." "Publicly" has no definition, so the kit's deliberate, owner-ratified `127.0.0.1:15432:5432` table (`README.md:149-166`) reads as a violation.
14. **`full-guide.md:90-92`** — "Drop Linux capabilities **unless explicitly required**"; "**Prefer** an explicit capability baseline (e.g., `cap_drop: [ALL]`)"; "Set `no-new-privileges` **where supported**". Three escape clauses, one example marker, zero constraints. Compose and Swarm both support `security_opt: [no-new-privileges:true]`.
15. **`full-guide.md:95`** — "Restrict outbound network access **where feasible** (environment-dependent)." Pure preference; no agent can fail this.
16. **`full-guide.md:96`** — "Prefer OSS scanners in CI (e.g., Trivy) and fail on critical issues **only** if policy requires." The whole predicate is deferred to a policy that is never located.
17. **`full-guide.md:106`** — "Do not invent policy. If no severity policy exists, do not fail builds by default." Followed literally, an agent ships a scanner that can never fail a build — a gate that gates nothing. *Replacement:* if no severity policy exists, the scan runs and its report is a required release artifact, and the merge request states which severities were present.
18. **`full-guide.md:110`** — "Surface `nofile` ulimit and relevant sysctls when high-load is required." "Surface" is not an action, "relevant" is not a set, and "high-load" is not a threshold.
19. **`full-guide.md:127`** — "**Any** rollout notes (restart strategy, zero-downtime considerations, Compose-versus-Swarm differences)". "Any" makes the item optional; it is the only place the skill addresses criterion 2.
20. **`SKILL.md:43-50`** — the troubleshooting map routes four of six symptom rows to section names that do not exist in `full-guide.md`, so following it exactly yields nothing.
21. **`SKILL.md:79`** — "Keep generic Docker and Swarm mechanics here; move Ala-specific service-family hard constraints into `alaa-services-contract`." A maintenance instruction that the file itself violates at `:52`, `:58` and `:64`.

### 7. Stale or unverifiable claims

The skill makes **no explicit version claim about any Docker component**. `references/SOURCES.md:7` correctly instructs re-checking "Dockerfile syntax, BuildKit or buildx behavior, Compose or Swarm compatibility, image provenance, registry behavior, CVEs" — but the guide states nothing versioned for that instruction to protect. The freshness discipline is real and the surface it guards is empty. Everything below was verified today, 2026-07-29, against official sources; each row records whether the skill's implied claim still holds.

| Subject | What I verified | Source | Verdict on the skill |
|---|---|---|---|
| Docker Engine current version | **29.6.2**, released 16 July 2026 (29.6.1 on 26 June, 29.6.0 on 18 June); 29.6.2 carries fixes for CVE-2026-15788 through CVE-2026-15793 | https://docs.docker.com/engine/release-notes/29/ | Skill states no version. Nothing stale; nothing stated. Phase 2 should name the current major line where a feature's availability depends on it. |
| Docker Compose current version | **v5.3.1**, released 7 July 2026. v5.3.0 (2 July) added native init-container support; v5.2.0 (23 June) replaced the state-reconciliation algorithm | https://github.com/docker/compose/releases | The skill's Compose surface predates the v5 line entirely and mentions no v5 capability. Init containers in particular bear directly on the kit's `db-provision` / `rabbitmq-provision` bootstrap-profile pattern and on the Swarm gap in `README.md:126`. |
| `docker compose` v1 syntax claims | Compose v1 (`docker-compose`, Python) is gone; the top-level `version:` property is **obsolete** — "only informative", retained "for backward compatibility", warns when present, and "Compose always uses the most recent schema to validate the Compose file, regardless of the `version` field". The `name:` property is current and is exposed as `COMPOSE_PROJECT_NAME` | https://docs.docker.com/reference/compose-file/version-and-name/ | **Holds.** The skill writes `docker compose up` (`full-guide.md:41`) and never the hyphenated form — `grep -rn "docker-compose"` over the whole skill returns nothing. The kit likewise emits `name:` and no `version:` (`render-runtime.sh:1292`). Correct on both sides. |
| BuildKit / Dockerfile syntax frontend | The documented directive is `# syntax=docker/dockerfile:1`, which pins to the stable major and lets BuildKit fetch "the latest stable version of the Dockerfile syntax before the build" | https://docs.docker.com/reference/dockerfile/ | **Absent, not stale.** `# syntax=` appears zero times in the skill. For a fleet whose Dockerfiles are otherwise ungoverned this is the single highest-value missing line. |
| `RUN --mount=type=secret` | Stable and not marked experimental. Dockerfile form `RUN --mount=type=secret,id=aws ...`; CLI `docker build --secret id=aws,src=$HOME/.aws/credentials .`; default target `/run/secrets/<id>`; env form `--mount=type=secret,id=X,env=VAR` and `--secret id=X,env=Y` | https://docs.docker.com/build/building/secrets/ | **Absent.** `mount=type` returns zero occurrences. `full-guide.md:29` and `:94` prohibit baking secrets into images and offer no build-time mechanism, which leaves `ARG`-passed credentials — recoverable from image history — as the path of least resistance. |
| SBOM and provenance attestations | Current flags are `--sbom` and `--provenance` on `docker buildx build`. **Provenance at `mode=min` is added by default.** `--provenance=mode=max`, `--provenance=false`, and `BUILDX_NO_DEFAULT_ATTESTATIONS` are the controls. All BuildKit exporters support attestations; `local` and `tar` write them to JSON files instead of the image manifest | https://docs.docker.com/build/metadata/attestations/ | **Stale by omission, and materially so.** `full-guide.md:98-107` frames SBOM and scan artifacts as optional and policy-gated, and `provenance`/`attestation` appear zero times. Provenance is now on by default, so the skill's mental model — evidence is something you opt into — is behind the tool. |
| Docker Swarm support status | Swarm **mode** is documented as current: "Swarm mode is an advanced feature for managing a cluster of Docker daemons. Use Swarm mode if you intend to use Swarm as a production runtime environment." No deprecation or maintenance-mode notice. The docs separately note *Classic* Swarm "is no longer actively developed" — a different product | https://docs.docker.com/engine/swarm/ | **Holds.** The skill's Swarm guidance (`full-guide.md:31-36,44,56,72,137`) targets a supported runtime. |
| Alpine base line | Current stable **3.23**, with 3.23.3 shipped as the latest patch alongside 3.20.9 / 3.21.6 / 3.22.3 | https://www.alpinelinux.org/posts/Alpine-3.20.9-3.21.6-3.22.3-3.23.3-released.html | The skill names no base image line. The kit uses `redis:8.6.1-alpine` (`render-runtime.sh:1518`), which is on a supported line. |
| Debian slim base line | Current stable **Debian 13 "trixie"**, point release 13.4 (14 March 2026) | https://www.debian.org/releases/stable/ | The skill names no base image line, so a Phase-2 author has no default to reach for and will pick one per repository. |
| `HEALTHCHECK` | Present in the current Dockerfile reference. Long-standing documented defaults are `--interval=30s`, `--timeout=30s`, `--start-period=0s`, `--retries=3`, with `--start-interval` (default `5s`) available from Docker Engine 25.0; `HEALTHCHECK NONE` disables any healthcheck inherited from the base image, and Compose's `test: ["NONE"]` is the equivalent | https://docs.docker.com/reference/dockerfile/ ; https://docs.docker.com/reference/compose-file/services/ (`healthcheck`) | **The skill's one healthcheck rule (`full-guide.md:28`) names no option at all.** `start_period` and `start-interval` matter here specifically: an Octane app that boots the framework, warms OPcache and caches config takes seconds to become ready, and without `start_period` the container is marked unhealthy during normal start-up. I could not retrieve the option table verbatim — both doc pages truncate before it — so treat the defaults above as well-established but re-confirm at authoring time. |
| `docker init` | GA, no deprecation notice. Generates `Dockerfile`, `compose.yaml`, `.dockerignore` and `README.Docker.md`. Supports ASP.NET Core, Go, Java, Node, PHP with Apache, Python, Rust, and Other | https://docs.docker.com/reference/cli/docker/init/ | The skill makes no claim about it. Worth a single line: `docker init`'s PHP template is Apache-based and is not a starting point for an Octane/Swoole service, so an agent reaching for it would be led away from the fleet's shape. |
| Registry mirror configuration | The daemon-level mechanism is `"registry-mirrors": ["https://host"]` in `/etc/docker/daemon.json`, or `--registry-mirror` at daemon start. Documented limitation: **"It's currently not possible to mirror another private registry. Only the central Hub can be mirrored."** Fair-use and Registry Access Management caveats apply | https://docs.docker.com/docker-hub/image-library/mirror/ | **The skill's registry section is ambiguous in a way that matters.** `full-guide.md:62-64` says "Route public upstream pulls through a configurable pull-through mirror" and names `mirror.cdn.ir`. There are two distinct mechanisms — daemon `registry-mirrors`, which only mirrors Docker Hub, and image-reference prefix rewriting, which works for any upstream. The kit uses the second (`PUBLIC_DOCKER_REGISTRY` / `IMAGE_PROXY_PREFIX`, `render-runtime.sh:301,1302`, default `mirror.cdn.ir/`). The skill does not say which it means, so an agent could configure `registry-mirrors` and find that non-Hub upstreams silently bypass it. |
| Trivy | Named at `full-guide.md:96`; project docs live at https://trivy.dev/latest/ (`SOURCES.md:31`) and the tool is current | https://trivy.dev/latest/ | Holds as a tool name. The *rule* around it does not survive the wording test (§6, item 16). |

`references/SOURCES.md` itself: all twelve official URLs resolve to live Docker or ecosystem documentation. The conflict-resolution order at `:33-39` (running repo files → official Docker docs → OCI specs → security tooling docs → this skill's local references) is well formed and should be carried forward unchanged. `:41-43`, which confines community sources to concrete troubleshooting and bars them from production policy, is a rule worth keeping verbatim.

### 8. Router audit

**The body carries three routing structures, none of them complete.**

- `SKILL.md:32-39` "Quick start" — six ordered steps, of which 3, 4 and 5 each say "read a reference". It is a router wearing a procedure's clothes.
- `SKILL.md:41-50` "Troubleshooting map" — six symptom rows. The destination column names *sections*, and four of the six name sections that do not exist:

  | Row | Destination as written | Does it exist in `full-guide.md`? |
  |---|---|---|
  | build-stage errors or dependency drift | "image-build and deterministic-runtime sections" | **No.** Nearest is `# Defaults (unless repo contradicts)` (`:16`). |
  | runtime crash or missing extension | "runtime contract and container-user sections" | **No.** Nearest is `# PHP/Laravel + Octane/Swoole notes` (`:76`). |
  | permissions or writable-path issues | "non-root user and filesystem guidance" | **No.** Content is scattered across `:23`, `:27`, `:89`, `:93`. |
  | healthcheck, startup, or readiness mismatch | "healthcheck and release-evidence sections" | **No healthcheck section exists.** One bullet at `:28`, one at `:80`. |
  | service discovery or proxy misrouting | "DNS alias and shared-network sections" | Approximately — `:47` and `:54`. |
  | Swarm rollout or image pull problems | "runtime-mode split and registry sections" | Yes — `:31` and `:61`. |

  This directly violates the skill's own maintenance rule at `SKILL.md:78` ("Keep the topic map aligned with the actual headings in the full guide"), except that the file the rule names — `00-topic-map.md` — is the one that *is* aligned. The unaligned router is the one the rule does not mention.

- `SKILL.md:65-72` "Reference navigation" — three entries, one per reference file, each with a one-line charter. This is the only correct router in the skill, and under the settled convention (eight or fewer references ⇒ list them in `SKILL.md` with a trigger condition each) it is the *sufficient* router at today's file count. Its charters are descriptions rather than trigger conditions ("Full preserved guidance, rules, examples, and checklists" tells an agent nothing about when to open the file).

**`references/00-topic-map.md`** is not a router over reference files. It is a verbatim list of the sixteen `#` headings inside `full-guide.md` (`:7-22`) plus a two-line working rule (`:24-27`). At three reference files it is premature; at the twelve-file structure §10 proposes it becomes correct and necessary, and it must then be rewritten to route across files rather than within one.

**`references/SOURCES.md`** is a provenance ledger, not a router. It is legitimate, it is the best-constructed file in the skill, and it must be kept. Under the tree convention it should be renamed `references/00-source-map.md`; the only inbound pointer is `SKILL.md:71-72`.

**Maintenance rules** (`SKILL.md:74-81`) are a fourth pseudo-router. `:79` and `:80-81` are real policy and belong in a reference; `:76-78` are instructions to a future editor about file shape and belong in a maintaining-this-skill section of the companion-boundary reference, following the pattern already set by `alaa-frontend-devops/references/90-companion-boundary.md:36-41`.
