`SKILL.md:213-247` as a block.

**Executable checks the skill must ship and does not:**

1. **`scripts/summarize-openapi.sh --check`** — regenerate the machine-derivable matrix sections and
   diff against the committed file. Exit 0 identical, 3 drift, 2 could-not-run. Add `--help`,
   `--self-test` against a small fixture spec. **This is the single highest-value addition to the
   skill**, because it closes the drift hole in §9.
2. **`scripts/check-arvan-manifests.sh <rendered.yaml>`** — the missing enforcement of the
   non-negotiables. Assert: every container has `resources.requests` and `resources.limits`;
   `requests.cpu == limits.cpu` and `requests.memory == limits.memory`; memory equals the formula from
   §6.1; `ephemeral-storage` present when the values file sets the flag; no document uses a kind on the
   unsupported list; every HPA targets a `Deployment` and never a `StatefulSet`; every
   secret-or-configmap volume mounts under a dedicated subdirectory rather than onto an application
   directory. Exit 0 clean, 3 findings, 2 could-not-run.
3. **`scripts/check-rendered-secrets.sh <rendered.yaml> <values.secret.yaml>`** — base64-decode every
   `Secret.data` value in the rendered file and fail if any equals a value in the secret values file
   that was supposed to be delivered by reference rather than by chart-managed creation; fail if the
   rendered file is group- or world-readable. Exit 3 on either.
4. **Make `scripts/verify-cluster.sh` fail.** Exit 3 when `MISSING_REQUIRED` is non-empty (`:122-126`),
   exit 3 when a `can-i` the deployment depends on returns `no`, exit 2 when the cluster is
   unreachable, exit 0 only when everything the deployment needs is present. Add `tr -d '\r'` to the
   pipelines at `:87`, `:117` and `:140`. Add `--self-test`.
5. **Make `scripts/render-helm.sh --help` exit 0**, make the secret values file optional so the script
   can run in CI where it is absent by design, create `${OUT}` with mode 600, and remove it in a trap
   unless `--keep` is passed. Fix the header example at `:10`, which cannot work as written because
   `:47-48` require both default files to exist in the caller's CWD.
6. **Standardise exit codes across all three scripts on 0 clean / 2 could-not-run / 3 findings**, and
   give all three `--help` and `--self-test`.
7. Fix the three `arvann-` typos (`references/arvan-constraints.md:39`,
   `references/openapi-1.25-capability-matrix.md:5`, `scripts/summarize-openapi.sh:9`).
8. Replace all six `.codex/skills/caas-arvan-kuber/scripts/` invocations with a runtime-neutral form,
   and drop the redundant spec argument at `SKILL.md:306`.
9. Remove `--create-namespace` from `assets/README.operator.md.template:42`; replace
   `--rollback-on-failure` at `:46` and `assets/RUNBOOK.operator.md.template:39` with a Helm-version
   conditional and state the version floor; add `rendered.yaml` and `*.rendered.yaml` to the gitignore
   guidance at `assets/values.secret.yaml.example:6-9`; drop or mark
   `runnerRegistrationToken` at `:49` as legacy-only.

**Frontmatter, rewritten.** Keep the Arvan-first framing. Strengthen the do-not-use clause by
promoting `SKILL.md:64`. Add an explicit hand-off to `alaa-k8s-helm` so the two descriptions
discriminate from both sides:

> Arvan CaaS platform facts for Kubernetes and Helm workloads: the namespace-scoped API surface,
> alias-versus-canonical namespace RBAC identity, `requests == limits` admission parity, panel-managed
> domain, public IP and disk lifecycle, and the exposure annotations Arvan does not document. Use it
> when a manifest, chart or deployment decision would be different on Arvan than on a stock Kubernetes
> cluster of the same version. Do not use it for generic Kubernetes or Helm authoring, validation or
> debugging when the target is not Arvan — that is `alaa-k8s-helm`. Do not use it to write GitLab
> runner configuration or to decide pipeline gates. Do not invent undocumented Arvan behaviour when
> live discovery can answer the question.

That is 634 characters, no angle brackets, and it names both the positive trigger and two named
alternatives.

---

## Shared closing

### Question 1 — exactly what each says about the other

**`alaa-k8s-helm` never names `caas-arvan-kuber`.** Not once, in any of its twenty-one files. What it
does instead is claim the territory:

- `SKILL.md:3` (frontmatter description): "Use it when the task involves … or managed namespace
  platforms **such as Arvan Cloud Container**."
- `SKILL.md:112` (lane trigger): "Arvan Cloud Container or similar managed namespace/container
  platforms."
- `references/00-topic-map.md:15`: "Also read `references/openshift-and-managed-platforms.md` if the
  chart must work on OpenShift or **Arvan Cloud Container**."
- `references/00-topic-map.md:61-64`: a dedicated routing entry, "**### Answer Arvan Cloud Container
  questions**", pointing at two references.
- `references/openshift-and-managed-platforms.md:10, 194-212`: a 19-line "**Arvan Cloud Container
  guidance**" section listing what the platform supports (`:200-204`: direct manifest deployment, Helm,
  kubeconfig, panel workflows for domains, internal networking, dedicated IP, console access and
  scaling) and five practical rules (`:206-212`).

Because the description is what the runtime matches against, **`alaa-k8s-helm` currently wins the
trigger for any prompt that says "Arvan"** — it names the platform in its description, and
`caas-arvan-kuber`'s description says "Arvan" too but without the phrase "Arvan Cloud Container". An
agent that loads `alaa-k8s-helm` for an Arvan task gets `openshift-and-managed-platforms.md:194-212`,
which is accurate as far as it goes and contains **none** of the three facts that actually matter: not
the namespaced-only API surface, not the alias/canonical identity model, not `requests == limits`. It
will happily emit a `NetworkPolicy` and a `PodDisruptionBudget`, both of which the platform does not
serve, because `references/kubernetes-resource-patterns.md:154-171` and
`references/openshift-and-managed-platforms.md:233` recommend exactly that. **This is the concrete
production failure the seam currently produces.**

**`caas-arvan-kuber` names `alaa-k8s-helm` four times**, all in one block and all with bare names
carrying neither trigger form:

- `SKILL.md:164`: "### Kubernetes and Helm core — 1) `alaa-k8s-helm`"
- `SKILL.md:168`: "Build or refine templates/manifests with `alaa-k8s-helm`."
- `SKILL.md:170`: "Validate with `alaa-k8s-helm`."
- `SKILL.md:171`: "If runtime issues occur, use `alaa-k8s-helm` with Arvan RBAC/namespace facts."

Framed by `SKILL.md:160`: "`caas-arvan-kuber` sets policy; companion skills perform domain-specialized
generation/validation."

So the relationship is asserted from one side only, and asserted in the weaker direction: the
platform skill defers chart mechanics to the general skill, while the general skill claims the
platform. **The boundary as it stands is a boundary `alaa-k8s-helm` has not agreed to.**

### Question 2 — how much of `caas-arvan-kuber` is generic, and how much could only come from Arvan

Line-by-line across `SKILL.md`'s 440 lines:

**Generic Kubernetes or Helm that `alaa-k8s-helm` already owns — ~205 lines (47%):**

| Lines | What | Already owned at |
|---|---|---|
| `:76-82` | Secret handling, dual create/reference mode | Not owned by `alaa-k8s-helm` today — a real gap in *both* skills, but a generic one |
| `:83-85` | HPA by workload type | `alaa-k8s-helm/references/kubernetes-resource-patterns.md:253-262` |
| `:87-90` | Namespace-scoped RBAC first | `…/kubernetes-resource-patterns.md:226-229`, `…/openshift-and-managed-platforms.md:214-246` |
| `:92-94` | Config mount safety | Owned by neither — generic, and genuinely useful |
| `:96-98` | Discovery-aware API version selection | `alaa-k8s-helm/SKILL.md:21`, `…/version-awareness.md:59-68` |
| `:213-247` | Agent prompting practice, anti-patterns | `/alaa-prompting-guide` |
| `:249-291` | Two prompt templates | Generic scaffolding |
| `:293-375` | Four-phase execution loop | `alaa-k8s-helm/SKILL.md:41-127` lanes, `…/validation-workflows.md` |
| `:337-348` | Implementation rules (probes, Deployment vs StatefulSet, Service first, secrets, RBAC) | `alaa-k8s-helm/references/authoring-workflows.md:94-127`, `…/kubernetes-resource-patterns.md:15-64` |
| `:394-404` | CI/CD rules | `/alaa-gitlab-ci-cd` and `/alaa-docker-production` — see question 3 |

**Genuinely Arvan-specific — ~110 lines (25%):**

| Lines | What | Corroboration |
|---|---|---|
| `:70-74` | `requests == limits` parity, decimal cores, memory 2× | **Parity confirmed** by Arvan's manifest page; **decimal cores and 2× uncorroborated** |
| `:100-101` | CPU-generation affinity presets, opt-in | Uncorroborated; label key not given |
| `:103-108` | `LoadBalancer` + `arvancloud.ir/domain` + MetalLB pool; ingress mode separate; TLS at the edge | LoadBalancer confirmed by Arvan; **annotations undocumented by Arvan — this is the skill's most irreplaceable content** |
| `:110-142` | Namespaced-only API surface and the unsupported-kind list | Derived from the vendored spec; scope conclusions sound, version conclusions decayed (§7) |
| `:144-156` | Alias-versus-canonical namespace identity | **Undocumented anywhere by Arvan. Irreproducible from generic knowledge. The single most valuable thing in either skill.** |
| `:377-383` | Panel multi-doc YAML, platform default resources, CDN-managed DNS for domains, disruptive disk lifecycle, config-mount shadowing | Disk lifecycle **confirmed** by Arvan's disk page; the rest plausible |
| `:385-392` | GitLab Runner on Arvan | **Mixed** — items 5 and 6 are Arvan-specific, items 1–4 are generic GitLab Runner behaviour (question 3) |

**Structural or navigational — ~125 lines (28%):** the three routers, the mission block, when-to-use,
the companion-orchestration section, the definition of done, the package-resource list.

**In the references the ratio inverts and is much healthier.** `arvan-rbac-namespace-facts.md` is ~95%
Arvan-specific; `openapi-1.25-capability-matrix.md` is ~100%; `arvan-constraints.md` is ~80%, with the
generic remainder being sections 2 through 5 (`:17-35`) that mirror the body's non-negotiables;
`arvan-links.md` is ~90%. **The dilution is entirely in the always-loaded body**, which is exactly why
the §10 target of 6 KB is achievable without losing a single Arvan fact.

**The honest summary:** roughly one line in four of the always-loaded body is Arvan knowledge. But
that quarter is high-density, verified in three places against Arvan's own documentation, includes one
model (alias/canonical identity) that exists in no public source, and is directly load-bearing for a
live repository — `service-ci-kit/ci/scripts/deploy.sh:15` defaults its namespace to `vk`, the exact
alias-namespace example from `references/arvan-rbac-namespace-facts.md:10`. This is not a skill built
from generic knowledge with an Arvan label. It is a small, real, hard-won core wrapped in a large,
avoidable shell.

### Question 3 — does the CI/CD material collide with `alaa-gitlab-ci-cd`?

**Yes, on the expression half, cleanly and provably. And the collision exposes a hole in D8 itself.**

D8 as stated: `alaa-gitlab-ci-cd` **owns how a gate is expressed on a runner and decides no gate.**

`caas-arvan-kuber` writes both halves.

**Collision A — runner expression.** `SKILL.md:385-392`, "GitLab Runner on Arvan (important)":

- `:387` "Job pods include multiple containers (`build`, `helper`, `init-permissions`) and all need
  pull access."
- `:388` "Ensure executor job pods render `image_pull_secrets`."
- `:389` "Keep helper image pinned and reachable in restricted egress environments."
- `:390` "Separate manager pod pull config from executor job pod pull config."

`image_pull_secrets` and `helper_image` are `[runners.kubernetes]` keys in GitLab Runner's
`config.toml`. `alaa-gitlab-ci-cd/SKILL.md:3` claims exactly that surface ("GitLab Runner
`config.toml`, Helm `values.yaml`, GitLab Kubernetes executor behavior"), routes it to
`references/runner-shell-and-kubernetes.md` (`SKILL.md:33`), and ships
`assets/templates/values-k8s-runner.yaml`, which is a complete `[runners.kubernetes]` block
(`:16-32`: `namespace`, `service_account`, `image`, `helper_image_autoset_arch_and_os`,
`allowed_pull_policies`, `allowed_images`, `allowed_services`, `node_selector`). **These four rules
are runner expression and belong to `alaa-gitlab-ci-cd` under D8.**

They are also, and this settles it, **not Arvan facts**. Multi-container job pods, `image_pull_secrets`
on the executor, helper-image pinning and the manager-versus-executor pull split are documented GitLab
Runner behaviour on any Kubernetes cluster — and `caas-arvan-kuber` says so itself:
`references/arvan-links.md:48-54` cites GitLab's own Kubernetes-executor and Helm-chart pages as the
source, and `:62-63` cites the runner chart's version caveats. The skill is restating another vendor's
documentation for a component another skill owns.

**And `alaa-gitlab-ci-cd` does not currently cover it.** A grep across the whole skill returns exactly
one hit for pull configuration — `SKILL.md:111`, "Prefer `pull_policy: always` on shared or untrusted
runners for private images" — and **zero** for `image_pull_secrets` or `imagePullSecrets`. So this is
not a case of two skills saying the same thing. It is a case of the *wrong* skill saying it and the
right skill being silent. Phase 2 must move `:387-390` into
`alaa-gitlab-ci-cd/references/runner-shell-and-kubernetes.md` — a transfer, not a deletion. This is an
out-of-lane recommendation and is recorded as such below.

`SKILL.md:391-392` are different and must **stay**:

- `:391` "Do not rely on `kubectl create namespace`, `kubectl get namespace`, or
  `helm --create-namespace` in runner jobs unless live evidence proves the runner has that scope" —
  a consequence of Arvan's namespaced-only API surface.
- `:392` "For RBAC denials, evaluate alias/canonical namespace identity mismatch before broadening
  privileges" — pure Arvan.

**Collision B — gate decision.** `SKILL.md:394-404` and `:399-403`: "Standard validation gates before
deploy: `helm lint`, `helm template`, optional schema validation (`kubeconform`/`kubeval`), optional
`kubectl apply --dry-run=client`". Plus `:396` immutable tags and digest pinning, `:397` pinned and
mirrored CI tooling images, `:398` never print registry or API secrets, `:404` migration and init jobs
must meet resource constraints. Restated at `SKILL.md:351-361` and
`references/arvan-constraints.md:96`.

Under D8, `alaa-gitlab-ci-cd` decides no gate — but D8 named `alaa-frontend-devops` as the gate
register owner *for frontend*, and **named no gate-register owner for backend Kubernetes delivery**.
Digest pinning and image minimisation route to `alaa-docker-production`. So `:396-397` go there. That
leaves `:399-403` — the Kubernetes and Helm gates — genuinely ownerless under D8 as written.

**The seam recommendation on this point:** name
`alaa-k8s-helm/references/validation-workflows.md` the **Kubernetes and Helm delivery gate register**,
the direct structural analogue of `alaa-frontend-devops`'s register. It already *is* one in everything
but name: seven ordered stages (`:19-25`), each with the predicate, the command, and the artifact
inspected (`:47-100`, `:104-176`). Under that:

- **`alaa-k8s-helm/references/validation-workflows.md` decides which checks must pass before a
  Kubernetes or Helm change is applied, and what artifact each check inspects.** It writes no
  `.gitlab-ci.yml` and no Dockerfile.
- **`alaa-gitlab-ci-cd` owns how each of those gates is placed on a runner** — the job graph, `rules:`,
  `needs:`, cache keys, artifact retention, the runner image reference, and the `[runners.kubernetes]`
  block — **and decides no gate.**
- **`alaa-docker-production` owns how the image the chart references is built and hardened** — layer
  ordering, multi-stage separation, digest pinning, minimisation — **and decides no gate.**
- **`caas-arvan-kuber` contributes Arvan-only gate *predicates* to that register** — "no rendered
  container may omit resources and `requests` must equal `limits`"; "no rendered document may use a
  kind absent from the Arvan capability matrix"; "no rendered manifest may reference a cluster-scoped
  object" — **and owns no gate placement and no runner configuration.**

That closes the hole without inventing a skill, gives each of the four a sentence it can state in its
own words, and gives `caas-arvan-kuber` a legitimate, narrow role in CI instead of the broad one it
currently claims.

### Question 4 — two skills or one, and the deciding test

**Keep two skills.** The merge does not survive the scrutiny a new-skill proposal would face, and the
discrimination that exists today is worth more than the duplication costs.

**The case for merging, stated fairly.** Roughly 47% of `caas-arvan-kuber`'s always-loaded body is
generic Kubernetes and Helm that `alaa-k8s-helm` already owns better. The two skills duplicate each
other on HPA policy, RBAC scoping, API-version selection, validation gates and the render-then-dry-run
loop. `alaa-k8s-helm` already ships an 8 KB managed-platform reference with an Arvan section in it, so
the merged home exists. One skill would mean one router, one version file, one set of scripts, and no
seam to police. And `caas-arvan-kuber` fails seven of ten criteria — a skill that fails that many is
not obviously worth preserving as a unit.

**Why it still fails.**

1. **Three sibling skills route to it by name.** `alaa-docker-production/SKILL.md:56` ("Pair when the
   task touches the primary Arvan Kubernetes production path, Helm values, OCI charts, or GitLab
   rollout mechanics"), `alaa-makefile/SKILL.md:141` ("Pair with `$caas-arvan-kuber` when Make targets
   enforce Arvan-safe delivery behavior"), `alaa-haproxy/SKILL.md:88` ("Pair when HAProxy runs on Arvan
   CaaS"). All three are in this batch and all three would have to be rewritten to point at a skill
   whose description does not contain the word "Arvan" — because if `alaa-k8s-helm` absorbed the
   content, its description would have to name Arvan to keep the trigger, and then it is a general
   skill advertising a specific vendor, which is defect class 4 written into the frontmatter on
   purpose. `alaa-makefile:141-142` is the sharpest evidence: it lists `$alaa-k8s-helm` and
   `$caas-arvan-kuber` on **consecutive lines** with **different trigger conditions** — "wrappers
   around Helm or Kubernetes operations" versus "enforce Arvan-safe delivery behavior". The
   discrimination is not theoretical; a sibling skill already relies on it.

2. **The Arvan core is real, current, and irreproducible.** Three of its claims verified against
   Arvan's own documentation today (`requests == limits`, stateless-only scaling with persistent
   storage disabling it, increase-only disks with disruptive detach and delete). One — the
   alias-versus-canonical namespace identity model — exists in no public source at all and is directly
   load-bearing for a live repository. A merge would put all of that behind a general skill's trigger,
   where it would be loaded on every Kubernetes task and read on none of the ones that need it.

3. **The failure mode of merging is worse than the failure mode of keeping two.** Two skills with a
   bad seam produces an agent that loads the general skill for an Arvan task and emits a NetworkPolicy
   — bad, and fixable by fixing the seam. One merged skill produces an agent that loads Arvan platform
   constraints for every vanilla Kubernetes task and refuses to emit a `PodDisruptionBudget` on a
   cluster that has served `policy/v1` since 1.21 — worse, and not fixable at all, because there is no
   longer a trigger boundary to correct.

4. **The duplication argument cuts the other way once the body is fixed.** After §10, the overlap
   drops from ~205 lines to near zero: everything generic is deleted and replaced with delegation
   lines. Merging to remove duplication that deduplication already removes is paying the trigger cost
   for a benefit already banked.

**The one-sentence deciding test:**

> **Load `/caas-arvan-kuber` (`$caas-arvan-kuber`) only when the answer depends on a fact that is true
> of Arvan's cluster and false of a stock Kubernetes cluster of the same minor version — the
> namespace-only API surface, alias-versus-canonical namespace RBAC identity, `requests == limits`
> admission parity, or the panel-managed domain, public-IP and disk lifecycle; for every other
> question about Helm, manifests, rollout, exposure or debugging, load `/alaa-k8s-helm`
> (`$alaa-k8s-helm`), including when the target happens to be Arvan.**

The test is answerable before either skill is loaded, it is symmetric — both skills can state it and
both descriptions can encode it — and its final clause is the part that does the work: *being on Arvan
is not by itself a reason to load the Arvan skill.* That single clause is what stops `caas-arvan-kuber`
from re-accreting generic Kubernetes content the next time someone deploys to Arvan and reaches for it.

**The three seam repairs Phase 2 must make, in order:**

1. **`alaa-k8s-helm` gives up the claim.** Remove "such as Arvan Cloud Container" from `SKILL.md:3`.
   Delete `SKILL.md:112`. Replace `references/00-topic-map.md:61-64` with a one-line delegation.
   Change `references/00-topic-map.md:15` to say OpenShift only. Move
   `references/openshift-and-managed-platforms.md:194-212` wholesale into
   `caas-arvan-kuber/references/arvan-constraints.md`, preserving it completely, and leave behind a
   delegation line at that location. **Keep** the generic managed-namespace-platform section at
   `:56-66` — an agent facing an unidentified managed platform still needs a posture, and that posture
   is genuinely general.
2. **`caas-arvan-kuber` gives up the generic 47%** per §10, and fixes all ten cross-skill call sites
   to the dual `/name` (`$name`) form.
3. **Both state the seam in their own words**, in their own bodies, using the deciding test above —
   because a boundary asserted from one side only is the state they are in today.

### Live-repository defects (become RFCs, not skill edits)

Found in `/mnt/user-data/uploads/service-ci-kit/`, which is the live consumer of both skills in this
lane. None of these is a skill edit.

1. **`ci/scripts/deploy.sh:15` — `namespace="${HELM_NAMESPACE:-vk}"`.** A shared kit defaults its
   deployment namespace to a specific tenant's alias namespace. `vk` is the exact alias-namespace
   example in `caas-arvan-kuber/references/arvan-rbac-namespace-facts.md:10`. Any consumer that forgets
   to set `HELM_NAMESPACE` deploys into another project's namespace, and `require_env` (`:19-25`)
   deliberately does not guard it. **Make `HELM_NAMESPACE` required with no default.**
2. **`ci/scripts/deploy.sh:275-276` — the pull-secret shotgun.** The script sets both
   `image.pullSecrets[0].name` and `imagePullSecrets[0].name` because the chart's value shape is
   unknown. Helm's `--set-string` on a path no template reads is silently ignored, so if the chart uses
   a third shape — `global.imagePullSecrets`, say — the deploy succeeds, the pods enter
   `ImagePullBackOff`, and `--atomic` rolls back with an error that names neither cause. **The chart's
   values contract should be asserted once at `helm template` time, not guessed at set time.**
3. **`ci/scripts/deploy.sh:69-92` — `ensure_namespace_reachable` runs `kubectl get namespace`**, which
   `caas-arvan-kuber/SKILL.md:391` and `references/arvan-constraints.md:31` both explicitly tell runner
   jobs not to rely on. The kit handles `forbidden` correctly by warning and continuing (`:81-84`), but
   **hard-dies on `NotFound` (`:87-89`)** — and `NotFound` is precisely what an alias-versus-canonical
   namespace mismatch produces for an identity that can nonetheless perform every namespaced operation
   it needs. The probe can fail the deploy for a namespace that works. **Downgrade `NotFound` to a
   warning and let the first namespaced operation be the real test.**
4. **`ci/scripts/deploy.sh:166` — `chart_ver="${HELM_CHART_VERSION:-0.2.6}"`.** A hardcoded chart
   version floor in a shared kit. Every consumer that does not override it is pinned to whatever
   `0.2.6` was on the day it was written, and nothing reports the drift.
5. **`ci/scripts/deploy.sh:283` — `--atomic`.** Renamed to `--rollback-on-failure` in Helm 4. It
   survives here only because the call is `helm upgrade --install`, which kept the legacy binding with
   a deprecation warning; `helm install --atomic` now fails outright. **Gate the flag on the detected
   Helm major.**
6. **`ci/scripts/deploy.sh:192-231` — hand-rolled `${VAR}` interpolation over a Helm values file.**
   It is fail-closed on a missing variable (`:213-214`, correct) and it re-scans the rendered output
   and dies on any residue (`:223-225`, correct). But it substitutes inside YAML comments and inside
   quoted literals with no awareness of either, and a substituted **value** that itself contains a `$`
   trips the residue check and kills the deploy with a message that blames the template. It also drops
   the trailing newline (`printf '%s'` at `:221`). **Use `helm --set-string` or `envsubst` with an
   explicit variable allowlist.**

### Out-of-batch findings

Recorded, not proposed for fixing.

1. **`ansible-validator/references/playbook.md:1106,1108`** lists `alaa-k8s-helm` twice in a
   three-item "works well in combination with" list, with two different rationales ("When Ansible
   manages Kubernetes or Helm resources" and "For debugging Kubernetes infrastructure managed by
   Ansible"), and uses bare names with no trigger syntax. `ansible-validator` is in Batch 7, so this is
   in-batch for edits but out of *this lane*.
2. **`alaa-gitlab-ci-cd` has no coverage of `image_pull_secrets` or `helper_image` for the Kubernetes
   executor** — one hit for `pull_policy` at `SKILL.md:111` and none for pull secrets anywhere,
   including in `references/runner-shell-and-kubernetes.md` and
   `assets/templates/values-k8s-runner.yaml`. It is the D8 owner of that surface and the surface is
   empty, while `caas-arvan-kuber/SKILL.md:387-390` holds the content. In-batch, out of this lane;
   flagged for whichever lane owns `alaa-gitlab-ci-cd`.
3. **`alaa-docker-production/SKILL.md:56`, `alaa-makefile/SKILL.md:141` and `alaa-haproxy/SKILL.md:88`**
   all use the bare `$caas-arvan-kuber` form with no `/` variant. In-batch, out of this lane. Note that
   these are the three call sites the merge recommendation in question 4 turns on, so whichever lane
   edits them must not change their targets.
4. **`UPGRADE-CARRYOVER.md:188`** asserts stale hardcoded model names in `alaa-k8s-helm`,
   `caas-arvan-kuber` and `alaa-gitlab-ci-cd`. Verified false for the two skills in this lane: both
   already carry the corrected `/alaa-prompting-guide` pointer, and no model or effort key exists
   anywhere in either directory including assets, emitted templates and `agents/openai.yaml`. The
   carry-over should be corrected so a later batch does not go looking again.

### Is a new skill required?

**No.**

Every gap this lane found has an owner that can take it without violating its own boundary. The
missing reliability, security, observability, testing and contract material is not new territory —
it is five existing doctrine owners that neither skill names, and the fix is call sites, not a skill.
The Kubernetes and Helm gate register that D8 leaves ownerless is not a new skill either: it is
`alaa-k8s-helm/references/validation-workflows.md`, which already contains the register in
everything but the name, and naming it costs one sentence in a file that already exists. The runner
material that `caas-arvan-kuber` should not hold moves to `alaa-gitlab-ci-cd`, which claims that
surface in its own description. The Arvan core stays where it is, in a skill three siblings already
route to by name.

The one thing that genuinely does not exist anywhere in this lane is an **executable checker for
rendered Kubernetes manifests** — nothing today can fail a Deployment that ships with no resources,
no probes, `privileged: true` and a mutated selector, in either skill. But that is a script, and it
belongs in `alaa-k8s-helm/scripts/` next to the two checkers already there, with `caas-arvan-kuber`
contributing Arvan-only predicates to it. A skill whose entire content is one script is not a skill.
Proposing one here would be inventing a boundary to justify a file.
## Appendix E — `alaa-haproxy`

### 1. What this skill is today

**Shape.** `SKILL.md` is 6,653 B on disk, roughly 6,285 B net of frontmatter, 151 lines. Five references
totalling 21,612 B: `00-topic-map.md` (2,742 B), `full-guide.md` (10,540 B), `20-platform-delivery.md`
(4,542 B), `30-security-observability.md` (2,083 B), `SOURCES.md` (1,695 B). `agents/openai.yaml` is
273 B. Thirty examples: nineteen `examples/haproxy/*.cfg`, seven `examples/kubernetes/*.yaml`, two
`examples/helm/*.yaml`, one GitHub Actions workflow, one GitLab CI snippet. **No scripts of any kind,
no `__pycache__`, no `.gitignore` in the skill directory.**

**Register.** The body is a clean router plus a version policy. The references are competent operational
prose but they are almost entirely *bullet lists of preferences* — "Prefer", "Review … together", "Use …
when", "where possible" — rather than rules with a stated scope and an observable condition. The examples
are the densest and most valuable content in the skill and they are also where its defects live, because
they are the only part an agent copies verbatim.

**Self-description.** `SKILL.md:12-15` positions the skill as covering "HAProxy `3.2`, which is still
marked `LTS`" and "HAProxy `3.3`, which is newer but not marked `LTS`". That two-branch framing is the
organising principle of the whole skill — the `-3.3.cfg` filename suffix, the branch-strategy section
(`SKILL.md:68-71`), the upgrade notes (`full-guide.md:125-161`) and the topic map (`00-topic-map.md:5-9`)
all rest on it. Section 7 shows that framing is now factually wrong on both halves.

**What it never mentions.** Grepped and confirmed absent from the entire skill: `cache`, `max-age`,
`immutable`, `etag`, `compression`, `monitor-uri`, `monitor fail`, `option redispatch`, `maxqueue`,
`show peers`, `localpeer`, config-preprocessor `.if`, environment-variable expansion `${…}`, `observe`,
`error-limit`, and the name `alaa-haproxy-lua`.

---

### 2. Ten-criteria verdict

| # | Criterion | Verdict | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Correctness and testability | **FAIL** | The skill mandates `haproxy -c -f <cfg>` at `SKILL.md:63` and `full-guide.md:12` but ships zero executable checks, so nothing enforces it. At least one shipped example does not parse: `examples/haproxy/09-connection-reuse.cfg:19` writes `idle-ping 30s` as a bare line in a `frontend` section, but `idle-ping` is a `bind` argument and a `server` argument, never a proxy-level directive (verified §7). `examples/haproxy/08-consistent-hash-affinity.cfg:14` writes `balance hash` with no sample expression. |
| 2 | Failure behaviour | **FAIL** | The peers question is unanswered anywhere: `full-guide.md:41-45` says only "Add peers when abuse state or affinity must survive node-level balancing" and "Test peer recovery and node loss before calling it production-ready" — the reader is told to test it, never told what the answer is. `option redispatch`, `maxqueue` and `show peers` appear nowhere. `hard-stop-after` is named once at `full-guide.md:24` and used in none of the nineteen configs. `01-baseline-http-tls.cfg:30` sets `retries 3` with no `option redispatch`, so all three retries go to the same failed server. |
| 3 | Security | **FAIL** | `30-security-observability.md:12` reduces PROXY protocol to "Use PROXY protocol only when both sides explicitly support it" — a compatibility statement, not a trust statement. `11-proxy-protocol-chain.cfg:17` and `18-tiered-edge-gateway.cfg:17` both put `accept-proxy` on a public `:443` listener; nothing in the skill says the PROXY header is an unauthenticated source-IP assertion that HAProxy trusts unconditionally. The `peers` listener in `12-peers-global-rate-limit.cfg:14-16` has no TLS and no authentication and the skill's nine security rules (`30-security-observability.md:5-13`) do not mention it. |
| 4 | Observability | **FAIL** | The skill names an owner at `SKILL.md:92-93` — "`$alaa-observability-soc` … Pair when logs, metrics, traces, alerting, or incident evidence requirements extend beyond HAProxy itself" — but that call site does not cover HAProxy's *own* new failure modes, and the skill decides the log-field list itself at `30-security-observability.md:17-23`. Concretely: a peers session going down is undiagnosable because `show peers` is never named, and the shipped bundle makes metrics unscrapable (`haproxy-configmap.yaml:30` binds `127.0.0.1:8405` while `haproxy-service.yaml:30-31` and `haproxy-servicemonitor.yaml:11` target that port through the pod IP). |
| 5 | Concurrency and load | **FAIL** | `04-rate-limit-stick-table.cfg:26` and `12-peers-global-rate-limit.cfg:28` use `http-request pause` as the first response to a flood. Pausing holds the stream and its resources instead of releasing them, so under a connection-exhaustion attack `pause` amplifies the load it is meant to shed; `04` sets no `maxconn` at all, in `global` or on the frontend. `full-guide.md:33` says only "Review `http-reuse`, queue behavior, and `strict-maxconn` together" — three nouns and a verb, no rule. `maxqueue`, backpressure and load shedding appear nowhere. |
| 6 | Clean code / config structure | **FAIL** | The domain analogue is config a reviewer can reason about, and its single most important rule — how a `defaults` section is associated with the proxies it governs — is absent (see §6 and the trust-boundary finding). `full-guide.md:197` says "Replace repeated ACL trees with maps where possible", a preference verb where a constraint was meant. No rule about section ordering, section naming, or config modularity. |
| 7 | Algorithm and data-structure choice | **FAIL** | Stick tables are sized `200k` (`04:18`) and `500k` (`12:20`) with no memory formula and no statement of what happens at saturation (LRU eviction of the oldest entry, which silently drops the attacker you were tracking). `08-consistent-hash-affinity.cfg` selects consistent hashing without stating the rebalance cost it buys. No complexity or memory budget is stated anywhere in the skill. |
| 8 | Configurability | **FAIL** | Every one of the nineteen configs hardcodes addresses, ports, certificate paths and thresholds — `gt 150`, `gt 50`, `ge 5` at `12:23-27`; `gt 200`, `ge 10` at `04:22-29`. HAProxy's official configurability capabilities — environment-variable expansion in the config file and the `.if`/`.elif`/`.endif` preprocessor — are never named, which also breaches the standing preference for wrapping the official capability rather than working around it. |
| 9 | Speed of development and debuggability | **SATISFIED** | `SKILL.md:59-65` is a genuine five-step quick start. `full-guide.md:212-219` gives "the first file to copy when …" for six scenarios, which is the right shape for fast work. `full-guide.md:186-208` organises troubleshooting by symptom class (502/503 bursts, tail latency, FD exhaustion, DNS churn) rather than as a numbered procedure. Gap, not a failure: `SKILL.md:117-129` lists only 13 of the 19 configs, so `02`, `05`, `06`, `07`, `08` and `09` are invisible from the always-loaded body. |
| 10 | Documentation | **FAIL** | No example states its preconditions, its target branch, or its failure mode in-file. `03-quic-http3.cfg` needs a QUIC-capable build and a specific TLS stack and says neither. `16-server-tls-sni-auto-3.3.cfg` carries one comment line. There is no operational runbook: nothing states what a given directive change means for live traffic or how to tell it went wrong. |

**Tally: 1 SATISFIED, 9 FAIL, 0 NOT-OWNED.**

No `NOT-OWNED` verdict is legitimate here. The skill names five companions at `SKILL.md:88-99`, but each
call site is a "pair when" hint rather than a delegation of a criterion, and in the two places where the
skill could have delegated (log field names, metric catalog) it decides the matter itself instead.

---

### 3. Defect classes actually found

**1. Stale model pins.** None. No model name appears anywhere in the skill. Clean.

**2. Wrong trigger syntax.** **Confirmed, total.** Every cross-runtime call site uses only the Codex `$`
form: `$caas-arvan-kuber` (`SKILL.md:88`), `$alaa-docker-production` (`:90`), `$alaa-observability-soc`
(`:92`), `$alaa-security-review` (`:94`), `$alaa-trust-gateway-auth` (`:96`),
`$alaa-crockford-base32-codecs` (`:98`). A grep for `/alaa-` and `/caas-` across the whole skill returns
nothing. From Claude Code every companion in this skill is uninvocable. `agents/openai.yaml:4` correctly
uses the bare `$` form and is exempt.

**3. Duplication between body and references.** Confirmed and extensive — see §5.

**4. Project-specific content in an always-loaded body.** `SKILL.md:19-25` pins three concrete release
facts (`3.2` labelled LTS, `3.2.15`, `3.3.6`, all dated `2026-03-19`) into the always-loaded body. These
are the fastest-decaying facts in the skill and they are loaded on every invocation. They belong in a
version reference with a `read:` date.

**5. Long numbered procedures.** Not present. `full-guide.md:186-208` is already symptom-organised. This
defect class does not apply.

**6. Description that only says when to use.** **Confirmed.** `SKILL.md:3` is 343 characters, contains no
angle brackets (validation-safe), and is well under 900. But it ends at "with live official-source
verification for version-sensitive guidance" and never says when *not* to use the skill. The body has a
"When NOT to use" section (`SKILL.md:37-41`) whose content never reaches the description, which is the
only text the router sees. The description also carries the stale claim "the current HAProxy 3.2 LTS line
and the newer 3.3 line" (§7), so the trigger surface itself is misinforming.

**7. Fragile tooling.** Not applicable — no scripts.

**8. Shipped `__pycache__`.** None.

**9. Measured against section 2.** Done in §2. Nine of ten fail.

**10. Shrink where possible.** The body can lose roughly 1,800 B: the live-version block
(`SKILL.md:17-25`, duplicated in `full-guide.md:5-10`), the 3.3 additions list (`:73-84`, duplicated in
`full-guide.md:127-136`) and the example bundle list (`:114-143`, duplicated in `00-topic-map.md:62-83`).

**11. Companion boundary.** **Confirmed missing.** There is no `90-companion-boundary.md` and no
"what this skill does not own" statement anywhere. `SKILL.md:88-99` is a "pair when" list, which is a
collaboration hint, not a boundary: it never says *this decision is not mine*.

**12. Two routers.** **Confirmed.** `SKILL.md:114-143` lists the example bundles and
`00-topic-map.md:62-83` lists them again as "Example index". Not verbatim — and the divergence is the
harm: the topic map indexes all nineteen configs with a one-line charter each, the body lists only
thirteen and gives no charter. An agent reading the body cannot discover `02`, `05`, `06`, `07`, `08` or
`09`. `SOURCES.md` is a legitimate provenance ledger and must not be touched.

**13. A rule with no tool that reports a violation.** **Confirmed, absolute.** The skill ships **zero**
executable checks. It states two commands an agent should run (`haproxy -vv`, `haproxy -c -f`) and ships
two CI files that run them against a *hypothetical consumer* config (`haproxy/haproxy.cfg`), never against
the skill's own nineteen examples. Nothing in the skill would have caught `09:19` or `08:14`.

---

### 4. Boundary map

#### 4.1 The D8 reciprocal sentence `alaa-haproxy` must state

The ruling: **`alaa-haproxy` owns how a cache or routing decision is expressed as a directive and decides
no policy.** The counterpart is already written from the other side, twice:
`alaa-frontend-devops/references/90-companion-boundary.md:17` — "how a cache or routing decision is
expressed as a directive: headers, compression, rewrites | `/alaa-haproxy` (`$alaa-haproxy`)" — and
`alaa-frontend-devops/references/30-serving-caching-and-public-path.md:5` — "This file states **policy**.
The directive that implements a policy on the edge — the header block, the compression settings, the path
rewrite, the cache rule — is written by `/alaa-haproxy` (`$alaa-haproxy`). This skill decides what the
policy is because the policy follows from how the build names its files; that skill decides how it is
expressed and it decides no policy."

The exact sentence `alaa-haproxy` states about its own half, to be written into `SKILL.md` and repeated
nowhere else:

> `alaa-haproxy` owns how a cache or routing decision is expressed as an HAProxy directive — the
> `http-response set-header Cache-Control` rule, the `compression` settings, the path rewrite, the
> deep-link fallback, and the ACL or map that selects a backend — and it decides no policy: which
> `Cache-Control` value belongs to which response class is decided by `/alaa-frontend-devops`
> (`$alaa-frontend-devops`), `references/30-serving-caching-and-public-path.md`, because that policy
> follows from whether the build gave the file a content-hashed name and the build owns that. When a
> caching or routing task arrives here without a stated policy, this skill asks for the policy and emits
> no directive rather than choosing a default.

The final clause is load-bearing and must survive Phase 2. "Decides no policy" on its own is silent about
the case that actually occurs — the policy is simply absent — and an agent will fill that silence with
`max-age=3600`, which is wrong for every row of the frontend skill's table
(`alaa-frontend-devops/references/30-serving-caching-and-public-path.md:19-26`).

#### 4.2 The ruling tested against the current text

**Finding first, because it dominates: the skill currently owns nothing on its half.** A grep for `cache`,
`max-age`, `immutable`, `etag`, `compression` and `content hash` across all 37 files of `alaa-haproxy`
returns **zero matches**. The frontend skill lists four obligations at
`alaa-frontend-devops/references/30-serving-caching-and-public-path.md:36-39` and `alaa-haproxy` answers
none of them:

- "The asset path prefix arrives at the origin unchanged: no rewrite strips it, and no rewrite duplicates
  it." — no `http-request set-path`, `replace-path` or `replace-uri` guidance exists.
- "HTML responses are not stored under the immutable policy that applies to hashed assets." — no
  `Cache-Control` guidance exists.
- "Compression is applied by content type, and the response body is byte-identical after decompression to
  the file on disk. A compression setting that alters bytes breaks Subresource Integrity" — `compression
  algo` and `compression type` are never named, and the SRI consequence is never stated.
- "Deep links resolve to the SPA or SSR entry, and a hard refresh on a deep link returns the same document
  as a client-side navigation to it." — no SPA fallback pattern exists.

So D8 for this skill is not a restatement exercise. It is a **genuinely new capability** the skill must
gain, and it is the one place where growth is unambiguously the right answer.

**Places where the skill decides a policy it may not decide.** Quoted, with disposition:

| Location | Quoted text | Disposition |
|---|---|---|
| `30-security-observability.md:17-23` | "Use a stable log format that includes: request ID / client address or trusted forwarded address / frontend and backend name / server name / status code / timing fields" | **Restated with the policy routed.** The field *names* and their contract are `alaa-services-contract`'s ("every shared name and value: log fields, metric catalog"). `alaa-haproxy` keeps the directive half: how a `log-format` string emits those fields, and the fact that `%[term_events]` exists. Rewrite as: "emit the fields named in `alaa-services-contract`; the `log-format` line that emits them is written here." |
| `30-security-observability.md:29` | "Track frontend and backend traffic, retries, errors, queue depth, and saturation." | **Restated with the policy routed.** The metric catalog is `alaa-services-contract`'s and the requirement level is `alaa-observability-soc`'s. This skill owns which HAProxy exporter surface exposes them. |
| `30-security-observability.md:30` | "Keep scrape endpoints cluster-private when possible." | **Restated as a directive.** "When possible" is a preference verb on a security control. The exposure decision is `alaa-security-review`'s; the directive is the `bind` address on the metrics frontend, which is this skill's. |
| `30-security-observability.md:5` | "Run as a non-root user when binding only high ports inside containers or pods." | **Moves.** Container user policy is `alaa-docker-production` and `alaa-k8s-helm`. What stays is HAProxy's own `user`/`group`/`chroot` directives and which ports the config binds. |
| `20-platform-delivery.md:7` | "Prefer high container ports and publish them via the Service or host mapping instead of requiring root inside the container." | **Moves** to `alaa-docker-production` / `alaa-k8s-helm`; restated here only as the `bind` port the config uses. |
| `20-platform-delivery.md:14-19` | "Run at least two replicas for externally visible traffic… Use a checksum annotation in Helm… Use a PodDisruptionBudget and anti-affinity or topology spread constraints." | **Moves** to `alaa-k8s-helm` and `caas-arvan-kuber`. Replica counts, PDBs and anti-affinity are not HAProxy directives. |
| `20-platform-delivery.md:22-26` | "Readiness should represent whether the pod can still accept new traffic. Liveness should be conservative. Startup probes are useful when…" | **Split.** Probe *design* moves to `alaa-k8s-helm`. What `alaa-haproxy` owes and does not supply is the directive that makes a readiness probe able to fail — `monitor-uri` plus `monitor fail`, which appear nowhere in the skill. |
| `20-platform-delivery.md:30-33` | "Prefer a non-root container… Set `allowPrivilegeEscalation: false`. Add `readOnlyRootFilesystem: true` only when the required runtime directories are mounted writable. Use a NetworkPolicy when…" | **Moves** to `alaa-k8s-helm` / `alaa-security-review`, except the HAProxy half that stays: *which* paths HAProxy must be able to write — the stats socket directory, `/var/lib/haproxy` when `chroot` is set, and the `shm-stats-file` path on 3.3. |
| `20-platform-delivery.md:41-56` | The whole "Helm patterns" section, "Good Helm values usually expose: image repository and tag / replica count / update strategy / …" | **Moves** to `alaa-k8s-helm`. This is chart authorship. |
| `20-platform-delivery.md:64-67` | "Minimum gates: print `haproxy -vv` / run `haproxy -c -f ...` on the effective config / render Helm templates if Helm is involved / dry-run Kubernetes manifests with `kubectl apply --dry-run=client`" | **Split.** The two HAProxy predicates stay and become a proper gate register (predicate, command, artifact). `helm lint`, `helm template` and `kubectl apply --dry-run` are `alaa-k8s-helm` predicates and move. |
| `20-platform-delivery.md:73` | "keep at least one GitLab CI or GitHub Actions example in-repo so agents can adapt from a known-good pattern" | **Retire.** This is the instruction that manufactures the boundary violation. Deleting the two files without deleting this line guarantees they come back. |
| `20-platform-delivery.md:77` | "prefer rolling changes with a fast rollback path" | **Moves** to `alaa-controlled-ops` / `alaa-k8s-helm`. `:78-79` ("keep one branch-aware config per estate", "do not mix experimental `3.3` directives into a shared config") **stays** — that is HAProxy-specific and correct. |

**The two provider-YAML files.**

`examples/gitlab-ci/gitlab-ci-snippet.yml` is the clearer case. It contains `stages:` (`:1-4`), `needs:`
(`:34-35`, `:43-44`), `rules:` with `when: manual` (`:45-47`), `artifacts: paths:` (`:25-27`) and two
runner image references, `alpine/helm:3.17.3` (`:20`) and `bitnami/kubectl:1.32` (`:30`, `:38`). Every one
of those is named in the ruling as routing to `alaa-gitlab-ci-cd`: "the job graph, `rules:` and `needs:`,
artifact retention and `expire_in`, and the runner image reference". **Moves** — retire the file and
restate its only HAProxy content (two commands and the branch of the binary they run under) as two rows
of the gate register.

`examples/github-actions/haproxy-validate.yml` is the same violation in a second provider's dialect:
`on:`/`jobs:`/`steps:` (`:3-13`), three marketplace actions (`actions/checkout@v4`,
`azure/setup-helm@v4`, `azure/setup-kubectl@v4`), and a pinned `version: v1.32.0` (`:21`). **Moves** —
retire, restate as gate-register rows. Note that a skill holding one GitLab file *and* one GitHub file is
evidence the content is provider-shaped rather than HAProxy-shaped: the HAProxy substance is identical in
both and occupies four lines out of the combined fifty.

What the register rows should say, carrying no provider syntax at all:

- *Predicate:* the effective config parses under the binary that will run it. *Command:*
  `haproxy -c -f <cfg>`. *Artifact:* the rendered config, including every included map and certificate
  path the config references.
- *Predicate:* the running build actually has the features the config uses. *Command:* `haproxy -vv`.
  *Artifact:* the feature block, asserted against the directives the config uses — QUIC before `quic4@`,
  kTLS before `ktls on`.
- *Predicate:* every example config in this skill parses under its declared branch. *Command:* the
  bundled `check_examples.py`. *Artifact:* `examples/haproxy/*.cfg`.

#### 4.3 The boundary table `alaa-haproxy` should ship

Owns: how any routing, caching, TLS-termination, rate-limiting or load-balancing decision is expressed as
an HAProxy directive; the branch-and-version discipline for HAProxy itself; the Runtime API surface; the
`log-format` string; the drain and reload directive set. Does not own, with owner named: caching *policy*
(`alaa-frontend-devops`); log field names and the metric catalog (`alaa-services-contract`); requirement
level and alerting (`alaa-observability-soc`); timeouts, retries and degradation *doctrine*
(`alaa-reliability-sla`); threat classification and exposure severity (`alaa-security-review`); chart and
workload authorship (`alaa-k8s-helm`, `caas-arvan-kuber`); image authorship (`alaa-docker-production`);
runner expression (`alaa-gitlab-ci-cd`); Lua scripting (see §Out-of-batch findings).

### 6. Wording-test failures

Each of these could be followed exactly by a competent agent that then does the wrong thing.

**Preference verb where a constraint was meant.**

- `full-guide.md:16` — "Prefer master-worker mode in practice". An agent may prefer otherwise. Every
  Kubernetes example ships `-W` (`haproxy-deployment.yaml:42`), so the intent is a constraint.
- `full-guide.md:31-33` — "Keep normal keep-alive when backend reuse is helpful and server capacity is
  proven. Use `option http-server-close` when backend idle connections are the real bottleneck. Review
  `http-reuse`, queue behavior, and `strict-maxconn` together." Three sentences, no observable condition.
  "Helpful", "proven", "the real bottleneck" and "review together" are all unmeasurable.
- `full-guide.md:197` — "Replace repeated ACL trees with maps where possible."
- `30-security-observability.md:30` — "Keep scrape endpoints cluster-private when possible." This is a
  security control governed by a preference verb.
- `20-platform-delivery.md:43` — "Treat chart values as chart-specific. Translate the patterns, not just
  the key names." No agent can act on this.

**Rule with no stated scope.** `full-guide.md:24` — "Use `hard-stop-after` when graceful shutdown could
otherwise hang on long-lived sessions." Which proxies? A `hard-stop-after` in `global` is process-wide; the
sentence gives no scope, no value, and no way to know whether "could otherwise hang" is true. It is used in
none of the nineteen examples, so the rule has no instance to learn from.

**Abstract noun standing in for an observable condition.** `full-guide.md:45` — "Test peer recovery and
node loss before calling it production-ready." *Production-ready* is the abstract noun; the sentence
delegates the criterion-2 answer to a test the agent must invent, and the skill never states what a
passing result looks like. This is the single most consequential wording failure in the skill because it
sits exactly where the peers answer should be.

**Prohibition without its positive replacement.**

- `SKILL.md:40` — "do not assume a directive exists just because it exists in another HAProxy branch."
  Correct, and the replacement is missing: the agent is not told to check `haproxy -vv` plus the branch
  manual, nor which manual, nor what to do when the directive is absent.
- `full-guide.md:86` — "Verify QUIC support from `haproxy -vv` before using `quic4@...` listeners." Stops
  at the check. It never says what to do when the check fails — that QUIC requires quictls, AWS-LC or
  WolfSSL for full support, or `limited-quic` for a reduced mode on stock OpenSSL (verified §7). An agent
  that runs `haproxy -vv`, sees no QUIC and has no replacement will either drop HTTP/3 silently or ship a
  config that will not start.
- `30-security-observability.md:25` — "Avoid logging secrets, full Authorization headers, or certificate
  details that are not needed." No positive replacement: no statement of which certificate fields *are*
  needed for incident response, though `full-guide.md:63` gestures at "enough certificate context".

**Sentence that is actively backwards.** `20-platform-delivery.md:19` — "Add a `preStop` hook that begins a
graceful stop before the pod disappears from Service endpoints." Followed exactly, this produces connection
refusals. HAProxy's soft-stop closes listeners; if the pod is still in the Service's endpoint list, new
connections arrive at a closed listener. The correct ordering is: fail readiness, wait for endpoint
propagation, *then* soft-stop. The three shipped `preStop` hooks implement the sentence as written and are
therefore all wrong: `haproxy-deployment.yaml:68`, `values-example.yaml:59` and
`values-production-example.yaml:114` all run `kill -USR1 1 || true; sleep 15` — signal first, sleep after.
The sleep must come first. This is a rule and three artifacts that all need reversing.

### 7. Stale or unverifiable claims

Verified 2026-07-29 against official sources.

| # | Claim | Citation | Source checked | Holds? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | "`3.2` is still labeled `LTS` in the docs index" and the skill covers "the current HAProxy 3.2 LTS line" | `SKILL.md:3`, `:14`, `:22`, `full-guide.md:8` | https://docs.haproxy.org/ | **Half true, materially misleading.** 3.2 is still *an* LTS. It is no longer *the current* LTS. The docs index today lists **3.4 (LTS)**, 3.3 (no label), **3.2 (LTS)**, 3.0 (LTS), 2.8 (LTS), 2.6 (LTS), and **3.5 (DEV)**. Everything from 3.1 down to 1.6 is EOL. |
| 2 | The skill covers "both" relevant branches: 3.2 and 3.3 | `SKILL.md:12-16`, `00-topic-map.md:5-9` | https://docs.haproxy.org/, https://www.haproxy.org/ | **Stale.** **HAProxy 3.4 exists, was released 2026-06-03, and is the current LTS.** The skill does not mention 3.4 anywhere. A new deployment advised by this skill today lands on a branch one or two generations behind, and the skill's entire two-branch framing — including the `-3.3.cfg` naming convention and the whole upgrade section — needs a third column. |
| 3 | "`3.3`, which is newer but not marked `LTS`" — positioned as the forward-looking choice | `SKILL.md:15`, `:70`, `full-guide.md:89-93` | https://www.haproxy.org/, https://endoflife.date/haproxy | **Inverted.** 3.3 is now the *older* non-LTS branch and reaches **end of life 2027-Q1 (security support to 2027-01-01)**, roughly six months out. The skill's advice to "Use `3.3` when you explicitly want newer features" now steers an estate onto a branch that is both behind 3.4 and near EOL. |
| 4 | "`3.2.15` is present in the official `3.2` release directory dated `2026-03-19`" | `SKILL.md:23`, `full-guide.md:10` | https://www.haproxy.org/, https://endoflife.date/haproxy | **Stale.** Latest 3.2 is **3.2.21**, released **2026-07-03**. Six patch releases behind. |
| 5 | "`3.3.6` is present in the official `3.3` release directory dated `2026-03-19`" | `SKILL.md:24`, `full-guide.md:10` | as above | **Stale.** Latest 3.3 is **3.3.12**, released **2026-07-03**. |
| 6 | 3.3 adds backend HTTP/3 over QUIC; `14-http3-backend-3.3.cfg` demonstrates it with `server … quic4@…` under `expose-experimental-directives` | `SKILL.md:77`, `full-guide.md:91`, `14-http3-backend-3.3.cfg:2,28-29` | https://www.haproxy.com/blog/announcing-haproxy-3-3 | **Holds.** Shipped as described, still experimental, and `expose-experimental-directives` is required and is present at `14:2`. The example's server line matches the official blog's own example almost exactly. |
| 7 | 3.3 adds persistent stats across reloads; `15-persistent-stats-3.3.cfg` uses `shm-stats-file` plus `guid` | `SKILL.md:78`, `full-guide.md:116`, `15-persistent-stats-3.3.cfg:2-3,14,19-20` | https://www.haproxy.com/blog/announcing-haproxy-3-3 | **Holds.** `expose-experimental-directives` + `shm-stats-file` are both required, GUIDs must be unique across the config, and stats survive reload but not restart. `15` gets all of this right. One gap: the skill never states the restart caveat, and never states that the `shm-stats-file` path must be writable — which collides with `readOnlyRootFilesystem: true` at `haproxy-deployment.yaml:45`. |
| 8 | 3.3 adds automatic backend TLS SNI via `sni-auto` / `no-sni-auto` / `check-sni-auto` / `no-check-sni-auto` | `SKILL.md:78`, `full-guide.md:74-75`, `16-server-tls-sni-auto-3.3.cfg:23-24` | https://www.haproxy.com/blog/announcing-haproxy-3-3 | **Holds.** All four keyword names are correct and `full-guide.md:74` correctly notes the behaviour is automatic, so `sni-auto` is explicit rather than enabling. Not stated anywhere, and it should be: with SNI derived from the `Host` header, an attacker-supplied `Host` selects the name the origin certificate is checked against, so an unexpected `Host` turns into a backend TLS failure and a 502. `16` has no host allow-list. |
| 9 | 3.3 adds Linux kTLS; `17-ktls-3.3.cfg` uses `ktls on` on `bind` and `server`, plus `option splice-auto` | `SKILL.md:83`, `full-guide.md:79`, `17-ktls-3.3.cfg:2,16-17,23-24` | https://www.haproxy.com/blog/announcing-haproxy-3-3 | **Holds, with an unstated requirement.** `ktls on` is correct on both `bind` and `server`, `option splice-auto` is correct, and `expose-experimental-directives` is required and present at `17:2`. What the skill omits: kTLS additionally requires **OpenSSL 3.x or AWS-LC** and a build compiled with kTLS support. `full-guide.md:219` says only "for Linux estates where the build, TLS library, and kernel capabilities were confirmed first" — it names the categories without naming the values. |
| 10 | Current state of QUIC support and its build requirements | `full-guide.md:85-87`, `03-quic-http3.cfg` | https://www.haproxy.com/blog/state-of-ssl-stacks | **The skill's claim is too thin to act on.** Verified: full QUIC needs a BoringSSL-compatible QUIC API — **quictls, AWS-LC (recommended for frequent upgraders), or WolfSSL ≥ 5.6.6**. Stock **OpenSSL 3.x does not provide it**; HAProxy works around this with a keylog-callback path exposed as the **`limited-quic`** option, which gives basic server-mode QUIC with **no 0-RTT resumption** and no performance optimisation. LibreSSL is incomplete. The skill names none of this and never names `limited-quic`. `03-quic-http3.cfg` binds `quic4@:443` with no note about the build or the TLS stack. |
| 11 | Prometheus exporter and the Runtime API surface used by `10-prometheus-runtime-api.cfg` | `10-prometheus-runtime-api.cfg:4,28`, `full-guide.md:100-104,115` | https://www.haproxy.com/documentation/haproxy-configuration-tutorials/alerts-and-monitoring/prometheus/ | **Holds.** `http-request use-service prometheus-exporter if { path /metrics }` is still the documented syntax through 3.3, no deprecation, and the exporter is enabled by default in standard builds. `show info`/`show stat`/`show errors`/`show events` are all current. Two gaps rather than errors: `extra-counters` (added 3.0) is not mentioned, and **`show peers` is never mentioned**, which is the one Runtime API command that would diagnose the failure in §Shared closing. |
| 12 | `haproxy:3.2.15` as the image to run | `haproxy-validate.yml:25`, `haproxy-deployment.yaml:41`, `values-example.yaml:6`, `values-production-example.yaml:6` | https://hub.docker.com/_/haproxy/ | **Stale.** Official image tags now include `3.4.1`, `3.4`, `latest` and **`lts`** (which now resolves to 3.4, not 3.2). `3.2.15` is six patches behind 3.2.21. Also no digest pinning and no `imagePullPolicy` on the Deployment. |
| 13 | `kubectl` at 1.32 | `haproxy-validate.yml:21` (`version: v1.32.0`), `gitlab-ci-snippet.yml:30,38` (`bitnami/kubectl:1.32`) | https://endoflife.date/kubernetes | **Stale and out of support.** Kubernetes **1.32 reached end of life 2026-02-28**. Supported today: 1.36 (latest 1.36.3, 2026-07-22), 1.35, 1.34. Secondary concern for Phase 2 to route to `alaa-gitlab-ci-cd`: the `bitnami/*` public catalog was restricted during 2025 and `bitnami/kubectl:1.32` should not be assumed pullable. |
| 14 | 3.3 deprecations and breaking changes listed at `full-guide.md:144-155` | `full-guide.md:146-155` | https://www.haproxy.com/blog/announcing-haproxy-3-3 | **All confirmed.** `master-worker` deprecated in favour of `-W`/`-Ws`; `tune.quic.frontend.*` → `tune.quic.fe.*`; `no-quic` → `tune.quic.listen`; `dispatch` and `option transparent` deprecated; `program` section removed; duplicate proxy/defaults/log-forward names now errors; default balance algorithm becomes `random`; `mode http` backends default to `option abortonclose`. This is the strongest section in the skill. Two additions it misses: the **minimum Linux kernel rises to 4.17**, and **`http-send-name-header` may no longer override `connection`, `content-length`, `host` or `transfer-encoding`** — which touches `09-connection-reuse.cfg:28` and `18-tiered-edge-gateway.cfg:27`. |
| 15 | 3.2 feature claims used by the examples | `04:26`, `08:17`, `09:19`, `01:43`, `01:59` | https://www.haproxy.com/blog/announcing-haproxy-3-2 | **Mostly hold, one does not.** Confirmed introduced in 3.2 and named correctly: `hash-preserve-affinity`, `strict-maxconn` (server keyword), `ssl-f-use`, the `pause` action for `http-request`/`http-response`, and the `log-forward` options `dont-parse-log` and `assume-rfc6587-ntf`. `crt-store` is 3.0, not 3.2 — `full-guide.md:51` correctly avoids claiming otherwise. **`idle-ping` is confirmed as a `bind` argument and a `server` argument only** — "you can now enable liveness checks on idle frontend connections via the `bind` directive's `idle-ping` argument… You can also set `idle-ping` on `server` directives" — which makes `09-connection-reuse.cfg:19` a parse error. |

**Verification note.** Two directive-level questions could not be settled from a primary source in this
session, because `docs.haproxy.org/*/configuration.txt` is blocked by this environment's egress policy for
direct fetch and the WebFetch renderer truncates the manual before section 4.2. Both are flagged for Phase
2 to confirm with a real `haproxy -c`: (a) that `balance hash` requires a mandatory sample expression, so
that `08-consistent-hash-affinity.cfg:14` is a parse error and not merely unusual; (b) the exact argument
form of `http-request pause`. Neither uncertainty affects the findings that depend on them, since both
files need editing regardless.

### 8. Router audit

**Convention.** Five references today. The settled rule is that a skill with 8 or fewer references lists
them in `SKILL.md` with a trigger condition each, and one with 9 or more uses `references/00-topic-map.md`.
At five, this skill is on the wrong side of that line *and* has both structures.

**Current state.** `SKILL.md:101-112` lists all five references with a label each — "Fast router and
example map", "Full preserved guidance, production checklists, branch comparison, and upgrade notes",
"Platform delivery patterns for containers, Kubernetes, Helm, and CI", "Security and observability
checklist", "Official links and live-source checkpoints". These are **descriptions, not trigger
conditions**: none says *open this when …*. An agent cannot tell from "Full preserved guidance" that
`full-guide.md` is where the 3.3 upgrade breaking-changes list lives. Meanwhile `00-topic-map.md` is a
router into sections of `full-guide.md`, which is a different job — intra-file navigation of a 220-line
document — and it is the only place all nineteen examples are indexed with a charter.

**Recommendation.** Phase 2 should split `full-guide.md` into properly scoped references, which takes the
count to eleven plus `SOURCES.md`. At that size `00-topic-map.md` becomes the correct and required router
and stays. The fix for defect 12 is therefore to **delete the duplicate example list from `SKILL.md:114-143`
and rewrite `SKILL.md:101-112` as a single pointer to the topic map**, not to delete the topic map.
`SOURCES.md` is a provenance ledger, is legitimate, and must be preserved.

**`agents/openai.yaml`.** 273 B, four interface keys plus `allow_implicit_invocation: true`. Correctly uses
the bare `$` form (`:4`), Codex-only, no model key, no effort key. Clean — the only file in the skill with
no defect.

---

### 9. Scripts and assets audit

**The skill ships no scripts.** No `.py`, no `.sh`, no `.js`, no `scripts/` directory, no `__pycache__`,
and no `.gitignore` in the skill directory. Every per-script question the brief asks — Windows
compatibility, `--help`, self-test, exit codes distinguishing "could not run" from "clean" — is vacuous
here, and that vacuum is itself the finding: **every rule in this skill is a preference, because nothing
reports a violation of any of them.**

**Assets that behave like executable content.** The thirty example files are the skill's real payload and
are copied verbatim by agents, so they deserve the same scrutiny.

*Configs that would not load, or load and do the wrong thing:*

- `09-connection-reuse.cfg:19` — `idle-ping 30s` as a proxy-level directive. `idle-ping` is a `bind` and a
  `server` keyword (verified §7). Parse error; the file cannot start. It should read
  `bind :443 ssl alpn h2,http/1.1 idle-ping 30s`.
- `08-consistent-hash-affinity.cfg:14` — `balance hash` with no sample expression, where the documented
  form takes a mandatory expression. Flagged for confirmation in §7; either way the file demonstrates
  consistent hashing without saying what it hashes, which defeats its own purpose.
- `haproxy-configmap.yaml:30` — `bind 127.0.0.1:8405` for the metrics frontend, while
  `haproxy-service.yaml:25-31` publishes a ClusterIP Service on 8405 and `haproxy-servicemonitor.yaml:11`
  scrapes it. A loopback bind is unreachable through the pod IP. **The shipped Kubernetes bundle can never
  be scraped**, and the ServiceMonitor will report the target down from the first rollout. `10-prometheus-runtime-api.cfg:26`
  makes the same bind correctly, because there the scrape is local.
- `haproxy-networkpolicy.yaml:13-19` — ingress `from: - namespaceSelector: {}` is the maximally permissive
  selector: every pod in every namespace may reach 8080 *and* the metrics port 8405. This is presented as
  the answer to `20-platform-delivery.md:33` ("Use a NetworkPolicy when the namespace is shared") and
  `30-security-observability.md:30` ("Keep scrape endpoints cluster-private"). It satisfies neither. Egress
  (`:20-29`) permits DNS and TCP 8080 only, so it would also block the peers port 1024 used by
  `12-peers-global-rate-limit.cfg` and the 8443/443 backends used by `18` and `19` — the bundle is not
  composable with the configs in the same skill.
- `haproxy-deployment.yaml:51-60` — readiness and liveness are both `tcpSocket` on port 8080, the traffic
  port. A TCP probe on HAProxy's own listener succeeds whenever the process is listening, regardless of
  backend health, and **cannot fail before termination** because the listener stays bound until shutdown.
  This directly contradicts `20-platform-delivery.md:22` ("Readiness should represent whether the pod can
  still accept new traffic"), `full-guide.md:174` and `30-security-observability.md:43`. The official
  HAProxy capability that would satisfy the rule is `monitor-uri` with `monitor fail if <cond>`, which the
  skill never names — a breach of the standing preference for the official capability.
- `haproxy-deployment.yaml:68`, `values-example.yaml:59`, `values-production-example.yaml:114` — the
  `preStop` ordering is reversed, per §6. `kill -USR1 1 || true; sleep 15` must become
  `sleep 15; kill -USR1 1 || true`, and the sleep must be shorter than
  `terminationGracePeriodSeconds: 30` (`haproxy-deployment.yaml:29`) with room for the soft-stop itself.
- `haproxy-deployment.yaml:45` — `readOnlyRootFilesystem: true` with `/run/haproxy` and `/var/lib/haproxy`
  mounted writable is correct as far as it goes, but it will break the 3.3 persistent-stats example, whose
  `shm-stats-file /dev/shm/haproxy-stats` (`15:3`) needs a writable `/dev/shm` that is not mounted here.
- `01-baseline-http-tls.cfg:6` — `chroot /var/lib/haproxy` combined with
  `stats socket /run/haproxy/admin.sock` (`:7`). The socket is created before the chroot so this works, but
  the interaction is exactly the kind of thing an agent gets wrong when adapting, and no comment explains it.

*Version drift in assets:* `haproxy:3.2.15` in four files; `kubectl` 1.32 in two; `alpine/helm:3.17.3` and
`bitnami/kubectl:1.32` in the GitLab snippet. See §7 rows 12 and 13.

*Provider YAML that belongs elsewhere:* the two CI files, per §4.2.

**Checks the skill should ship and does not.** Six, all runnable on Windows, all with `--help`, a
`--self-test` using fixtures under `tempfile.mkdtemp()` rather than a directory inside the repository, and
exit codes **0 clean / 1 violation found / 2 could not run** so a missing binary or a blocked network never
reads as a pass:

1. `scripts/check_examples.py` — run `haproxy -c -f` over every `examples/haproxy/*.cfg` against a binary
   whose branch matches the file's declared branch (`*-3.3.cfg` → 3.3, otherwise the current LTS). Asserts
   each file parses. This is the check that would have caught `09:19` and `08:14`.
2. `scripts/check_defaults_scope.py` — parse each config; assert every `frontend`/`backend`/`listen` either
   follows an unnamed `defaults` with no intervening second `defaults`, or carries an explicit
   `from <name>`. Flag any file containing two or more `defaults` sections and no `from`. This encodes the
   trust-boundary rule in §Shared closing as an executable check.
3. `scripts/check_peers_locality.py` — for any config with a `peers` section, assert a `localpeer` keyword
   is present or an accompanying note documents the `-L` requirement; assert the peers `bind` carries
   `ssl`; assert the file states the node count its thresholds assume.
4. `scripts/check_rate_limit_key.py` — for any `track-sc*` keyed on `src`, assert the same file states
   whether its listener carries `accept-proxy` and carries the trust note for that choice.
5. `scripts/check_versions.py` — fetch the branch table from `docs.haproxy.org` and the release
   directories, compare against the pinned facts in the version reference and against every image tag in
   `examples/`; exit 2 on network failure, 1 on drift. Given §7, this check would fire today on six
   distinct facts.
6. `scripts/check_metrics_reachable.py` — assert no example binds a metrics frontend on a loopback address
   while a Service or ServiceMonitor in the same example bundle targets that port. Catches the
   configmap/service/servicemonitor contradiction above.

### 10. Rewrite brief for Phase 2

**Target body size.** `SKILL.md` to **≤ 4,200 B net of frontmatter**, down from ~6,285 B. Removals:
the live-version block (`:17-25`), the 3.3 additions list (`:73-84`), the branch-strategy section
(`:68-71`), the example bundle list (`:114-143`), the source-priority list (`:43-57`, which belongs with
`SOURCES.md`). Additions: the D8 sentence from §4.1, a "what this skill does not own" pointer, and `/name`
alongside `$name` at every call site.

**Frontmatter description.** Rewrite to add a "do not use for" clause and drop the branch claim, which
decays. Keep under 900 characters and use no angle brackets. Shape: what it does; when to use it; then —
do not use for generic reverse-proxy advice where HAProxy-specific behaviour is not the decision surface;
do not use for HAProxy Lua scripting; do not use to decide a caching policy, only to express one; do not
use for Kubernetes workload or chart authorship. Naming the branch in the description is what made it stale
this cycle — name the discipline instead.

**Reference file list, with a one-line charter each.** Twelve files; at that count `00-topic-map.md` is the
required router.

| File | Charter |
|---|---|
| `references/00-topic-map.md` | The router. Open first; maps a task to exactly one reference below, and indexes all nineteen configs with a one-line charter each. |

| `references/20-core-config-and-timeouts.md` | How a config is structured so a reviewer can reason about it: **the `defaults` association rule**, timeouts, `retries` with `option redispatch`, `maxconn`, `http-reuse`, `strict-maxconn`, maps over ACL trees. |

### Out-of-batch findings

**`alaa-haproxy-lua` is unreachable from `alaa-haproxy`.** Established from `alaa-haproxy`'s own text
alone, as instructed; the directory was not read and no edit to it is proposed. Note for the record that
`alaa-haproxy-lua` is **not present in the staged tree** at
`/mnt/user-data/uploads/skills/skills/sohrab/`, which contains only the eight batch members plus
`alaa-frontend-devops`, so nothing about its contents is asserted here.

The finding: **`alaa-haproxy` never names it.** A grep for `haproxy-lua` across all 37 files returns
nothing. Lua is mentioned exactly twice in the whole skill, and neither mention is a call site:

- `references/full-guide.md:11` — "Use `haproxy -vv` to confirm the actual binary features before enabling
  QUIC, advanced TLS policy, Lua, tracing, or experimental directives." Lua appears here as the third item
  in a list of build features to check for. It routes nowhere.
- `SKILL.md:99` — "`$alaa-crockford-base32-codecs` … Pair when HAProxy Lua work needs shared Crockford
  Base32, UUIDv7, or pure codec helpers that must match backend, frontend, or CLI code."

The second is the damaging one. It is the only sentence in the skill that contemplates an agent *writing*
HAProxy Lua, and it routes that agent to a **codec** skill — correct for the narrow question of which
Base32 implementation to use, and actively misleading about ownership, because it is the sole companion
named in a Lua context. An agent reading `alaa-haproxy` end to end would conclude that HAProxy Lua is
`alaa-haproxy`'s own subject, with a codec helper available. It would never discover that Lua scripting has
its own owner. Since `SKILL.md:12` declares this skill "the HAProxy source of truth for this pack", the
silence is read as authority.

Per the brief, silence is not delegation; silence is `FAIL`. This is not proposed as an edit to
`alaa-haproxy-lua` — it is a missing row in `alaa-haproxy`'s own boundary table, which is in batch. Phase 2
should add `Lua scripting in HAProxy | /alaa-haproxy-lua ($alaa-haproxy-lua)` to
`references/90-companion-boundary.md` and qualify `SKILL.md:99` so the codec skill is named as a helper
*within* Lua work rather than as its owner. The reciprocal half — whether `alaa-haproxy-lua` routes back —
is out of batch and is not assessed. Recommend the carry-over plan assign `alaa-haproxy-lua` to a batch,
since it is currently in none and this pairing has now produced a live routing defect.

### Shared closing

#### A. The peers question: would `12` share the table it claims to share?

**No, and it would fail silently.** Three independent preconditions are unmet or unstated.

*The local peer must be identifiable.* HAProxy resolves the local peer by matching a peer name in the
section against the local peer name — the machine's hostname by default, overridable by the `localpeer`
keyword or the `-L` command-line option. `12-peers-global-rate-limit.cfg:14-16` declares `peer edge-a` and
`peer edge-b` and sets no `localpeer`; `localpeer` and `-L` appear nowhere in the skill. In the delivery
model this skill's own examples assume — a Deployment with pods named
`haproxy-<replicaset-hash>-<random>` (`haproxy-deployment.yaml:1-14`) — the hostname will never equal
`edge-a` or `edge-b`. When no peer matches, the section is not activated: HAProxy neither dials out nor
listens. **The config still passes `haproxy -c -f`, HAProxy still starts, the stick table is still created,
and the rate limit still works — locally, on each node, in isolation.** That is the worst available failure
shape. It is invisible to the exact validation the skill mandates at `SKILL.md:63`, it produces no error in
the logs, and the metric it guards looks entirely normal.

*The table identity must match on both nodes.* Peers replicate a table under the name of the proxy that
declares it. Both nodes must define `fe_api` with the same `type` and `size`. A node upgraded with
`size 1m` while its partner stays at `500k` has the table refused for sync while every other part of the
config runs normally. `12:20` states neither requirement.

*The peers transport must be trustworthy.* `peer edge-a 10.0.70.11:1024` makes the node bind :1024 for the
peers protocol with **no TLS and no authentication**. Anything that reaches that port can write stick-table
entries. Since `gpc0` drives `http-request deny deny_status 429` (`12:27,29`), an attacker with reach can
push `gpc0 ≥ 5` for chosen source addresses and deny service to specific clients, or push a counter down to
evade the limit entirely. The skill's nine security rules (`30-security-observability.md:5-13`) do not
mention it, and the shipped NetworkPolicy (`haproxy-networkpolicy.yaml:20-29`) permits no egress on 1024 at
all — so composing the skill's own artifacts yields a policy that blocks the sync while leaving it
unauthenticated anywhere the policy is absent.

#### B. What happens to the limit when a peer is unreachable — the criterion-2 answer

This is the question the skill most needs to answer, and `full-guide.md:45` answers it with "Test peer
recovery and node loss before calling it production-ready." The answer has three parts and all three are
absent.

**1. Peers replication does not sum counters — so the limit is not global even when every peer is healthy.**
HAProxy pushes updated entries to connected peers and a receiving peer applies the received values to its
local entry. There is no aggregation step. This is confirmed from the vendor's own product boundary:
HAProxy Enterprise ships a **Global Profiling Engine** whose entire purpose is the summation that peers
does not perform — "if LoadBalancer1 receives two requests and LoadBalancer2 receives three requests, the
Global Profiling Engine will sum those numbers to get a total of five, then push that to both LoadBalancer1
and LoadBalancer2". If plain peers summed counters, that module would not need to exist. So
