`sc_http_req_rate(0) gt 150` at `12:23` is **not** a 150 req/s cluster-wide limit; it is an approximation
that converges somewhere between the busiest single node's local rate and the true total, and that loses
increments whenever two nodes update the same key concurrently. The filename
`12-peers-global-rate-limit.cfg`, the topic map's "shared abuse state with peers" (`00-topic-map.md:75`)
and `full-guide.md:214`'s "the first file to copy when local stick tables are no longer enough" all promise
a global limit the mechanism does not deliver.

**2. When a peer is unreachable the limit fails open, and it fails open by exactly the node count.** Each
node continues counting only the traffic it sees. A client spreading requests across two edge nodes is
measured at roughly half its true rate on each, so the admitted rate rises to as much as 2 × 150 =
300 req/s while every node believes it is enforcing 150. On a three-node cluster it is 450. **Nothing in the
config, the logs or the metrics reports that the limit has multiplied** — and `show peers`, the one Runtime
API command that would show the session down, is never mentioned in the skill.

This is a security-relevant availability failure, so it is worth naming which doctrine owner it belongs to
under the brief's discriminating question — *when this dependency cannot answer, does proceeding without it
let something through that must not get through?* For a rate limiter protecting a backend from overload the
answer is availability, and the degradation doctrine is `alaa-reliability-sla`'s. For a rate limiter that is
the only control in front of a credential endpoint the answer is yes, something gets through, and the
fail-closed doctrine is `alaa-security-review`'s. `alaa-haproxy` should state the mechanical fact — a peer
outage multiplies the admitted rate by the node count — and route the *choice* of what to do about it to
those two owners rather than deciding it.

**3. On reconnect there is no catch-up.** Peers exchange current entry state, not a journal, so requests
admitted during the partition are simply gone from the counters. Worse in the other direction:
`expire 30m` at `12:20` keeps entries alive far beyond the 10-second measurement window, so a stale `gpc0`
replicated from a recovering peer can re-block a client that has been behaving for twenty minutes.

**The honest guidance the skill owes:** a stick-table limit with peers is an **abuse damper, not a quota**.
If the requirement is a hard global quota, open-source HAProxy peers cannot provide it, and the skill must
say so and name where the requirement goes instead. If it is a damper, set the per-node threshold to
`global_budget / node_count`, state the node count the config assumes as a comment in the file, and alert on
peer-session loss via `show peers` — because the moment a peer drops, the limit silently becomes
`node_count` times looser than the number written in the config.

#### C. How `04`, `12` and `18` contradict one another

Each file loads. Together they are incoherent, and the skill's own instruction is to compose them —
"Start from the closest bundled example" (`SKILL.md:64`) and the six "first file to copy" pointers at
`full-guide.md:212-219`.

`04:19` and `12:21` both key the limiter on `src` via `http-request track-sc0 src`. `11:17` and `18:17` both
establish that in this estate HAProxy sits behind an upstream load balancer, binding `:443` with
`accept-proxy`. Neither rate-limiting file has `accept-proxy`; the tiered-edge file has `accept-proxy` and
no rate limiting. There is no way to compose them that is correct, and neither failure mode is stated:

- **Deploy `04` or `12` in `18`'s topology without `accept-proxy`** and `src` is the upstream load
  balancer's address, not the client's. Every client behind that LB collapses into a handful of table
  entries, and the first LB address to exceed 150 req/s trips `deny deny_status 429` for **everyone behind
  it**. The rate limiter becomes a self-inflicted outage, and it fires precisely under load — the one
  moment it must not.
- **Deploy them with `accept-proxy` on a listener untrusted clients can reach** — which is what `18:17`
  ships, on a listener its own name calls the edge, with nothing restricting who may connect — and the
  PROXY header is an unauthenticated assertion HAProxy trusts unconditionally. A client forges any source
  address it likes, gets a fresh stick-table entry per forged address, and bypasses the limit at will,
  while also being able to drive a chosen victim's `gpc0` to the deny threshold.

The rule the skill should state, once, in `40-rate-limiting-and-peers.md`: **the rate-limit key must be the
identity the trust boundary establishes, not `src` by default.** On a directly exposed listener, `src` is
correct and `accept-proxy` must be absent. Behind a PROXY-protocol LB on a listener reachable *only* from
that LB, `src` is correct because `accept-proxy` has already rewritten it. Behind an LB that sets
`X-Forwarded-For`, the key is the right-most untrusted hop, never `req.hdr(x-forwarded-for)` unqualified.
And every listener carrying `accept-proxy` must be unreachable except from its specific upstream — an
obligation that lands in a NetworkPolicy or security group, which `haproxy-networkpolicy.yaml` does not
express today.

One further composition hazard in both files: `http-request pause` (`04:26`, `12:28`) is the *first*
response to a flood. Pausing holds the stream and its resources rather than releasing them, so against a
connection-exhaustion attack `pause` amplifies the pressure it was added to relieve — and `04` sets no
`maxconn` anywhere, in `global` or on the frontend, so there is no ceiling on how much it can hold.

#### D. The trust-boundary question: does the skill state the `defaults` association rule?

**No. The rule appears nowhere in `alaa-haproxy`.**

The rule, confirmed against HAProxy's official configuration documentation: a `defaults` section "will apply
to any `frontend` or `backend` that follows it, up until the next `defaults` section"; multiple `defaults`
sections do not inherit from one another — "a `defaults` section doesn't inherit settings from other
`defaults` sections. Each `defaults` section conveys only the settings it directly specifies"; a named
`defaults` is selected explicitly with `from`, and without `from` a proxy inherits "the nearest preceding
`defaults` section". A `defaults` section **never applies file-wide**. The documentation additionally warns
that relying on ordering "is *not* recommended because it can lead to ambiguity and slow troubleshooting" —
which is an argument for naming every `defaults` and using `from` everywhere.

A grep for `defaults` across the skill's prose returns exactly one line, `full-guide.md:152`: "Duplicate
names across `frontend`, `backend`, `listen`, `defaults`, `log-forward`, and duplicate `server` names are
now startup errors in `3.3`." That is a statement about *naming collisions*, not about *association*. It is
the only place the word appears outside the example configs, and a reader could take from it that `defaults`
sections have names without ever learning what a name is for.

**Do the examples mislead?** Yes, by uniform omission. Eighteen of the nineteen configs open with exactly
one unnamed `defaults` placed above every proxy; the nineteenth (`05-syslog-log-forward.cfg`) has none.
**Not one uses a named `defaults`. Not one uses `from`.** Each file in isolation is correct. The pattern
they teach is that `defaults` is a file header — a global block that governs the file — and that reading is
wrong in exactly the way that costs a production incident.

The failure is triggered by the skill's own workflow. `SKILL.md:64` says "Start from the closest bundled
example" and `full-guide.md:212-219` names six files as starting points for six scenarios; a real task
usually needs two of them. Concatenate `01-baseline-http-tls.cfg` (`mode http`, `timeout client 30s`,
`option forwardfor`, `retries 3`) with `02-tcp-l4.cfg` (`mode tcp`, `timeout client 1m`, no
`timeout http-request`) and the result parses, starts, and serves traffic — while every proxy after the
second `defaults` at `02:7` silently runs in TCP mode with different timeouts and **no** `option
forwardfor`. Nothing warns. The same shape applies to a `defaults`-level `http-request del-header` or a
`timeout http-request`: an agent adds the directive to the `defaults` it can see at the top of the file and
believes it now applies to the proxy it is protecting, when a second `defaults` further down has already
taken over. That is precisely the failure the corrected `trust_boundary_check.py` was fixed to detect — a
delete-header or a timeout silently not applying to the proxy you thought it did — and a skill that
declares itself "the HAProxy source of truth for this pack" (`SKILL.md:12`) while omitting the rule leaves
that checker as the only thing standing between an agent and the mistake.

**What Phase 2 must add**, in `references/20-core-config-and-timeouts.md`, stated once: the association rule
in the terms above; the instruction that every config with more than one `defaults` section names each one
and every proxy selects its own with `from`; the reminder that in 3.3 a duplicate `defaults` *name* is a
startup error, so naming is cheap and collisions are caught; and a worked two-`defaults` example showing a
directive applying to one proxy and not the other. Then encode it as `scripts/check_defaults_scope.py`
(§9 item 2), because until a tool reports a violation this is a preference, not a rule.

#### E. Is a new skill genuinely required?

**No.** Every gap found in this lane is closable inside `alaa-haproxy`'s existing boundary, and closing them
there is the correct answer rather than a concession.

The two largest gaps both belong to `alaa-haproxy` by the ruling already in force. The caching, compression
and rewrite content the skill entirely lacks is content decision D8 explicitly assigns to it — expressing a
cache or routing decision as a directive is its half by name, and the frontend skill has already written the
counterpart from its side, twice. Adding
`references/50-caching-routing-and-rewrites.md` is this skill honouring a boundary it has been given, not
reaching across one. The rate-limiting and peers failure analysis is squarely HAProxy mechanism — how
`peers` replicates, what `show peers` reports, what a stick table does at saturation — and no other skill
could state it without becoming an HAProxy skill.

The one place a new skill might have looked warranted is Lua, and it is not warranted, because the owner
already exists: `alaa-haproxy-lua` is on disk as its own directory. That gap is a missing *route*, not a
missing owner, and the fix is one row in `alaa-haproxy`'s boundary table. The correct action there is to
have the carry-over plan assign `alaa-haproxy-lua` to a batch — it is currently in none — not to create
anything new.

Two gaps are best answered by routing to owners that already exist rather than by new prose anywhere:
log field names and the metric catalog go to `alaa-services-contract`, and the choice of what a rate
limiter should do when its peers are gone goes to `alaa-reliability-sla` and `alaa-security-review` under
the discriminating question in §B. `alaa-haproxy` states the mechanism; those owners state the policy. That
is the boundary working as designed, and it needs no new skill to hold it.
## Appendix F — `alaa-makefile`

### 1. What this skill is today

`alaa-makefile` is a 7,359-byte / 177-line always-loaded body (`SKILL.md`) routing to 13 files in
`references/` (11 topical guides + `00-topic-map.md` + `SOURCES.md`) and 3 bash scripts. Total pack
size 231,395 bytes — a 1:30 body-to-reference ratio, the largest in the batch.

The body is well organised on its face: a "Start fast" decision block (`SKILL.md:12-32`), a
"When NOT to use" section (`SKILL.md:34-38`), four task lanes (`SKILL.md:49-111`), authoring rules
(`SKILL.md:114-126`), validation rules (`SKILL.md:128-133`), companion routing (`SKILL.md:135-141`)
and a full reference index (`SKILL.md:143-162`). The frontmatter description (`SKILL.md:3`) is 538
characters, contains no angle brackets, and carries an explicit "Do not use it for…" clause — it
already meets the section 7 bar and needs no change.

Underneath that, three things are true and none of them are visible from the body.

**First, the pack is an unreconciled merge of two retired skills.** `SKILL.md:10` says so plainly:
"This skill replaces the old split `makefile-generator` and `makefile-validator` workflow." Both
predecessors' `SKILL.md` files were moved into `references/` verbatim and never rewritten.
`references/authoring-full-guide.md:1` still opens `# Makefile Generator`; its line 539 says
"Detailed guides in `docs/`", a directory that does not exist. `references/validation-full-guide.md:1`
opens `# Makefile Validator`; its lines 469-480 draw a directory tree containing
`skill.md # This file`, a `docs/` directory and an `examples/` directory, none of which exist, and
its lines 313, 332, 385, 410 and 418 give a hard-coded install path `.codex/skills/alaa-makefile/scripts/`
that does not match this layout. `authoring-full-guide.md:115-125` instructs the agent to call
`mcp__context7__resolve-library-id` and `mcp__context7__get-library-docs`; line 24 instructs it to
use `AskUserQuestion`. These are the retired skills' tool contracts, still being issued as orders.

**Second, the subject matter is not this fleet's subject matter.** Measured over the eight authoring
and review references, 280 fenced code examples divide as 135 C/C++ compilation (48%), 16
task-runner / container / CI (6%), 129 neither (46%). In the four references the "Generate or
redesign" lane loads first (`SKILL.md:55-58`), C/C++ is 68-88% of examples and task-runner content is
0-12%. Section 2 below and section 3 item **F-2** develop what this costs.

**Third, the fleet has no Makefile at all today.** `find` across both staged kits returns no
`Makefile`, `makefile`, `*.mk`, `justfile` or `Taskfile`. `service-ci-kit` expresses every gate as a
runner `script:` line invoking a shared shell script — `service-ci-kit/gitlab/platform-app-php-service.yml:160`
is `- bash .service-ci-kit/ci/scripts/build_image.sh`, and `.../:219` is
`- bash .service-ci-kit/ci/scripts/release.sh`, against the eleven scripts in
`service-ci-kit/ci/scripts/`. So the skill is not being asked to document an existing Makefile
estate; it is being asked to define what the fleet's first Makefile should be. It currently has no
opinion on that question.

### 2. Ten-criteria verdict

| # | Criterion | Verdict | Evidence |
|---|-----------|---------|----------|
| 1 | Correctness and testability | **FAIL** | The skill ships 19 executable checks in `scripts/validate_makefile.sh:249-527`; 6 are dead code and 1 emits a hard error on legal syntax (section 9). No script has a self-test. `SKILL.md:130` mandates running the validator, so a broken validator is a correctness failure of the skill itself, not merely of a helper. The generator's own Go template fails the skill's own validator with 2 errors (section 9). |
| 2 | Failure behaviour | **FAIL** | The skill's central safety claim — that a Makefile must fail loudly — is stated only as preference. `SKILL.md:132` says "Treat recipe indentation, shell failure behavior … as high-priority issues", which names no condition and no verdict. The one hard rule about silent success, `.ONESHELL` without `-e`, is stated at `references/validation-full-guide.md:78` and its check at `scripts/validate_makefile.sh:451-466` never fires (section 9). `references/best-practices.md:348-351` actively teaches `-rm -rf $(BUILD_DIR)` as "Method 1" of error handling — the leading `-` is the silent-success defect, presented as the fix. Nothing anywhere states what a Makefile target must do when the command it wraps exits non-zero. |
| 3 | Security | **SATISFIED** | `references/security-guide.md` is the strongest file in the pack: secrets from environment not literals (`:10-32`), injection prevention with an allow-list (`:70-92`), path-traversal validation (`:150-164`), `mktemp` + `trap` for temp files (`:166-175`), checksum/GPG verification of downloads (`:248-263`), TLS pinning via `--proto '=https' --tlsv1.2` (`:265-273`). The hardcoded-credential check at `scripts/validate_makefile.sh:317` is one of the checks that genuinely works, and fired as an ERROR in testing. |
| 4 | Observability | **FAIL** | The domain needs this and the skill is silent. A Makefile that fronts CI gates is where a developer first sees a failure, yet nothing states what a target must print on failure, that the failing command and its exit code must be identifiable, or that a target's output must be diffable against the runner's. `references/security-guide.md:233-243` has an `audit-log` macro writing to `/var/log/makefile-audit.log`, which is a hardcoded host path, not an observability contract. No route to `alaa-observability-soc` or `alaa-services-contract` anywhere in the pack. |
| 5 | Concurrency and load | **SATISFIED** | `references/optimization-guide.md:8-234` covers parallel safety properly: shared-resource corruption under `-j` (`:25-50`), `.NOTPARALLEL` global and per-target (`:52-64`, `:111-140`), `.WAIT` with a correct 4.4 version floor (`:66-109`), and a comparison table (`:185-211`). `references/common-mistakes.md:833-870` covers the `npm install` race concretely. This is the criterion the pack handles best after security. |
| 6 | Clean code, SOLID, patterns | **SATISFIED** | `SKILL.md:44` ("Prefer small, explicit targets and clear variables over clever macro-heavy Makefiles") plus `references/makefile-structure.md:290-334` (modular `include`), `:338-399` (non-recursive make preferred, with the Miller citation), and `references/best-practices.md:172-190` (namespaced `docker/build` targets) give an agent a defensible structural doctrine. |
| 7 | Algorithm and data-structure choice | **NOT-OWNED: alaa-bash-shell** | Call site `SKILL.md:137`: "Pair with `$alaa-bash-shell` when recipe complexity becomes shell-script complexity." Make has no algorithmic surface of its own; once a recipe carries logic it is a shell program. The route exists and names the owner, so this is legitimate delegation — but see section 6, the trigger condition is untestable as worded. |
| 8 | Configurability | **SATISFIED** | `references/variables-guide.md:62-87` (`?=` semantics and override precedence), `:215-259` (the full GNU directory variable set with `DESTDIR`), `:413-444` (environment interaction, `export`/`unexport`, `ifndef` and `$(error)` guards at the boundary). `SKILL.md:122` states the operative rule as a constraint: "Use `?=` for user-overridable values and `:=` for immediate project-owned values." |
| 9 | Speed of development and debuggability | **FAIL** | The routing promises small reads (`SKILL.md:42`, `references/00-topic-map.md:3`) but every rule is restated in 2-8 files (section 5), so an agent that opens the "smallest matching reference" gets a partial view of a rule whose other half is elsewhere, with no cross-links. The validator is slower than the thing it validates — it creates a Python venv and pip-installs `mbake` on every single invocation (`scripts/validate_makefile.sh:114-126`), measured at roughly 20-40 seconds per file with no caching and no offline path, and `SKILL.md:130` mandates it "for any non-trivial change". `references/authoring-full-guide.md:315-350` requires the agent to emit a 7-section prose validation report after every generation. This is guidance that will be bypassed. |
| 10 | Documentation | **FAIL** | The pack documents Make; it does not document what shipped, how it is operated, or how it fails. `SKILL.md:164-169` ("Deliverable rules") asks the agent to "State what you validated", which is the closest thing to an operability requirement and names no artifact. Two of the eleven references are stale copies of retired skills' own documentation describing directory layouts that do not exist (`references/authoring-full-guide.md:537-546`, `references/validation-full-guide.md:466-480`). |

**Tally: 4 SATISFIED, 5 FAIL, 1 NOT-OWNED.**

### 3. Defect classes actually found

**F-1 — Wrong trigger syntax at every call site (defect class 2). Blocking.**
All five companion routes use only the Codex `$name` form: `SKILL.md:137, 138, 139, 140, 141`. There
is not one `/name` form anywhere in `SKILL.md`. Under the settled convention this skill is invisible
from Claude Code at every routing point it has. `agents/openai.yaml:4` correctly uses the bare `$`
form and is right as it stands — it is Codex-only.

**F-2 — The reference set teaches a different craft than the fleet practises (defect class 4/9).**
48% of code examples across the authoring and review references are C/C++ compilation; 6% are
task-runner, container or CI. `references/variables-guide.md` spends lines 132-271 on `CC`, `CXX`,
`LD`, `AR`, `RANLIB`, `YACC`, `LEX`, `CFLAGS`, `CXXFLAGS`, `LDLIBS`, `YFLAGS`, `LFLAGS` and the full
GNU install-directory set including `MAN1DIR`, `MAN2DIR` and `INFODIR`. `references/patterns-guide.md`
gives 8 numbered project patterns of which 5 are C/C++/mixed-C (`:218-389`, `:475-523`); the Go
pattern (`:391-435`) and the Docker pattern (`:525-553`) are one each, and the Docker one is 27
lines with no error handling, no digest pinning and a bare `docker push`.
`references/makefile-structure.md:226-258` and `references/optimization-guide.md:731-770` cover
header-dependency generation, precompiled headers, LTO and unity builds. None of this is wrong; none
of it is what a Makefile fronting `docker compose` and `bash .service-ci-kit/ci/scripts/*.sh` needs.
This is the single largest content problem in the pack and the reason its size is not earning
anything.

**F-3 — Two routers (defect class 12).**
`references/00-topic-map.md` is the router (13 files ≥ 9, so the convention selects a topic map).
`SKILL.md:143-162` then lists all 13 reference files again under "Reference navigation", grouped by
the same four lanes the topic map uses. The lane sections at `SKILL.md:53-58`, `:71-76` and `:88-91`
list them a *third* time. `references/SOURCES.md` is a provenance ledger and is legitimate — do not
touch it.

**F-4 — Duplication between body and references (defect class 3).**
`SKILL.md:115-121` states the modern preamble. `references/authoring-full-guide.md:152-160`,
`references/best-practices.md:24-32` and `references/security-guide.md:318-328` each state it again.
`SKILL.md:122` states the `?=`/`:=` rule; `references/variables-guide.md:62-87`,
`references/best-practices.md:257-308`, `references/makefile-structure.md:56-129` and
`references/common-mistakes.md:377-413` state it again. Full measurement in section 5.

**F-5 — Fragile / broken bundled tooling (defect class 7/13). Blocking.**
Six of nineteen checks in `scripts/validate_makefile.sh` are dead code, one emits a hard error on
legal syntax, and `scripts/add_standard_targets.sh` fails on 100% of invocations. Detail in
section 9. Under defect class 13 the correct count of what this skill ships as working executable
checks is **12 of 19 claimed**, and the six dead ones include every check that inspects a recipe
line — that is, every check about what a target actually *does*.

**F-6 — A rule with no tool that reports a violation (defect class 13).**
`SKILL.md:124` ("Use order-only prerequisites for directories…"), `SKILL.md:125` ("Prefer `$(MAKE)`
for recursive calls, never bare `make`") and `SKILL.md:126` ("Keep help output and target names
stable enough for humans and CI to rely on") each have either a dead checker (`:124` →
`scripts/validate_makefile.sh:500`; `:125` → `:405`) or none at all (`:126`). Stated as rules,
enforced as nothing.

**F-7 — Unverifiable statistics presented as findings.**
`references/common-mistakes.md:929-936` presents four percentages ("35% of developers face issues…",
"40% experience inaccurate profiling…", "60% reduction…", "40% faster…") attributed to "research from
build system studies (2024-2025)", sourced at `:946-947` to two moldstud.com articles. The same
numbers are reused inline as authority at `:82` ("Up to 40% faster rule resolution"), `:276-278`
("35%+ of developers"), `:316` ("Over 60% reduction"), `:642` ("40%+ in some cases") and `:676`
("up to 60%"). `references/optimization-guide.md:712-723` presents a benchmark table with no
methodology and no source. See section 7.

**F-8 — Not found: stale model pins (class 1), `__pycache__` (class 8), CRLF in shipped scripts,
description defects.** `grep` for model names returns nothing. `find` for `__pycache__`/`*.pyc`
returns nothing. All three scripts are LF. The description is 538 chars, bracket-free, with a
do-not-use clause. Credit where due — these are the classes this pack got right.

### 4. Boundary map

**What the skill says today.** Three of the five companion routes touch the D8 seam:

- `SKILL.md:138` — "Pair with `$alaa-gitlab-ci-cd` when the Makefile is the operator surface for GitLab jobs or release automation."
- `SKILL.md:139` — "Pair with `$alaa-docker-production` when targets mainly wrap image builds, Compose, or production container workflows."
- `SKILL.md:141` — "Pair with `$caas-arvan-kuber` when Make targets enforce Arvan-safe delivery behavior."

All three say "pair with". None states ownership. "Pair with" is a suggestion to read two things; it
does not tell an agent which one decides when they disagree. Under the standing D8 ruling that is
the half of the boundary this skill owes and has not written.

**On the brief's specific claim.** The brief records that "`alaa-makefile:141-142` already routes to
`caas-arvan-kuber` on consecutive lines, which is one of the better-discriminated call sites in the
batch". The citation is off by a line — the `caas-arvan-kuber` route is a single line, `SKILL.md:141`,
and `:142` is blank; `:140` is the `alaa-k8s-helm` route. On the substance I have to **overrule the
assessment**. Judged by the wording test, `SKILL.md:141` is the *weakest* of the five, not the
strongest: "when Make targets enforce Arvan-safe delivery behavior" is an abstract noun standing in
for an observable condition — an agent cannot look at a Makefile and determine whether a target
"enforces Arvan-safe delivery behavior". The best-discriminated route in the set is `SKILL.md:140`
("when Make targets are wrappers around Helm or Kubernetes operations"), which names commands an
agent can grep for. Ranking the five by testability: `:140` (good) > `:139` (good but hedged by
"mainly") > `:138` (adequate) > `:137` (circular — "when recipe complexity becomes shell-script
complexity") > `:141` (untestable). Reciprocity is near-absent in the other direction:
`caas-arvan-kuber/SKILL.md:176` names `alaa-makefile` back, and none of `alaa-docker-production`,
`alaa-gitlab-ci-cd`, `alaa-k8s-helm` or `alaa-haproxy` mention it at all.

**The sentence the skill should state.** I tested the brief's reading against the material and the
live kits and it holds; the evidence strengthens it. `service-ci-kit/gitlab/platform-app-php-service.yml:160`
and `:219` show the fleet's gates are already commands with definitions living in
`service-ci-kit/ci/scripts/`, invoked from a runner. A Makefile that fronts them adds one thing and
only one thing: a second, local caller of the same command. So:

> A Make target is a **local invocation** of a command whose definition is owned elsewhere.
> `alaa-makefile` decides how the invocation is named, ordered, made re-runnable and made to fail
> loudly; it does not decide what the command is. Where a target fronts a CI gate, the command it
> runs must be byte-identical to the command the runner runs, and its exit status must be the
> gate's verdict unmodified — `alaa-gitlab-ci-cd` owns how that gate is expressed on a runner.
> Where a target fronts an image build or a Compose invocation, `alaa-docker-production` owns the
> Dockerfile, the image expression and the Compose file including the fail-closed interpolation
> invariant; `alaa-makefile` owns only the target name, its prerequisites and its failure
> propagation. This skill writes no provider YAML, no Dockerfile and no Compose file.

The operative consequence — "the local verdict must equal the runner's verdict" — is the skill's
actual reason to exist on this fleet, and it is the one sentence that makes the rest of the pack
subordinate to something. It also generates testable rules the skill does not currently have: a
target that fronts a gate may not add flags the runner does not pass, may not swallow the exit
status behind `-` or `|| true`, and may not re-implement the gate inline.

Two further boundaries the skill should name and does not: `alaa-reliability-sla` for why a retry or
timeout exists (the pack has no retry or timeout content at all), and `alaa-observability-soc` /
`alaa-services-contract` for what a target emits on failure (criterion 4).

### 5. Duplication

I measured rather than asserted, and the headline is that **the frontend batch's pattern is not
present here.** Normalised exact-line overlap across the eleven topical references:

| file | distinct lines | also appear elsewhere | % duplicated |
|---|---|---|---|
| `makefile-structure.md` | 260 | 76 | 29.2% |
| `targets-guide.md` | 303 | 85 | 28.1% |
| `patterns-guide.md` | 263 | 67 | 25.5% |
| `variables-guide.md` | 299 | 54 | 18.1% |
| `best-practices.md` | 395 | 55 | 13.9% |
| `optimization-guide.md` | 388 | 47 | 12.1% |
| `authoring-full-guide.md` | 278 | 30 | 10.8% |
| `common-mistakes.md` | 397 | 36 | 9.1% |
| `validation-full-guide.md` | 373 | 29 | 7.8% |
| `bake-tool.md` | 483 | 36 | 7.5% |
| `security-guide.md` | 155 | 4 | 2.6% |

No pair exceeds 18.3% verbatim overlap (`patterns-guide.md` → `targets-guide.md`). **No file is a
near-copy of another, and no file should be retired on verbatim-duplication grounds.** The brief's
four suspected pairs do not hold as verbatim duplicates: `authoring-full-guide.md` overlaps its four
suspected sources by 5.8%/6.1%/5.8%/5.0%, and `validation-full-guide.md` overlaps `common-mistakes.md`
by 0.0% and `bake-tool.md` by only 5.8%.

**The real duplication is conceptual, and it is total.** I sampled 20 canonical rules the skill
teaches and counted how many files teach each:

| rule | files |
|---|---|
| `.DELETE_ON_ERROR` | **8** — authoring-full-guide, best-practices, common-mistakes, makefile-structure, optimization-guide, security-guide, targets-guide, validation-full-guide |
| `-MMD -MP` dependency generation | **8** — authoring-full-guide, best-practices, common-mistakes, makefile-structure, optimization-guide, patterns-guide, targets-guide, variables-guide |
| automatic variables (`$@ $< $^`) | **7** |
| `?=` for user override | **5** |
| hardcoded credentials | **5** |
| `.NOTPARALLEL` | **5** |
| `.PHONY` rationale | **4** |
| `:=` vs `=` | **4** |
| order-only prerequisites | **4** |
| tabs not spaces | **4** |
| "Recursive Make Considered Harmful" | 3 |
| modern header (`-eu -o pipefail`) | 3 |
| wildcard cached with `:=` | 3 |
| mbake usage | 3 |
| ccache, VPATH, pkg-config, checkmake, help-target, unsafe-`rm` | 2 each |

**20 of 20 sampled rules are taught in more than one file.** Concrete instances with citations:
`.DELETE_ON_ERROR` and the identical GNU manual quotation ("This is almost always what you want make
to do, but it is not historical practice; so for compatibility, you must explicitly request it")
appear at `references/best-practices.md:69-70` and again at `references/common-mistakes.md:59-60`.
The `?=`/`:=` operator explanation appears at `references/makefile-structure.md:56-129`,
`references/variables-guide.md:12-130`, `references/best-practices.md:253-308` and
`references/common-mistakes.md:377-413`. The automatic-variables table appears at
`references/makefile-structure.md:203-212`, `references/variables-guide.md:279-299` and
`references/best-practices.md:779-796`. The mbake false-positive list appears at
`references/authoring-full-guide.md:303-313`, `references/validation-full-guide.md:568-573`,
`references/bake-tool.md:12-20` and again in code at `scripts/validate_makefile.sh:201-206`.

This is a direct violation of section 7's "Every instruction appears exactly once across the whole
skill." It is not fixable by retiring a file; it is fixable only by assigning each rule one owner
file and cutting it everywhere else.

**Nominated for retirement** (Phase 2 moves to `_to_delete/2026-07-29-batch7/`; nothing is deleted):

1. **`references/authoring-full-guide.md`** — 552 lines. Not because it duplicates (10.8%), but
   because it is a *retired skill's `SKILL.md`*, not a reference. It issues tool orders that no
   longer apply (`:24` `AskUserQuestion`, `:115-125` `mcp__context7__*`), documents a `docs/`
   directory that does not exist (`:539-546`), and its unique content is a 7-section mandatory prose
   report format (`:315-382`) that criterion 9 identifies as a bypass risk. Reproduction of what
   survives: the header choice at `:141-160` is already in `SKILL.md:115-121`; the language build
   rules at `:185-225` are already in `patterns-guide.md:218-473`; the standard-target block at
   `:227-253` is already in `targets-guide.md:77-261`; the Docker error-handling and `.NOTPARALLEL`
   patterns at `:393-425` are the only genuinely unique material and move to the new
   `ci-entrypoint.md`.
   `git show HEAD:references/authoring-full-guide.md | sed -n '393,425p'` recovers the part worth keeping.

2. **`references/validation-full-guide.md`** — 607 lines. Same reason: the retired
   `makefile-validator` `SKILL.md`. It describes a directory layout that does not exist (`:466-480`),
   hard-codes an install path that is wrong for this pack in five places (`:313, 332, 385, 410, 418`),
   and re-documents `bake-tool.md`'s subject at `:212-222` and `:521-547`. Its unique and correct
   content is the capability list at `:25-88` and the exit-code contract at `:254-258` — both of
   which belong in the validator's own `--help` output, not in a reference.
   `git show HEAD:references/validation-full-guide.md | sed -n '25,88p;254,258p'` recovers it.

3. **`references/best-practices.md`** — 861 lines. This one *is* nominated on duplication grounds,
   but conceptual rather than verbatim: it is a digest of the other files. Its eleven sections map
   one-to-one onto `makefile-structure.md` (File Organization), `targets-guide.md` (Target
   Declarations), `variables-guide.md` (Variable Management), `optimization-guide.md` (Performance
   Optimization), `security-guide.md` (Security), `patterns-guide.md` (Advanced Patterns) and
   `common-mistakes.md` (Portability). It contributes exactly two things not stated elsewhere: the
   namespaced-target convention at `:172-190` and the `VERBOSE ?= 0` / `Q :=` quiet-mode idiom at
   `:394-418`. Both are one paragraph.
   `git show HEAD:references/best-practices.md | sed -n '172,190p;394,418p'` recovers them.

I do **not** nominate `common-mistakes.md`, `bake-tool.md`, `optimization-guide.md`,
`security-guide.md` or the four topic guides. `common-mistakes.md` is the pack's only
failure-class-organised file and is the right shape for the section 5 restructure (symptom →
consequence → fix), needing only its fabricated statistics stripped. `security-guide.md` is the
strongest file in the pack at 2.6% duplication.

### 6. Wording-test failures

Each of these could be followed exactly by a competent agent that then does the wrong thing.

| Citation | Text | Failure |
|---|---|---|
| `SKILL.md:115` | "Prefer a clear header and special-target preamble **when the repo benefits from** GNU Make hardening" | Preference verb plus an unobservable condition. "Benefits from" is not a property an agent can evaluate. The positive replacement is a rule: every Makefile in this fleet sets the preamble; a Makefile that must run under `/bin/sh` states why in a comment. |
| `SKILL.md:132` | "Treat recipe indentation, shell failure behavior, credential leakage, and unsafe variable expansion **as high-priority issues**" | Names four conditions and no verdict. "High-priority" is a feeling. Replace with: these four are blocking; a review with any of them open does not pass. |
| `SKILL.md:124` | "Use order-only prerequisites for directories **when that avoids timestamp-driven rebuild noise**" | The trailing clause is always true for directories, so the scope is vacuous, and the checker is dead (`scripts/validate_makefile.sh:500`). Either state it unconditionally or drop it. |
| `SKILL.md:137` | "when recipe complexity becomes shell-script complexity" | Circular — defines complexity by complexity. Replace with a countable threshold, e.g. a recipe of more than N commands, or any recipe containing a loop, conditional or function definition. |
| `SKILL.md:141` | "when Make targets enforce Arvan-safe delivery behavior" | Abstract noun for an observable condition (section 4). |
| `SKILL.md:18` | "Prefer stricter POSIX compatibility only when the target environment **really needs it**" | "Really needs it" has no test. The observable version names the environment: a target that must run in the `busybox`/`alpine` default shell, or under BSD make. |
| `SKILL.md:46` | "Read `references/SOURCES.md` when … **latest/current behavior** … matters" | "Matters" is unbounded. |
| `SKILL.md:111` | "These are good for deterministic scaffolding, **but do not stop there**" | Prohibition with no positive replacement — does not say what "not stopping" consists of. |
| `references/best-practices.md:348-351` | "`clean:` / `-rm -rf $(BUILD_DIR)`" presented as "Method 1" of error handling | Actively teaches the silent-success defect as a remedy. A rule that produces the bug it is meant to prevent. |
| `scripts/validate_makefile.sh:353` | "Consider using 'set -e', '||', '&&' **or '-' prefix** for error control" | Same defect, emitted by the tool. The `-` prefix suppresses the error; it is not error control. |
| `references/authoring-full-guide.md:93-95` | "**CRITICAL:** You MUST explicitly use the Read tool to consult relevant docs during generation, even if you have prior knowledge." | Constraint verb applied to an obsolete instruction (the paths it names do not exist), which trains the agent to disregard "MUST". |

### 7. Stale or unverifiable claims

Checked 2026-07-29 against primary sources.

| Claim | Where | Source checked | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|
| GNU Make current release | pack-wide version floors | [ftp.gnu.org/gnu/make](https://ftp.gnu.org/gnu/make/), [NEWS via Fossies](https://fossies.org/linux/make/NEWS) | **HOLDS.** Latest stable is **4.4.1, 26 Feb 2023**; nothing newer exists as of today. The pack's floors are all correct: `.ONESHELL` 3.82+ (`authoring-full-guide.md:312`), modern preamble 4.0+ (`authoring-full-guide.md:150`, `best-practices.md:45`), `.WAIT` and `.NOTPARALLEL`-with-prerequisites 4.4+ (`optimization-guide.md:66-68, 109, 111-124, 187-192`). `optimization-guide.md:68` correctly dates 4.4 to October 2022. |
| `.NOTINTERMEDIATE`, `--shuffle`, `$(let …)`, `$(intcmp …)` | **not named anywhere in the pack** | [GNU Make 4.4 announcement](https://lists.gnu.org/archive/html/info-gnu/2022-10/msg00008.html) | **GAP, not a stale claim.** All four are 4.4 features and the skill names none of them. `.NOTINTERMEDIATE` is the material omission: the pack teaches `.INTERMEDIATE`/`.SECONDARY`/`.PRECIOUS` in three files (`targets-guide.md:429-439`, `best-practices.md:528-543`, `optimization-guide.md:352-366`) and never mentions the 4.4 target that turns the behaviour off. `--shuffle` is directly relevant to criterion 5 — it is the official way to *test* the parallel-safety the pack spends 230 lines describing, and the pack never suggests running it. Also unmentioned: `--jobserver-style` and named-pipe jobservers. |
| Deprecations in 4.4 | pack silent | same announcement | Nothing the fleet uses. 4.4 deprecated OS/2 (EMX), AmigaOS, Xenix and Cray; nothing was removed. No action. |
| **mbake repository URL** | `references/SOURCES.md:20` gives `https://github.com/StackExchange/mbake` | fetched | **STALE — the URL is a 404.** The canonical repository is `https://github.com/EbodShojaei/bake`, which the pack states correctly in three other places (`bake-tool.md:990`, `validation-full-guide.md:589`, `common-mistakes.md:942`). `SOURCES.md` is the provenance ledger and is the one file that has it wrong. |
| mbake maintained, and invoked as the skill invokes it | `SKILL.md:3`, `scripts/validate_makefile.sh:122, 177, 193` | [EbodShojaei/bake](https://github.com/EbodShojaei/bake), [PyPI](https://pypi.org/project/mbake/) | **HOLDS.** Actively maintained; latest **v1.4.6, 31 Mar 2026**; 747 stars; installs as `pip install mbake`. Subcommands `format`, `validate`, `init`, `config`, `update` all exist as documented in `bake-tool.md:121-218`. The script's `mbake validate` and `mbake format --check` invocations are correct — verified live in section 9, both ran and returned sensible output. |
| **checkmake repository URL** | `references/SOURCES.md:19` gives `https://github.com/mrtazz/checkmake` | fetched | **STALE but not broken.** The project has moved to the `checkmake` org; `mrtazz/checkmake` redirects. Canonical is `https://github.com/checkmake/checkmake` (1.2k stars, 368 commits, active). The `go install github.com/checkmake/checkmake/cmd/checkmake@latest` path used at `scripts/validate_makefile.sh:536`, `bake-tool.md:933, 982` and `validation-full-guide.md:284` is correct. Fix `SOURCES.md:19`. |
| unmake maintained | `references/SOURCES.md:21`, `scripts/validate_makefile.sh:563` | [mcandre/unmake](https://github.com/mcandre/unmake) | **HOLDS with a caveat.** Alive, latest **v0.0.27**, URL correct. Output format uses `warning:` so the counter at `scripts/validate_makefile.sh:574` will match. **Caveat:** upstream documents invocation against a *directory* (`unmake .`, a "recursive POSIX makefile linter"); `scripts/validate_makefile.sh:569` invokes `unmake "$file"` against a single file, which is undocumented. Phase 2 should verify single-file invocation or switch to the documented form. |
| Docker Buildx Bake | — | — | **The brief's premise is incorrect and I am correcting it.** `references/bake-tool.md` is **not** about Docker Buildx Bake. It contains 0 occurrences of "buildx", "bake.hcl" or HCL syntax and 141 occurrences of "mbake"; line 1 reads `# mbake Tool Reference`. It is documentation for **mbake, the Python Makefile formatter** — which is `alaa-makefile`'s own named validation tool (`SKILL.md:3`). Applying the deciding test — *does this decide something about a Makefile, or about an image?* — mbake decides Makefile formatting, so the subject belongs here and **must not** be routed to `alaa-docker-production`. The filename is the trap; only the filename. That said the file is badly overweight for what it decides: of 1,010 lines, roughly 400 are editor integration (VS Code `:592-656`, Vim `:658-674`, Emacs `:676-698`), CI YAML for GitHub Actions and GitLab (`:455-551`) that belongs to `alaa-gitlab-ci-cd`, and shell recipes for batch reformatting (`:700-793`). It should shrink to the ~200 lines that state what mbake asserts, how it is invoked, its exit codes and its known false positives — not be retired. Rename to `mbake-tool.md` to remove the trap. |
| `make --profile=profile.log` | `references/optimization-guide.md:565` and `:776` | executed locally | **FALSE — the flag does not exist.** `make --profile=/tmp/p.log` returns `make: unrecognized option '--profile='`; `make --help` contains zero occurrences of "profile". The pack teaches a fabricated flag twice, in a section headed "Profiling Tools". `make -d --debug=basic` at `:559` *is* accepted. Remove both `--profile` lines. |
| "35% of developers…", "40% faster…", "60% reduction…" | `references/common-mistakes.md:82, 276-278, 316, 642, 676, 929-936`; sourced at `:946-947` | moldstud.com | **UNVERIFIABLE.** Sourced to a content-farm domain, not a study. `references/SOURCES.md:40` states the pack's own rule: community posts are "for concrete troubleshooting after manuals … Do not use them as normative … guidance." These numbers are used as normative authority in six places, violating the pack's own conflict-resolution policy. Delete the numbers; keep the underlying advice, which is independently sound. |
| Benchmark table (45s→3s, "3-4x", "10x") | `references/optimization-guide.md:712-729` | none given | **UNVERIFIABLE.** No methodology, no hardware, no source. Delete or label as illustrative. |

### 8. Router audit

**Structure — two routers, and a third listing.** With 13 files in `references/` the convention
selects `references/00-topic-map.md` as the single router. It exists and is good: 39 lines, four
lanes, one trigger condition per file, and a closing working rule (`:36-39`) telling the agent to
open the smallest match. But `SKILL.md:143-162` re-lists all 13 files under the same four groupings,
and the lane sections at `SKILL.md:53-58`, `:71-76`, `:88-91` and `:104-109` list them a third time.
Three listings of the same 13 files inside one skill. Delete `SKILL.md:143-162` entirely and replace
the per-lane file lists with a single pointer to the topic map.

**Route quality — the topic map's own routes are good.** `00-topic-map.md:7-16` gives five
discriminated conditions ("Need overall structure, sections, and file layout", "Need variable
conventions and override behavior", …). These pass the wording test. The weakness is that the
conditions describe *Make topics*, not *tasks the fleet has* — there is no route for "I need a
target that runs the CI gate locally" because no file answers it.

**Companion routes.** Assessed in section 4: one good (`SKILL.md:140`), two adequate (`:138`,
`:139`), one circular (`:137`), one untestable (`:141`). All five carry the wrong trigger syntax
(F-1). Missing routes that the ten criteria require: `alaa-observability-soc` /
`alaa-services-contract` (criterion 4), `alaa-reliability-sla` (criterion 2),
`alaa-testing-strategy` (the `test:` target is the fleet's most-used target and the pack never says
what it should assert), and `alaa-project-constitution` for the quality bar itself.

**`SOURCES.md` is a legitimate provenance ledger** (`SOURCES.md:9-30` primary sources, `:32-36`
conflict resolution, `:38-40` community-source policy) and must be kept. It needs two URL
corrections (section 7) and nothing else. Do not confuse it with the router.

### 9. Scripts and assets audit

I wrote four Makefiles in `/tmp` — one clean fleet-shaped file, one carrying the defects the skill
names as mattering most, one isolating `.ONESHELL`, one isolating recipe-line checks — and ran all
three scripts against them. Results are empirical.

#### `scripts/validate_makefile.sh` (25,197 bytes, 659 lines) — does it earn its size? **No.**

**Windows:** partially prepared, better than the batch average. `PYTHONUTF8`/`PYTHONIOENCODING` are
exported (`:10-11`); `check_dependencies` falls back `python3` → `python` → `py -3` (`:73-81`);
`resolve_venv_python` (`:89-99`) and `resolve_mbake` (`:101-111`) both probe `Scripts/*.exe` as well
as `bin/`; `VENV_DIR` respects `TMPDIR` (`:34`). It needs `bash` (process substitution at `:340`),
so Git Bash or WSL, which is acceptable. **However** the pack ships no `.gitattributes`, so a
Windows checkout with `core.autocrlf=true` will give all three scripts CRLF line endings and the
`#!/usr/bin/env bash` shebang will fail — this is the second Windows defect class named in the
brief, and it is unguarded. Add `*.sh text eol=lf`.

**`--help`: absent.** `main` prints usage only when `$# -eq 0` (`:608-619`). `bash validate_makefile.sh --help`
treats `--help` as a filename and dies at `:133` with `[ERROR] File not found: --help`, exit 1.
Verified.

**Self-test: none.** Zero occurrences of any self-test, `--test`, bats or shunit harness.

**Exit codes: they do NOT distinguish "could not run" from "clean".** The documented contract
(`references/validation-full-guide.md:254-258`) is 0 clean / 1 warnings / 2 errors, and
`print_summary:594-603` implements it. But `error_exit` (`:53-56`) uses `exit 1` for *every*
could-not-run condition — no Python (`:80`), file not found (`:133`), file unreadable (`:137`), venv
creation failed (`:118`), mbake install failed (`:122`), mbake not locatable (`:109`) — and usage
(`:618`) also exits 1. Verified: a nonexistent file returns **1**, and a clean-but-warned Makefile
also returns **1**. **A CI gate calling this script cannot distinguish a passing validation from a
broken environment.** For a tool whose purpose is to be a gate, this is the most serious design
defect in the script. Fix: reserve 0/1/2 for verdicts and use 3+ for could-not-run.

**A latent `set -e` abort.** `:7` sets `set -euo pipefail`; `:86` does `((WARNINGS++))` with
`WARNINGS` still 0. Post-increment returns the old value 0, so the arithmetic command's exit status
is 1, and `check_dependencies` is called at `:627` *without* `|| true`. So on any machine where GNU
make is absent the script exits 1 silently at the dependency check — reported as "warnings found".
Verified the mechanism (`bash -c 'set -e; W=0; ((W++)); echo reached'` prints nothing, exit 1). The
other counter sites are accidentally protected because their callers use `|| true` (`:632-647`).

**What it caught** on the defective Makefile (exit 2, 1 error / 4 warnings / 5 info): hardcoded
credential `API_TOKEN` as an ERROR (`:317` — works well, and the pattern list is broad and good);
missing `.DELETE_ON_ERROR` (`:258`); missing `.PHONY` (`:295`); missing `SHELL`/`MAKEFLAGS`
(`:270-292`); variables without defaults used in `rm -rf`/`curl` (`:330-348`); undocumented default
target (`:370`). Six checks, all correct.

**What it missed — and why.** Six of nineteen checks are dead code from **one bug**: inside a
double-quoted `grep -E` pattern, `\t` is not a tab. `grep -E "^\t..."` compiles to "line begins with
the letter *t*". Verified: `grep -nE "^\t" file` matched the line `that line` and did not match a
real tab-indented line. Every affected check inspects a recipe line, i.e. every check about what a
target actually does:

| line | check | consequence |
|---|---|---|
| `:351` | recipe commands lack error handling | Fires whenever any target name starts with "t" (e.g. `test:`) and never otherwise. It fired on my **clean** Makefile and on the **defective** one identically — a constant, not a signal. |
| `:405` | bare `make` instead of `$(MAKE)` | Dead. `SKILL.md:125` states this as a rule; my defective Makefile contains `make -C sub build` and it was not reported. |
| `:454, :456` | `.ONESHELL` without `.SHELLFLAGS` | Dead, and this is the worst one. I built a Makefile with `.ONESHELL:` and no `.SHELLFLAGS`, whose `deploy:` recipe does `cd /nonexistent` then `echo`. `make deploy` **printed "still ran - failure was swallowed" and exited 0.** The validator emitted no `.ONESHELL` warning at all. The single check guarding the fleet's most dangerous silent-success mode does not run. |
| `:500` | `mkdir -p` without order-only prerequisite | Dead — verified no match against a Makefile containing a tab-indented `mkdir -p build`. |
| `:514` | `docker build` / `npm install` without `.NOTPARALLEL` | Dead — verified no match against tab-indented `docker build` and `npm install` recipes. This is the check most relevant to a Compose-fronting Makefile. |

A seventh check is dead from a different bug: `:382`, recursive `=` with `$(shell …)`. The pattern
`"…\s*=\s*\$\(shell"` is written in double quotes, so bash strips the backslash before `$` and
`grep -E` then reads `$` as an end-of-line anchor. Verified: the as-written pattern returns no match
against `BUILD_DIR = $(shell git rev-parse --show-toplevel)/build`; the single-quoted form matches.
My defective Makefile contained exactly that line and it was not reported.

Beyond the dead checks, three defects the brief names were missed by design, because no check for
them exists anywhere in the script: **the leading `-`** that makes a rule silently succeed (`-rm -rf
$(BUILD_DIR)/*` in my test file went unflagged — and, as noted in section 6, `:353` *recommends* the
`-` prefix); **a multi-command recipe with no `set -e`** (three sequential `go test` lines, only the
generic constant-fire INFO); and **`sh`-vs-`bash` divergence** — my `source ./env.sh` and
`declare -A` targets, both of which fail under the default `/bin/sh`, drew zero comment. `grep` for
`source`, `declare`, `bashism` or `/bin/sh` in the script returns 0 hits, despite `SKILL.md:24-27`
making the `/bin/sh`-vs-`bash` decision one of four framing decisions.

**Two false positives that block.** (a) The tab check `:306` matches `^(  |    |        )[a-zA-Z@$(]`,
which flags legal variable line-continuations. Verified end-to-end: a Makefile whose only offence is
`SOURCES = main.c \` continued with four-space-indented `utils.c` was reported as
`✗ Potential spaces instead of tabs in recipes detected` and returned **exit 2**. It also misses 3-,
5-, 6- and 7-space indentation entirely. (b) `syntax_check:160` runs `make -n --dry-run` against the
*default goal* and reports any failure as `✗ Syntax errors detected`. A missing prerequisite file is
not a syntax error. Verified: the skill's own generated Go template reports "Syntax errors detected"
solely because `go.mod`/`go.sum` are absent from the directory; `touch go.mod go.sum` and it passes.
Any correct Makefile validated in a checkout without materialised sources gets a hard error.

**Two round-trip failures.** The skill's canonical header — every rule in `SKILL.md:115-121` — still
draws a warning and **exit 1** from the skill's own validator (mbake "Would reformat", plus the
constant-fire error-handling INFO despite `.SHELLFLAGS := -eu -o pipefail -c` being present).
And `generate_makefile_template.sh`'s **Go** template, the one the fleet would actually use, fails
the validator with **2 errors, exit 2** — it omits `.DELETE_ON_ERROR`, `SHELL`, `.SHELLFLAGS` and
both `MAKEFLAGS` lines, all of which `SKILL.md:115-121` requires. The generator and the validator do
not agree with each other or with the body.

**Cost.** Creates a venv and pip-installs mbake on every invocation (`:114-126`), ~20-40s per file,
no cache, no offline path, no `--no-venv` escape for an environment that already has mbake.

#### `scripts/add_standard_targets.sh` (10,468 bytes, 442 lines) — **completely non-functional.**

`set -euo pipefail` at `:11`; `local targets_added=0` at `:380`; `((targets_added++))` at `:395` and
`((targets_skipped++))` at `:388`, both inside `main`, which is called at `:441` with no `|| true`.
The first increment from 0 returns exit status 1 and `set -e` kills the script. Verified in every
mode:

- `add_standard_targets.sh Makefile clean test help` → exit **1**, Makefile **unmodified**. `bash -x` shows the last executed line is `+ (( targets_added++ ))`.
- `-n/--dry-run` → exit **1** after printing one "Would add" line.
- Against a Makefile where the target already exists (skip path) → exit **1** after one "Skipped" line.

Only `-h/--help` (`:336`) and `-l/--list` (`:340`) work, because both `exit 0` before reaching the
loop. **The script has never worked for its stated purpose.** `SKILL.md:109` and
`references/authoring-full-guide.md:519-531` both advertise it as a working tool. Windows: LF, no
CRLF guard. Exit codes: `--help` 0, `-l` 0, missing file 1 (`:373`) — and 1 is also the accidental
failure code, so could-not-run is again indistinguishable. No self-test. Separately, even if the
counter bug were fixed, `add_missing_variables:241-326` unconditionally injects `PROJECT := myproject`,
`TARGET := $(PROJECT)`, `BUILDDIR := build` and `VERSION := 1.0.0` into the user's file — junk for a
Makefile fronting Compose — and rewrites the whole file through a fragile
`tail -n +$(...grep -c $'\n'...)` header-split at `:311`.

#### `scripts/generate_makefile_template.sh` (13,356 bytes, 598 lines) — **works.**

The one script that earns its place. All seven types (`c`, `c-lib`, `cpp`, `go`, `python`, `java`,
`generic`) generate and every output parses under `make -n`. Verified individually.

- **`--help`: yes** (`:88-91`, exit 0), and the usage text at `:47-78` is accurate and lists all types with examples.
- **Self-test: none.**
- **Exit codes: partially.** Unknown type → 1 with no file created (`:575-580`, verified). Existing file without `-f` in non-interactive mode → 1 (`:544-548`) — a genuinely well-handled case. **But** a missing required `PROJECT_TYPE` exits **0** (`:592-595`), so a caller that forgets the argument sees success and no file. Should be 1 or 2.
- **Windows:** LF, no `.gitattributes` guard. `[[ -t 1 ]]` colour detection (`:21`) and `[[ -t 0 ]]` interactivity detection (`:535`) are correct and portable. No `Path(__file__).parents[N]`-class fragility; `SCRIPT_DIR` (`:12`) is computed safely, though it is assigned and never used in either generator script (dead variable, shellcheck SC2034).
- **Content defects:** the `cpp` type is produced by `sed 's/gcc/g++/g; s/\.c/.cpp/g'` over the C template (`:561`), which leaves the variables named `CC`/`CFLAGS` rather than `CXX`/`CXXFLAGS`. And the **Go** template (`:250-301`) — the fleet-relevant one — omits the entire hardening preamble that `SKILL.md:115-121` mandates, which is why it fails the sibling validator.

#### Assets

`agents/openai.yaml` (7 lines) is correct and needs no change: `$alaa-makefile` at `:4` is the right
Codex-only form. No `__pycache__`, no `*.pyc`, no `.DS_Store`. No `examples/` directory despite
`references/validation-full-guide.md:477-479` claiming `examples/good-makefile.mk` and
`examples/bad-makefile.mk` exist.

### 10. Rewrite brief for Phase 2

**Target body size: 150-165 lines (≤ 6,800 bytes), down from 177 lines / 7,359 bytes.** The body
loses the triple reference listing (`SKILL.md:143-162`, 20 lines) and the per-lane file lists
(~14 lines), and gains the boundary sentence (~8 lines) and the CI-entrypoint rules (~10 lines). It
must not grow. References may and should grow — the pack is over-covered on C and under-covered on
its actual job, so this is a redistribution, not an expansion.

**Body changes, in order:**

1. Keep the frontmatter description at `SKILL.md:3` **unchanged** — 538 chars, no brackets, has its do-not-use clause.
2. `SKILL.md:137-141` — convert all five routes to dual syntax: `/alaa-bash-shell` (`$alaa-bash-shell`), and likewise for the other four. Rewrite `:137` and `:141` to observable conditions per section 6. Add four routes: `/alaa-observability-soc`, `/alaa-reliability-sla`, `/alaa-testing-strategy`, `/alaa-services-contract`.
3. `SKILL.md:143-162` — **delete**. Replace with two lines pointing at `references/00-topic-map.md` and `references/SOURCES.md`.
4. `SKILL.md:53-58, 71-76, 88-91, 104-109` — strip the per-lane file lists; keep the "Use this lane for" trigger bullets, which are good.
5. Add a **Boundary** section stating the section-4 sentence in the skill's own words — local invocation, definition owned elsewhere, local verdict must equal the runner's verdict, writes no provider YAML / Dockerfile / Compose file.
6. `SKILL.md:115, 124, 132` — rewrite per section 6 from preference to constraint.
7. `SKILL.md:132` — replace with the blocking-defect list, adding the three the pack currently misses: leading `-` on a gate-fronting recipe, multi-command recipe without `set -e` or `.ONESHELL` + `-e`, and `bash`-only builtins in a recipe that has not set `SHELL`.

**Reference file list after the rewrite, one-line charter each:**

| file | charter |
|---|---|
| `00-topic-map.md` | The only router. Add a lane for "front a CI gate locally". |
| `SOURCES.md` | Provenance ledger. Fix `:19` → `checkmake/checkmake`, `:20` → `EbodShojaei/bake`. Keep otherwise. |
| **`ci-entrypoint.md`** *(new)* | **The file this skill exists for.** A Make target as a local invocation of a kit-owned command; target naming against `service-ci-kit/ci/scripts/*`; exit-status propagation unmodified; the `-`/`|| true`/`.ONESHELL`-without-`-e` prohibitions with their positive replacements; what a target prints on failure; `make -n` as the diff against the runner. Absorbs `authoring-full-guide.md:393-425`. |
| **`compose-and-container-targets.md`** *(new)* | Targets that front Compose and image builds, stating at the top that `alaa-docker-production` owns the Compose file and image expression and this file owns only target name, prerequisites and failure propagation. Absorbs `patterns-guide.md:525-553` and `security-guide.md:276-312`. |
| `targets-guide.md` | Standard targets, `.PHONY`, prerequisites, order-only. **Sole owner** of `.PHONY` rationale and order-only prerequisites. Strip C-linking examples. |
| `variables-guide.md` | Assignment operators, override precedence, environment boundary. **Sole owner** of `?=`/`:=`. Cut `:132-271` (the C toolchain and man/info directory sets) to a short table. |
| `makefile-structure.md` | File layout, includes, modular `.mk`. **Sole owner** of `.DELETE_ON_ERROR` and the special-target preamble. Cut `:226-258` (header dependency generation) → `native-toolchain.md`. |
| `patterns-guide.md` | Pattern and static-pattern rules. **Sole owner** of automatic variables. Cut patterns 1-4 and 7 (C/C++) → `native-toolchain.md`. |
| `common-mistakes.md` | **Restructure by failure class** per defect class 5: symptom → consequence → smallest fix → escalation. Delete the fabricated statistics at `:82, 276-278, 316, 642, 676, 929-936, 946-947`. |
| `security-guide.md` | Unchanged except: promote to the review lane's first read. Strongest file in the pack. |
| `optimization-guide.md` | Parallel safety and incremental builds. **Sole owner** of `.NOTPARALLEL`/`.WAIT`. Delete `--profile` at `:565` and `:776`; delete the benchmark table at `:712-729`. Add `--shuffle` and `.NOTINTERMEDIATE`. |
| `mbake-tool.md` *(renamed from `bake-tool.md`)* | What mbake asserts, invocation, exit codes, known false positives. Cut to ~200 lines: drop editor integration `:592-698` and CI YAML `:455-551`. Rename removes the Buildx-Bake trap. |
| **`native-toolchain.md`** *(new)* | Receives all C/C++/Java compilation material so it is preserved completely but loads only when a native build is genuinely in scope. |

**Retire to `_to_delete/2026-07-29-batch7/`** (nominate only; Phase 2 moves): `authoring-full-guide.md`,
`validation-full-guide.md`, `best-practices.md` — with the salvage commands in section 5.

**Executable checks the skill should ship and does not:**

1. **Fix the six dead `^\t` checks** — replace every `grep -E "^\t…"` with `grep -E $'^\t…'` at `scripts/validate_makefile.sh:351, 405, 454, 456, 500, 514`. This alone restores a third of the validator.
2. **Fix the `$(shell` check** at `:382` — single-quote the pattern.
3. **Fix `add_standard_targets.sh`** — `((n++))` → `n=$((n+1))` at `:388` and `:395`, or append `|| true`. Then add a self-test asserting it actually adds a target.
4. **Separate the exit-code namespaces** in all three scripts: 0 clean / 1 warnings / 2 errors / 3 could-not-run. Change every `error_exit` at `scripts/validate_makefile.sh:53-56` to `exit 3`, and `generate_makefile_template.sh:594` from 0 to 2.
5. **Add `--help`** to `validate_makefile.sh` (the other two have it).
6. **Add `--no-venv` / `MBAKE_BIN` override** so a prepared CI image skips the 20-40s pip install.
7. **New check: leading `-` on a recipe line in a target that fronts a gate** — currently unwritten, and `:353` recommends the defect. Remove `'-' prefix` from that message.
8. **New check: multi-command recipe with no `set -e`, no `&&` chaining and no `.ONESHELL`+`-e`.**
9. **New check: `bash`-only constructs (`source`, `declare -A`, `[[`, arrays, `pipefail`) in a Makefile that has not set `SHELL := bash`.** Maps directly to `SKILL.md:24-27`, which frames the decision and then ships nothing to enforce it.
10. **New check: a target whose recipe re-implements a `service-ci-kit/ci/scripts/*.sh` command inline rather than invoking it** — the executable form of the boundary sentence.
11. **Fix the two false positives:** rewrite the tab check at `:306` to fire only inside recipe context and to catch any 1-8 space indent; and make `syntax_check:160` distinguish "missing prerequisite" from "syntax error" by checking for `missing separator`/`unterminated`/`unexpected` rather than treating any non-zero exit as a syntax failure.
12. **Add `.gitattributes` with `*.sh text eol=lf`** — the pack's only unguarded Windows defect class.
13. **Add a self-test to each script** (`--self-test`) running it against a known-good and known-bad fixture and asserting the exit code. The pack currently ships zero.
14. **Regenerate the Go template** at `generate_makefile_template.sh:250-301` to include the `SKILL.md:115-121` preamble, so the generator and validator stop contradicting each other.

#### Defects found in a live repository (RFC, not a skill edit)

Neither kit contains a Makefile, so there is no Makefile defect to report. One observation for the
RFC pile: `service-ci-kit` exposes eleven gate scripts in `ci/scripts/` invoked from
`gitlab/platform-app-php-service.yml:160, 219` and elsewhere, but offers no local entrypoint, so a
developer cannot run a gate the way the runner runs it without reading the YAML and copying the
`bash .service-ci-kit/ci/scripts/<x>.sh` line by hand. That is the concrete gap the new
`ci-entrypoint.md` charter is written against, and it may warrant a `Makefile` shipped *in the kit*
rather than authored per service — a decision for the kit owners, not for this skill.

### Out-of-batch findings

None. `alaa-bash-shell` is named at `SKILL.md:137` and would be the natural owner of the
`sh`-vs-`bash` recipe check, but I have not inspected it and propose no change to it.

---

### Shared closing

**Is any new skill genuinely required?** **No.** Every gap I found sits inside `alaa-makefile`'s own
boundary. The C/C++ over-coverage is a redistribution into `native-toolchain.md`; the CI-entrypoint
absence is the skill's own core subject, unwritten; the mbake material is the skill's own named tool
and stays despite its misleading filename; the Compose and gate-expression boundaries are already
owned by `alaa-docker-production` and `alaa-gitlab-ci-cd` and need only a reciprocal sentence here,
not a new owner. The observability and failure-semantics gaps route to existing doctrine owners
(`alaa-observability-soc`, `alaa-reliability-sla`). Nothing requires a skill that does not exist, and
inventing one would only add a fourth listing of the same thirteen files.

The three most serious defects, in order. **First**, six of nineteen checks in
`validate_makefile.sh` are dead code from a single `grep -E "^\t"` bug — and because every one of
them inspects a recipe line, the validator cannot see anything a target actually *does*. I
demonstrated the cost concretely: a `.ONESHELL` Makefile whose `deploy` recipe fails at `cd /nonexistent`
prints "still ran" and exits 0, and the validator says nothing. **Second**,
`add_standard_targets.sh` fails on 100% of invocations — a `set -e` plus `((n++))`-from-zero abort
that means the script has never once done its job, while `SKILL.md:109` advertises it. **Third**, the
skill has no position on what it is for on this fleet: 48% of its examples are C compilation, 6% are
task-running, the fleet has no Makefile at all, and the one sentence that would make the pack
coherent — a Make target is a local invocation whose verdict must equal the runner's verdict — is
absent. Close behind: exit code 1 means both "passed with warnings" and "could not run", which makes
the tool unusable as the CI gate it is designed to be.
## Appendix G — `ansible-generator`

### 1. What this skill is today

`ansible-generator` is a four-file authoring skill: a 44-line, 2,096-byte always-loaded body
(`SKILL.md:6-48`), four references totalling 3,195 lines, and a 22-file `assets/templates/` tree that
is meant to be the structural foundation for everything it produces (`references/playbook.md:605-633`).
It ships **no scripts** and **no executable check of any kind**. Its entire verification story is one
sentence of delegation: "Validate with the `ansible-validator` skill (ansible-lint, check mode,
Molecule); fix and re-run" (`SKILL.md:37`).

The body is a genuine router and a good one by this programme's conventions: four references, listed
inline with a one-line charter each (`SKILL.md:45-48`), which is correct for a skill under the
nine-reference threshold. There is no `00-topic-map.md` and therefore no two-router defect. The
`source-map.md` is a provenance ledger, not a second router, and is legitimate.

The description (426 characters, no angle brackets) already carries a real "do not use for" clause
naming the sibling: "Do not use to validate or debug existing Ansible with no generation need (use
`ansible-validator`)" (`SKILL.md:3`). That is better discrimination than most of this repository has.

What the skill actually asserts:

- FQCN for every module, idempotency, verb-first task names, snake_case variables prefixed by role
  name, `no_log` on sensitive tasks, tags, header documentation (`references/playbook.md:479-517`).
- A module-selection priority that puts `ansible.builtin.*` first and explicitly recommends rewriting
  collection modules as `ansible.builtin.command` shell-outs when the validation environment lacks the
  collection (`references/playbook.md:519-582`).
- A mandatory eight-item pre-delivery checklist (`references/playbook.md:751-762`) and a mandatory
  output presentation format (`references/playbook.md:764-791`).
- 1,463 lines of module patterns (`references/module-patterns.md`) and 895 lines of best practice
  (`references/best-practices.md`), of which lines 265-641 are a 377-line Jinja2 deep dive on filters,
  lookups, control structures, whitespace control and macros that has no counterpart anywhere else in
  the batch.

The empirical headline: **six of the eight YAML files in the role and playbook scaffold do not parse.**
`assets/templates/role/defaults/main.yml`, `role/vars/main.yml`, `role/vars/Debian.yml`,
`role/vars/RedHat.yml`, `role/meta/argument_specs.yml` and `playbook/basic_playbook.yml` all raise a
`yaml.ParserError` or `ConstructorError` under PyYAML. The skill's own sibling's extractor reports the
same five role files and exits 1 (run recorded in §9).

### 2. Ten-criteria verdict

| # | Criterion | Verdict | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Correctness and testability | **FAIL** | Ships zero executable checks. Its own scaffold does not parse: `assets/templates/role/defaults/main.yml:6`, `role/vars/main.yml:6`, `role/vars/Debian.yml:4`, `role/vars/RedHat.yml:4`, `role/meta/argument_specs.yml:18`, `playbook/basic_playbook.yml:19` all fail YAML parse. `assets/templates/project/.ansible-lint:151-263` is rejected outright by ansible-lint 26.6.0 (exit 3). The "test-driven where the domain allows" bar is not met: nothing tests the generated artifact against a broken implementation. |
| 2 | Failure behaviour | **FAIL** | The only failure machinery anywhere is `until`/`retries`/`delay` on one health check (`references/playbook.md:110-112`), one async example (`references/playbook.md:703-719`) and rescue/always examples (`references/module-patterns.md:624-773`). None is stated as a rule; the mandatory checklist (`references/playbook.md:751-762`) contains no failure item. Nothing on `any_errors_fatal`, `max_fail_percentage`, `serial` batch sizing, connection timeout, or what a half-completed play leaves behind. |
| 3 | Security | **FAIL** | Rules exist as reminders (`references/playbook.md:497-501`, `references/best-practices.md:643-673`) but the shipped artefacts contradict them: `assets/templates/project/ansible.cfg:15` ships `host_key_checking = False`; `ansible.cfg:70-72` ships blanket `become = True` / `become_user = root`; `assets/templates/role/defaults/main.yml:20` defaults `bind_address: 0.0.0.0`. No trust boundary, no vault-id rotation, no `no_log` interaction with `--diff`, no owner named for fail-closed. |
| 4 | Observability | **FAIL** | Silent. `assets/templates/project/ansible.cfg:44` leaves `# log_path` commented out; `ansible.cfg:34` sets `callback_whitelist = timer, profile_tasks` under a key ansible-core no longer accepts. No run identifier, no structured callback, no statement of how a failed play is diagnosed after the fact. |
| 5 | Concurrency and load | **FAIL** | `assets/templates/project/ansible.cfg:53` ships `forks = 5`, which is the stock default. `references/best-practices.md:802-812` gives two sentences on forks and `serial` with no budget. Nothing on `strategy`, `throttle`, `ControlPersist` sizing for a large fleet, or fact-gathering cost across many hosts. |
| 6 | Clean code, SOLID, design patterns | SATISFIED | Role layout, defaults-vs-vars separation, role-name variable prefixing and OS-specific `include_vars` are stated as rules (`references/playbook.md:133-163`), and `meta/argument_specs.yml` is mandated (`references/playbook.md:285-293`). ansible-lint's `role-argument-spec` and `var-naming` rules can test these. |
| 7 | Algorithm and data-structure choice | **FAIL** | Silent, and no other owner is named. No complexity budget, nothing on `loop` over large inventories, nothing on the cost of `with_*` versus `loop`, nothing on fact-gathering subsets as a cost decision (`references/best-practices.md:792-800` shows `gather_subset` as a snippet, not a budget). |
| 8 | Configurability | SATISFIED | `references/playbook.md:285-293` mandates `meta/argument_specs.yml` with types, choices, required flags and defaults — validation at the boundary, testable by `role-argument-spec`. Variable precedence and defaults-vs-vars are stated (`references/best-practices.md:136-149`). The precedence list itself is wrong; see §7. |
| 9 | Speed of development and debuggability | **FAIL** | The mandatory presentation block is stated twice verbatim (`references/playbook.md:437-459` and `764-791`), which is ceremony an agent must reproduce on every delivery. The "validate → fix → re-validate" loop (`references/playbook.md:430-433`) has no bounded stopping condition. And an agent that follows `references/playbook.md:133` — "Copy and customize the complete role structure from `assets/templates/role/`" — starts from five files that will not parse. |
| 10 | Documentation | SATISFIED | Header comment contract (`references/playbook.md:54-66`), README template (`assets/templates/role/README.md`), variable documentation rule (`references/playbook.md:514-517`), `meta/main.yml` guidance (`references/best-practices.md:851-875`). |

Tally: **2 SATISFIED, 8 FAIL, 0 NOT-OWNED.** No `NOT-OWNED` verdict is available because the skill
names no doctrine owner anywhere: grepping the whole directory for `alaa-security-review`,
`alaa-reliability-sla`, `alaa-observability-soc`, `alaa-services-contract`, `alaa-prompting-guide`,
`alaa-project-constitution`, `alaa-controlled-ops` or `alaa-testing-strategy` returns nothing. The one
cross-skill call site it does have — `SKILL.md:37`, `SKILL.md:26` — points only at `ansible-validator`.

### 3. Defect classes actually found

**Class 2 — wrong trigger syntax. Confirmed, and in its most severe form.** `references/playbook.md`
writes `$ansible-validator` twelve times (`:11`, `:51`, `:157`, `:185`, `:418`, `:421`, `:423`, `:633`,
`:660`, `:735`, `:761`, `:803`). `agents/openai.yaml:4` correctly uses the bare `$` form and is exempt.
But there is not one `/ansible-validator` anywhere in the skill, and `SKILL.md` uses neither form — it
writes a bare backticked `` `ansible-validator` `` at `:3`, `:26` and `:37`. From Claude Code the
sibling skill is therefore invisible at every call site in the reference, and merely a noun in the body.

**Class 3 — duplication between body and references. Confirmed.** `references/playbook.md:6-23`
restates the body's `# Ansible Generator` / `## Overview` / `## Source freshness` / `## When NOT to use`
sections. `references/playbook.md:14-17` is a near-verbatim copy of `SKILL.md:19-22`;
`references/playbook.md:19-23` restates `SKILL.md:24-28`. The file even carries its own H1 at `:6`
after the H1 at `:1`. Internally the file duplicates itself: the presentation format block appears at
`:437-459` and again at `:764-791`, and "Module Selection Priority" appears at
`references/playbook.md:519-536` and again at `references/best-practices.md:890-895`.

**Class 5 — long numbered procedures nobody reads in order. Confirmed but low severity.** The
troubleshooting section `references/playbook.md:733-749` is two numbered lists of five items each,
symptom-only, with no diagnosis/retry/escalation structure. It is short enough to restructure cheaply.

**Class 6 — description that only says when to use. NOT found.** `SKILL.md:3` has a three-clause "do
not use" and names the sibling. This is one of the batch's better descriptions.

**Class 7 — fragile tooling. Not applicable in the `Path(__file__).parents[N]` sense** (no scripts).
But the moral equivalent is present: `assets/templates/project/ansible.cfg:26` hardcodes
`fact_caching_connection = /tmp/ansible_facts`, a path that does not exist on Windows control nodes and
is world-writable on shared Linux hosts.

**Class 8 — shipped `__pycache__`. NOT found.** `find` over the tree returns no `__pycache__` and no
`*.pyc`. `.gitignore:2-3` ignores them anyway.

**Class 10 — shrink where possible.** The always-loaded body is already 2,096 bytes. It should not
shrink; it should gain the boundary sentences named in §4 and the `/name` trigger forms. References
should grow.

**Class 11 — companion boundary. Half-present.** The skill names `ansible-validator` but names no other
skill in the batch and no doctrine owner. See §4.

**Class 12 — two routers. NOT found.** Four references, listed inline, no `00-topic-map.md`. Correct.

**Class 13 — a rule with no tool that reports a violation. Confirmed, and total.** The skill ships zero
executable checks. Every one of its "MANDATORY" rules (`references/playbook.md:414`, `:751`, `:766`) is
a preference. The one config it ships that could enforce anything —
`assets/templates/project/.ansible-lint` — is rejected by ansible-lint 26.6.0 with exit code 3, so a
project scaffolded from this skill cannot be linted at all until a human deletes 113 lines of it.

**Additional defect not in the standard list — the shipped scaffold does not parse.** Six of eight
template YAML files fail. Two distinct root causes:

1. Every placeholder is written as `[ name ]`, which YAML reads as a one-element flow sequence, so
   `[ role_name ]_package_name: [ package_name ]` is not a placeholder at all — it is a mapping key
   that begins with a sequence. `assets/templates/role/defaults/main.yml:6`,
   `role/vars/main.yml:6`, `role/vars/Debian.yml:4`, `role/vars/RedHat.yml:4`.
2. Every top-level key in those four files is indented two spaces under a bare `---` with column-0
   comments interleaved, producing `wrong indentation: expected 0 but found 2` and then a hard
   `syntax error: expected <block end>` at the first value.
   `assets/templates/role/meta/argument_specs.yml:18-26` has the same problem at 21-column indent, and
   `assets/templates/playbook/basic_playbook.yml:19` at 6-column indent.

**Additional defect — the scaffold violates the skill's own mandatory checklist.**
`references/playbook.md:757` states: "**Booleans correct** — Use `true`/`false` (NOT `yes`/`no`) to pass
ansible-lint." The skill's own templates use `yes`/`no` at `assets/templates/role/tasks/main.yml:40`
(`backup: yes`), `:50` (`enabled: yes`), `assets/templates/playbook/basic_playbook.yml:14-15`
(`become: [ yes/no ]`, `gather_facts: [ yes/no ]`), `:24` and `:32` (`update_cache: yes`), and in the
reference prose at `references/best-practices.md:710`, `:718`, `:787`, `:795`,
