| Checkov Ansible policy IDs `CKV_ANSIBLE_1-6`, `CKV2_ANSIBLE_1-6`, `CKV_AWS_88`, `CKV_AWS_135` | `references/playbook.md:255-282` | [Checkov Ansible policy index](https://www.checkov.io/5.Policy%20Index/ansible.html) | **Hold.** 12 Ansible-specific checks plus the two AWS ones — exactly the set listed. Checkov itself is **3.3.8** (2026-07-09) and the `--framework ansible` path works. Formatting at `:269-271` is corrupted (`-`, `*`, `- *CKV2_ANSIBLE_4:**`). |
| Checkov as the security scanner for Ansible | `SKILL.md:3`, `references/playbook.md:212-354` | executed | **Materially overstated.** Twelve checks, all about TLS/HTTPS/GPG. Run against a playbook with a hardcoded password, an AWS key pair, a DB connection string and an RSA private key, `--framework ansible` reported **zero** findings; `--framework secrets` reported **six** (`CKV_SECRET_2` ×2, `CKV_SECRET_6` ×2, `CKV_SECRET_4`, `CKV_SECRET_13`) including both AWS keys and the private key. The skill never enables the `secrets` framework. |
| `driver: name: docker` in the molecule template | `assets/molecule.yml.template:13-14` | executed with a stock `pip install molecule` (26.6.0) | **Fails as shipped:** `ERROR Failed to find driver docker`; `molecule drivers` lists only `default`. It works once `molecule-docker` is installed, with `WARNING Driver docker does not provide a schema`. |
| `pip install molecule molecule-docker` | `scripts/test_role.sh:106`, `scripts/setup_tools.sh:141,154,164,174,181,190`, `references/playbook.md:798` | PyPI; [Molecule installation](https://docs.ansible.com/projects/molecule/installation/) | **Deprecated route.** `molecule-docker` 2.1.0 was last released **2022-09-29**. The official docs now say "Molecule uses the 'delegated' driver by default", route drivers through `molecule-plugins` (`pip install "molecule-plugins[podman]"`), and explicitly tell upgraders to *uninstall* the standalone driver packages to avoid conflicts. The skill should install `molecule-plugins[docker]`. |
| `molecule init scenario --driver-name docker` | `references/playbook.md:482`, `scripts/test_role.sh:56`, `scripts/validate_role.sh:323` | Molecule usage docs | Stale advice; the current `init` flow assumes the default driver. `validate_role.sh:323` omits the flag entirely and `test_role.sh:56` includes it — the skill contradicts itself. |
| `lint: \| set -e / yamllint . / ansible-lint .` key | `assets/molecule.yml.template:107-110` | [Molecule configuration](https://docs.ansible.com/projects/molecule/configuration/) lists no `lint:` key; executed — molecule 26.6.0 accepts the file without a schema error | Dead configuration. Molecule no longer runs it; `scripts/test_role.sh:146-147` even comments that "molecule lint was removed in v5+". The template still ships the key. |
| `driver`/`platforms`/`provisioner`/`verifier` model | `assets/molecule.yml.template` throughout | [Pre Ansible-Native Configuration](https://docs.ansible.com/projects/molecule/pre-ansible-native/) | **Legacy but supported.** The docs class these as "pre ansible-native", "maintained for compatibility", with drivers "displaced by collections". No removal date is published. So the template is not broken, it is on the deprecated path, and the skill should say so. |
| `callback_whitelist: profile_tasks, timer, yaml` | `assets/molecule.yml.template:76` | `ansible-config validate -t all` | **NO.** Same removed key as the generator's `ansible.cfg`. Renamed `callbacks_enabled` in 2.11. |
| Platform images: `ubuntu:20.04`, `debian:11` | `assets/molecule.yml.template:33`, `:46` | — | Ubuntu 20.04 reached standard-support EOL in 2025; Debian 11 is on LTS-only. Testing a production role against two out-of-support bases is a false signal. |
| `references/playbook.md:507-521` "Test Sequence" lists `lint` as step 2 | `:510` | `molecule --help` action list: check, cleanup, converge, create, dependency, destroy, idempotence, prepare, side-effect, syntax, test, verify | **NO.** There is no `lint` action. `scripts/test_role.sh:146-147` knows this; the reference does not. |
| Source-map URLs | `references/source-map.md:14-15` | fetched | Both `ansible.readthedocs.io` URLs return **302 → docs.ansible.com**. `references/security_checklist.md:463` points at `docs.ansible.com/ansible/latest/user_guide/playbooks_best_practices.html`, a path from the pre-2021 documentation layout. |
| `mcp__context7__resolve-library-id` / `get-library-docs` as a documented lookup path | `references/playbook.md:604-612`, `:1078-1079`, `ansible-generator/references/playbook.md:328-331` | — | Unverifiable from here: an MCP server that may or may not be present. The skill states no fallback ordering that survives its absence beyond "Fallback to WebSearch" at `:662`. Should be phrased as capability-conditional. |

### 8. Router audit

`SKILL.md:41-48` lists six references inline with a one-line charter each — correct form, no
`00-topic-map.md` needed, `source-map.md` correctly present as a provenance ledger. No two-router
defect.

Defects:

1. **The router omits the skill's entire executable surface.** Ten scripts, 2,153 lines, zero mentions
   in `SKILL.md`. This is the single most consequential routing defect in the pair: the workflow at
   `SKILL.md:31-36` describes running `ansible-lint` and `--check --diff` by hand, which is precisely
   what the scripts exist to stop an agent from doing ad hoc.
2. **No trigger conditions.** `SKILL.md:43-48` gives descriptions, not conditions. The conditions do
   exist — `references/playbook.md:103-115` states exactly when each reference must be read — but they
   live inside the file they gate.
3. **`references/playbook.md` is a second body plus a second reference map.** `:6-43` duplicates the
   router; `:995-1013` is a second catalogue of references and assets.
4. **Neither trigger form for the sibling.** `SKILL.md:25` writes bare `` `ansible-generator` ``.
5. **The only boundary list is wrong.** `references/playbook.md:1107` names a non-existent skill and
   `:1106`/`:1108` name the same one twice.

### 9. Scripts and assets audit

Every script was copied to `/tmp` and executed against reconstructed fixtures (the originals were not
staged) plus the generator's role scaffold. Environment: Linux, bash 5, Python 3.11.15,
`ansible-core 2.19.11`, `ansible-lint 26.6.0`, `yamllint 1.38.0`, `molecule 26.6.0`, `checkov 3.3.8`.

**Exit-code matrix (measured).**

| Script | no args | `--help` | missing target | tool missing + no network | clean input | dirty input |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `validate_playbook.sh` | 1 | 1 | 1 | **1** | 0 | 1 |
| `validate_role.sh` | 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 | 0 | 1 |
| `validate_playbook_security.sh` | 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 | 0 | **1 (same as "could not run")** |
| `validate_role_security.sh` | 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 | 0 | 1 |
| `scan_secrets.sh` | 1 | 1 | 1 | n/a | 0 | 1 |
| `check_fqcn.sh` | 1 | 1 | 1 | n/a | **0** | **0** |
| `test_role.sh` | 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 | — | 1 |
| `setup_tools.sh` | 0 | 0 | 0 | n/a | 0 | 1 |
| `extract_ansible_info_wrapper.sh` | 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 | 0 | 1 |

**No script implements `--help`.** `--help` is consumed as the positional target and fails "target not
found". **No script has a self-test.** **No script distinguishes "could not run" from "found
problems"** — the sole exit code for both is 1.

The "could not run" case was tested deliberately, not treated as blocked. With `ansible-playbook`,
`ansible-lint` and `yamllint` shadowed off `PATH` and pip pointed at an unreachable index,
`validate_playbook.sh` printed `ERROR: No matching distribution found for wheel`, ran its cleanup trap,
and exited **1** with no summary block at all. An orchestrator reading only the exit code cannot tell
that from a lint failure.

**Per-script findings.**

**`validate_playbook.sh` (240 lines).** Windows: **no** (`$TEMP_VENV/bin/...` at `:48,57,112`).
`--help`: no. Self-test: no. Exit codes: 0 clean / 0 warnings-only / 1 errors / 1 could-not-run.
Behaviour is otherwise sound: against my reconstructed `good-playbook.yml` it passed all three stages;
against `bad-playbook.yml` it reported **19 ansible-lint failures** and exited 1. It hardcodes the
skill's own configs (`:142-146`, `:185-189`) with no override. It scans the whole containing directory
for nothing — that is the security script's defect, not this one's.

**`validate_role.sh` (357 lines).** Windows: **no** — `$TEMP_VENV/bin` plus `ln -s` at `:248` plus
unquoted `$YAML_FILES` at `:207`. `--help`: no. Self-test: no.

Its stage 2 is **logically inverted**. `:208` reads:

```
if ! $YAMLLINT_CMD $YAMLLINT_CONFIG "$file" 2>&1 | grep -v "warning  found forbidden document start"; then
    YAML_ERRORS=$((YAML_ERRORS + 1))
fi
```

The pipeline's status is `grep`'s, and `grep` exits 0 when it printed anything. Measured on three probe
files:

| Probe | true yamllint status | Script counts an error? |
|---|---|---|
| clean, no `---` | 0 (pass) | **yes** (false positive) |
| clean, with `---` | 0 (pass) | no |
| **syntactically broken, with `---`** | **1 (fail)** | **no** (false negative) |

Because every conventional Ansible file starts with `---`, and because the skill's own `.yamllint`
flags that `---` as a warning, the filename header line is always printed, `grep` always exits 0, and
**the YAML stage of role validation can never report a failure.** Run against the generator's
five-broken-file scaffold, the script printed five `syntax error` lines and then
`✓ YAML syntax check passed`.

`:283` runs `run_ansible_lint ... 2>&1` inside an `if`, so `LINT_EXIT=$?` at `:286` reads the exit of
the pipeline including the redirect — usable, but the same construct at `validate_playbook.sh:192-195`
is written without `2>&1`, so the two scripts differ where they claim to be identical.

**`validate_playbook_security.sh` (198 lines) / `validate_role_security.sh` (197 lines).** Windows:
**no**. `--help`: no. Self-test: no. `:45-101` and `:39-95` are byte-identical.

Confirmed working against a real Checkov: my `bad-playbook.yml` produced `Passed: 4, Failed: 4` and the
script correctly exited 1, listing `CKV_ANSIBLE_2` and `CKV2_ANSIBLE_2`.

Three defects. First, **`:111-114` silently widens the scan from the named file to its whole containing
directory**, so "validate this playbook" reports findings from every sibling file with no indication of
which file they came from. Second, **the failure branch at `:190` interpolates `$FAILED`, which is only
assigned inside the success branch at `:131`** — when Checkov cannot produce a report the user sees
`✗ Security validation failed with  security issue(s)`. Third, **Checkov's inability to run is reported
as a security failure**: against a directory containing no Ansible-parseable resources, Checkov printed
a proxy traceback (it tries to reach `api0.prismacloud.io/bridgecrew/api/v2/guidelines`) and no
`Passed checks:` line, so `:159` and `:162` fell through to `✗ Checkov scan failed`, exit 1. The
`No Ansible files found` branch at `:159` never fires because Checkov does not emit that string.

**`scan_secrets.sh` (190 lines).** Windows: yes under Git Bash (no venv path). `--help`: no. Self-test:
no. Exit 0 clean / 0 warnings-only / 1 secrets found — and 1 for "no argument" and 1 for "target not
found".

Against a planted fixture containing a plaintext password, an AWS access key ID, an AWS secret access
key, a GitHub-shaped token, a `postgres://user:pass@host` DSN and a `BEGIN RSA PRIVATE KEY` block:

| Planted secret | Caught? |
|---|---|
| `db_password: "hunter2-plaintext"` | **yes** (three times over: `:116`, `:144`, `check_password_values`) |
| `api_token: "ghp_…"` | yes (`:122`) |
| `aws_secret_access_key: "wJalr…"` | yes (`:128`) |
| `dsn: "postgres://svc:s3cr3t@…"` | yes (`:133`) |
| `-----BEGIN RSA PRIVATE KEY-----` | yes (`:137`) |
| **`aws_access_key_id: AKIAIOSFODNN7EXAMPLE`** | **NO** |
| **`another_key: "AKIAIOSFODNN7EXAMPLE"`** | **NO** |

Root cause: `scan_pattern` calls `grep -n -i "$pattern"` at `:65`/`:67` — **basic** regular expressions —
while the patterns at `:127` and `:129` are written in **extended** syntax. `AKIA[A-Z0-9]{16}` under BRE
matches a literal `{16}`; `aws_access_key_id:\s*['\"]?[A-Z0-9]` under BRE requires a literal `?`.
Verified directly: `grep -E` matches both lines, `grep` matches neither. The single highest-signal
credential shape in the entire pattern list is undetectable.

Symmetric defect: **it fails correct code.** `scan_pattern` has no `{{` / `vault_` / `!vault` filter —
that filtering exists only inside `check_password_values` at `:100`. A playbook whose every secret is
correctly indirected (`db_password: "{{ vault_db_password }}"`,
`api_token: "{{ lookup('env','API_TOKEN') }}"`, `no_log: true`) is reported as
`✗ Found 2 secret(s) and 1 warning(s)` and exits 1. A scanner that red-lights the correct pattern will
be switched off.

**`check_fqcn.sh` (252 lines).** Windows: yes under Git Bash; **fails on macOS system bash 3.2** because
`:157` uses `declare -A`. `--help`: no. Self-test: no. **Always exits 0** (`:224` and `:252`) — it can
never gate anything, and `:250-251` says so explicitly, contradicting `assets/.ansible-lint:40` which
enables `fqcn[action-core]` as an error.

Correctness: run against a fully FQCN-correct playbook it reported **two false positives** —
`group → ansible.builtin.group` matching the `group: root` *parameter* of `ansible.builtin.template`,
and `gather_facts → ansible.builtin.gather_facts` matching the `gather_facts: true` *play keyword*. The
grep at `:168` is line-oriented and structure-blind; `user:`, `command:`, `file:`, `service:`, `find:`,
`meta:` and `mount:` are all common parameter names that will fire the same way.

Efficiency: `:52-154` defines 59 + 26 + 9 = **94** module names and `:197-213` performs one full
`grep -r` traversal per name. One `yaml.safe_load` plus a key walk would be correct *and* two orders of
magnitude cheaper.

**`test_role.sh` (210 lines).** Windows: **no**. `--help`: no. Self-test: no. Exits 1 when the scenario
is missing (good) and 1 on stage failure.

Two structural defects. First, **`set -e` plus a `run_stage` that returns 1**: `:148`, `:149`, `:150`
and `:164` call `run_stage` as a bare command, so the first failing stage kills the script. The
idempotence block (`:153-160`), the verify stage (`:164`), the `molecule destroy` cleanup (`:169`) and
the entire summary (`:173-209`) never execute — so a failing `create` leaves the test containers
running and the user sees no summary. Reproduced with a minimal harness: only the first stage prints,
exit 1.

Second, **the idempotence check is a home-grown substring match on a capability Molecule provides
natively.** `:155` runs `molecule converge` again and greps for `changed=0`. Molecule has an
`idempotence` action (confirmed in `molecule --help`); the template even lists it in `test_sequence`
(`assets/molecule.yml.template:122`). With the shipped four-platform template, one host reporting
`changed=0` while three report `changed=5` satisfies `grep -q` and the script declares idempotence
passed. This is the standing preference "wrap the official capability" inverted.

Third, it never runs `prepare` even though the template declares `prepare: prepare.yml`
(`assets/molecule.yml.template:93`), and never runs `side_effect` or `cleanup`.

**`setup_tools.sh` (196 lines).** Windows: partially — the checks work under Git Bash, but the install
advice at `:129-191` branches on `$OSTYPE` for `darwin*` and `linux-gnu*` and falls through to a generic
`pip3 install` for everything else, so a Windows user gets no `py -m pip` guidance and no note about
`Scripts/` vs `bin/`. `--help`: no (arguments are ignored entirely). Self-test: no. Exit 0 if the six
required tools are present, 1 otherwise.

What it installs and from where: **PyPI only, via `pip3 install --upgrade`** — `ansible`, `ansible-lint`,
`yamllint`, `checkov`, optionally `molecule molecule-docker` (`:134`, `:151`, `:161`, `:171`, `:178`,
`:187`, and the molecule line at `:141`/`:154`/`:164`/`:174`/`:181`/`:190`). On macOS it also offers
`brew install ansible ansible-lint yamllint` (`:137`). **Nothing is pinned** — not a floor, not a
ceiling, not a hash. Note the asymmetry with the scripts: `setup_tools.sh` installs `ansible` (the
community package, currently 14.2.0, ~1 GB of collections) while every auto-venv installs `ansible-core`
(`validate_playbook.sh:116`). The two paths give different collection availability, which changes
whether `fqcn` and `syntax-check` findings appear at all.

It also documents minimum versions nowhere and enforces them nowhere, while
`references/playbook.md:801-806` states four floors. This is the clearest instance of defect class 13 in
the skill: the version floor is a preference.

**`extract_ansible_info_wrapper.sh` (39 lines) / `extract_ansible_info.py` (274 lines).** Windows:
wrapper **no** (`$TEMP_VENV/bin/activate` at `:33`); the Python is portable (`pathlib`, no
`parents[N]`, resolves nothing from `__file__` except via the wrapper's `SCRIPT_DIR`). `--help`: no.
Self-test: no. `:27` uses `trap "rm -rf $TEMP_VENV" EXIT` with the variable expanded at trap-definition
time and unquoted — safe here because `mktemp` output has no spaces, but it is the wrong idiom.

The Python is the most reliable assertion in the skill (it correctly found and reported all five
unparseable files in the generator's scaffold, exit 1) and simultaneously carries an inverted
classification. `:28-37` lists `BUILTIN_MODULES` as **short** names; `:210-220` adds **FQCN** strings to
`self.modules`; `:248-249` then computes `builtin_modules` and `custom_modules` by testing FQCN strings
against the short-name set. Measured:

- On a correct, fully-FQCN playbook: `builtin_modules: []`, `custom_modules: ["ansible.builtin.apt",
  "ansible.builtin.package", "ansible.builtin.service", "ansible.builtin.template"]`.
- On the bad playbook, which uses only unqualified builtins: `modules: []` — because `:223` filters
  short names that are in `BUILTIN_MODULES`.

So the tool reports "four custom modules" for the best input and "no modules at all" for the worst.
Since `references/playbook.md:573-687` triggers an expensive Context7/WebSearch documentation lookup on
"custom modules detected", this defect turns every clean playbook into a web-research task and every
non-FQCN playbook into a silent pass.

Also `:195-198` puts `block`, `rescue` and `always` in the *skip* list at the top of the key loop, then
`:202-207` tries to recurse into them — the `continue` at `:199` fires first, so block/rescue/always
bodies are never walked. Modules inside a `block:` are invisible to the extractor.

**Assets.**

| Asset | Windows | Valid today | Finding |
|---|---|---|---|
| `assets/.ansible-lint` (123 lines) | n/a | Accepted (exit 0) | `:53` `task_name_prefix: "{path}:"` kills `NameRule` silently — the headline defect. `:38-43` and `:46-50` list `name[casing]` twice. `:22` excludes the skill's own `test/`. `:106` sets an option for an opt-in rule that is never enabled. 66 of 123 lines are commented-out catalogue. |
| `assets/.yamllint` (92 lines) | n/a | Valid | `:56` permits `yes`/`no` where ansible-lint forbids them. `:60-61` forbids the `---` that every Ansible file has. Both contradictions verified by execution. |
| `assets/molecule.yml.template` (154 lines) | n/a | Loads, driver fails | `:13-14` `driver: docker` fails on a stock molecule install. `:76` `callback_whitelist` is a removed key. `:107-110` `lint:` is dead. `:33`/`:46` test against out-of-support base images. |
| `test/README.md` (384 lines) | n/a | — | See below. |

**The `geerlingguy.mysql` fixture — verdict.** The role directory was not staged, so I judge it from
what the skill says about it. `test/README.md:51-70` names the role, gives its upstream URL
(`:55`), and states what it exemplifies. That is provenance of a sort. It fails the bar on four counts:

1. **No pin.** No commit, no tag, no version, no `ansible-galaxy` requirement entry. A vendored copy
   with no pin cannot be refreshed reproducibly and cannot be diffed against upstream.
2. **No refresh rule.** Nothing says when it is re-synced, by whom, or what a drift from upstream means
   for the tests that depend on it.
3. **No licence artefact.** `geerlingguy.mysql` is MIT; vendoring ~100 KB of it into another repository
   without carrying the LICENSE file is a redistribution defect, not a test-fixture question.
4. **It is unreferenced by anything executable.** Grepping the whole skill for `geerlingguy`,
   `test/roles`, `test/playbooks`, `good-playbook` or `bad-playbook` returns hits **only** inside
   `test/README.md`. `SKILL.md` does not mention `test/`; `references/playbook.md` does not; there is no
   runner script; and `assets/.ansible-lint:22` actively excludes `test/` from directory scans. The
   fixture is documented, not used.

Compounding this, `test/README.md:165-198` instructs the reader to add *more* vendored upstream roles by
`git clone` — eight named repositories — which converts an already-unpinned fixture into a growth
policy.

**Verdict: it is 100 KB of someone else's code shipped by accident that has been retro-fitted with a
paragraph of prose.** The intent behind it was legitimate — a real, well-built role is a genuinely good
integration fixture — but a fixture nobody runs, nobody pins, and the lint config excludes is
indistinguishable from residue. Nominate the vendored tree for retirement and replace it with a pinned
`ansible-galaxy` install in a fixture-fetch script; keep the two playbooks (once they are restored) as
first-party fixtures with an expected-findings file.

**One further finding about `test/`: the fixtures do not exist in the staged tree.**
`test/playbooks/good-playbook.yml` and `test/playbooks/bad-playbook.yml` are documented at
`test/README.md:19-49` and invoked at `:78-90`, `:111-114`; neither file is present. Every command in
`test/README.md` that names a playbook is currently a broken instruction.

**What the shipped toolchain actually catches, measured against `test/README.md:35-47`.** The README
claims eleven defect classes in `bad-playbook.yml`. Using my reconstruction:

| Claimed defect | Caught by | Verdict |
|---|---|---|
| Hardcoded password | `scan_secrets.sh` only | caught |
| Tasks without names | nothing — `name[missing]` is disabled by `assets/.ansible-lint:53` | **missed** |
| Using shell instead of modules | `command-instead-of-module` | caught |
| Missing `changed_when` | `no-changed-when` ×2 | caught |
| Command injection (unquoted variables) | nothing — verified passing under `profile: production` | **missed** |
| Missing file permissions | `risky-file-permissions` ×2 | caught |
| Deprecated `with_items` | nothing — and it is not deprecated | **missed / claim false** |
| Handler name mismatch | nothing static; `--syntax-check` passes, ansible-lint passes; only a real run errors | **missed** |
| Overly permissive `777` | `yaml[octal-values]` fires on the *unquoted* form only; quoted `mode: '0777'` and `mode: '0666'` on a secret both pass | **effectively missed** |
| Disabled SSL verification | Checkov `CKV_ANSIBLE_2` — but only if the separate security script is run | caught, conditionally |
| Missing OS conditionals | nothing | **missed** |

**Five of eleven advertised defect classes are caught, one is caught only by a second script, one claim
is factually wrong, and four are missed outright** — including the two that matter most for a
security-sensitive service: command injection and unnamed tasks.

### 10. Rewrite brief for Phase 2

**Target body size:** 2,600–2,900 bytes (currently 2,106). It must grow, because it must name the
scripts and the seams. This is the one skill in the pair where body growth is the correct answer.

**Body structure (`SKILL.md`):**

```
frontmatter           keep; description is 484 chars and compliant
# Ansible Validator
## Overview           4 lines
## Source freshness   2 lines
## When NOT to use    4 lines
## What to run        NEW, 8 lines — the script table: command, what it asserts, exit contract
## Workflow           6 steps, each naming the script that performs it
## Boundaries         NEW, 6 lines — the seam sentences from §4, with /name and $name
## Reference map      6 entries, each with a trigger condition
```

**Reference file list after the rewrite:**

| File | Charter | Trigger |
|---|---|---|
| `references/playbook.md` | The validation procedure and the tool-by-tool detail: lint, syntax, check mode, Checkov, Molecule. | Read when running a full validation. |
| `references/failure-classes.md` | **NEW.** The recovery content from `references/common_errors.md` and `references/playbook.md:814-869`, restructured by failure class: symptom, diagnosis command, smallest retry, escalation. | Read when a check fails. |
| `references/best_practices.md` | The ruleset a finding is measured against. Rules only. | Read when a warning needs a remediation citation. |
| `references/module_alternatives.md` | Short-name → FQCN and removed-module → replacement mapping, current against ansible-core 2.21. | Read on an FQCN or deprecation finding. |
| `references/security_checklist.md` | The security predicates, each paired with the command that evaluates it. | Read on any security finding. |
| `references/molecule.md` | **NEW.** Everything Molecule, extracted from `references/playbook.md:462-571` plus the driver-model migration. | Read only when a `molecule/` directory exists. |
| `references/source-map.md` | Provenance ledger. Keep; update the two readthedocs URLs. | Read before any version claim. |

**Content moves:**

1. `references/playbook.md:6-43` → delete (duplicates `SKILL.md`).
2. `references/playbook.md:45-115` (the 70-line ASCII tree) → replace with an eight-row table: stage,
   command, what it asserts, exit meaning, reference on failure.
3. `references/playbook.md:462-571` → move to `references/molecule.md`.
4. `references/playbook.md:814-869` + `references/common_errors.md` in full → merge into
   `references/failure-classes.md`, deduplicated. `common_errors.md:670-727` is a copy of
   `best_practices.md:804-841` and only one may survive.
5. `references/playbook.md:995-1013` → delete; the router owns the catalogue.
6. The molecule doctrine, currently at `:14-17`, `:82-87`, `:466-467`, `:1049-1054`, `:1116-1117` →
   state once, in `references/molecule.md`, in the rewritten form from §6 item 1.
7. The dual-scan doctrine at `:95-102`, `:309-315`, `:974-975` → state once, in
   `references/security_checklist.md`, and correct it: the pair is Checkov `--framework ansible,secrets`
   plus the grep scanner, not Checkov-ansible plus grep.
8. `references/best_practices.md:152-176` (the correct variable-precedence table) is the pair's single
   source of truth; `ansible-generator/references/best-practices.md:138-148` routes to it.
9. `references/playbook.md:1102-1108` → replace with the six seam sentences from §4. Delete
   `terraform-validator`.

**Files nominated for retirement** (move to `_to_delete/2026-07-29-batch7/`):

- `test/roles/geerlingguy.mysql/` — the whole vendored tree, replaced by a pinned fetch script
  (`scripts/fetch_fixtures.sh` calling `ansible-galaxy role install geerlingguy.mysql,<version> -p test/roles`).
- `references/common_errors.md` — retired *as a file* after its content merges into
  `references/failure-classes.md`. Nothing is lost; the three fabricated claims (`required` filter ×2,
  the `with_items` deprecation warning) are corrected in transit.

Nothing else. Every script is repairable and none should be deleted.

**Repairs that are not moves:**

- `assets/.ansible-lint:53`: `{path}` → `{stem}`, or delete the key. This single line restores
  `name[missing]` and `name[casing]`. Remove the duplicate `name[casing]` from `warn_list:47`. Remove
  `test/` from `exclude_paths:22`, or scope it to `exclude_paths: ['test/roles/']`.
- `assets/.yamllint:56`: drop `yes`,`no`,`on`,`off` from `allowed-values`. `:60-61`: set
  `document-start: present: true`. Then delete the compensating `grep -v` at `validate_role.sh:208`.
- `scripts/validate_role.sh:203-211`: rewrite the YAML stage as
  `if ! $YAMLLINT_CMD $CONFIG "$file" >/dev/null 2>&1; then YAML_ERRORS=$((YAML_ERRORS+1)); fi`, and
  quote the loop (`while IFS= read -r -d '' file` over `find -print0`).
- `scripts/scan_secrets.sh:65,67`: `grep -n -i -E`. Add the `{{` / `vault_` / `!vault` / `lookup(`
  filter to `scan_pattern`, not only to `check_password_values`. Add a `--allow-vaulted` default.
- `scripts/check_fqcn.sh`: replace the 94-grep loop with a single YAML walk that inspects task keys
  only. Keep the migration table output. Decide whether FQCN is an error or advice and make the exit
  code say so — currently the script and `assets/.ansible-lint:40` disagree.
- `scripts/test_role.sh:148-164`: `run_stage "x" || true` with an explicit failure tally, so the
  summary and `molecule destroy` always run; replace the `changed=0` grep at `:155` with
  `molecule idempotence -s "$SCENARIO"`; add `prepare` to the sequence.
- `scripts/validate_playbook_security.sh:111-118`: scan the named file, not its directory (Checkov
  accepts `-f`). `:190`: initialise `FAILED=0` at the top. `:159-165`: add an explicit
  "Checkov produced no report" branch that exits **2**.
- All six venv bootstraps: resolve `bin` vs `Scripts` (`[ -d "$TEMP_VENV/Scripts" ] && VBIN=Scripts || VBIN=bin`),
  and cache the venv under a stable path keyed by a pinned requirements hash instead of rebuilding it
  per invocation.
- `scripts/setup_tools.sh`: install `ansible-core` (not `ansible`) to match the auto-venvs; replace
  `molecule-docker` with `molecule-plugins[docker]`; add a `requirements.txt` with floors
  (`ansible-core>=2.20`, `ansible-lint>=26.0`, `yamllint>=1.35`, `molecule>=25.0`, `checkov>=3.3`) and
  compare the detected versions against them, exiting 1 when a floor is not met.
- `scripts/extract_ansible_info.py:248-249`: classify by resolving the FQCN's collection prefix, not by
  membership in a short-name set. `:195-207`: move the `block`/`rescue`/`always` recursion above the
  `continue`.
- `references/security_checklist.md:270` delete `warn: false`; `:442` `--profile security` → `safety`;
  `:447` delete the fabricated `ansible-galaxy collection scan`; `:463` update the dead user_guide URL.
- `references/best_practices.md:208`, `references/common_errors.md:203,374`: `required` → `mandatory`.
- `references/common_errors.md:468-472`: delete the fabricated deprecation warning; state that `with_*`
  is discouraged and `loop` is preferred, with no claim of deprecation.
- `references/common_errors.md:299`: replace the plaintext `ansible_ssh_pass` example with a vault
  lookup. `:322`: scope the sudoers line to specific commands.
- `assets/molecule.yml.template`: `callback_whitelist` → `callbacks_enabled`; delete the `lint:` key;
  refresh platform images to supported bases; add a comment stating that `driver`/`platforms` are the
  pre-ansible-native path and naming `molecule-plugins[docker]` as the current driver package.
- Every cross-skill call site gains both trigger forms.

**Executable checks the skill should ship and does not:**

1. **`test/run_fixtures.sh` (plus a Python driver for Windows).** Restore `good-playbook.yml` and
   `bad-playbook.yml`, add `expected-findings.txt` for each, and assert: the good playbook exits 0 on
   every script; the bad playbook produces *exactly* the expected rule IDs. This is the check that
   would have caught the `{path}` defect, the inverted YAML stage, and the `check_fqcn.sh` false
   positives — all three are visible the moment a fixture has an expected-output file.
2. **`scripts/self_test.sh`** — run every script with no arguments, with `--help`, with a nonexistent
   path, and with a valid path while the required tool is shadowed off `PATH`; assert the four exit
   codes are distinct (0 clean / 1 findings / 2 could-not-run / 64 usage). Today all four are 1.
3. **`--help` on all ten scripts**, and a shared `lib/common.sh` holding the venv bootstrap, the colour
   constants and the summary block — approximately 300 duplicated lines collapse into one file.
4. **`scripts/check_assets.sh`** — validate `assets/.ansible-lint` and `assets/.yamllint` by running
   the tools against a two-file fixture and asserting the expected findings appear. A config that
   silently disables a rule is exactly what this catches.
5. **A `--format json` / `--sarif` mode on the two aggregate scripts**, wrapping ansible-lint's existing
   `-f sarif` rather than reimplementing it, so findings can reach a gate and an aggregator. This also
   closes criterion 4.
6. **A secrets check that wraps Checkov's `secrets` framework** instead of competing with it:
   `checkov -f <file> --framework ansible,secrets`. Measured, that framework alone catches both AWS key
   shapes and the private key that the grep scanner misses. Keep the grep scanner for the
   Ansible-specific shapes Checkov does not model (`vault_` conventions, `no_log` absence), and state
   that division in one sentence.
7. **A permissions check and an injection check**, because nothing in the current toolchain reports
   `mode: '0777'` or `{{ var }}` interpolated into `shell:`. Both are single-pass YAML walks:
   fail on any `mode` string whose last digit permits write to other, and on any `command`/`shell`
   free-form or `cmd` value containing `{{` without `| quote`.

---

### Shared closing

#### The overlap, measured

Both directories were compared file by file after normalising whitespace and dropping comments and
lines under 13 characters.

| Generator side | Validator side | Identical content lines | Topic overlap |
|---|---|---|---|
| `references/best-practices.md` (895 lines) | `references/best_practices.md` (860 lines) | 36 (9.3% of the generator file) | **14 of 16 sections** |
| `references/playbook.md` (813 lines) | `references/playbook.md` (1,125 lines) | 3 | low — these are genuinely different documents |
| `references/module-patterns.md` (1,463 lines) | `references/module_alternatives.md` (216 lines) | 24 | complementary, not duplicative |
| `references/source-map.md` (24 lines) | `references/source-map.md` (24 lines) | 7 (53.8%) | **near-total** |
| `assets/templates/project/.ansible-lint` (267 lines) | `assets/.ansible-lint` (123 lines) | — | same purpose, incompatible content |

**The `best-practices` pair, section by section.** Fourteen subjects are taught twice:

| Subject | Generator | Validator |
|---|---|---|
| Project directory structure | `best-practices.md:3-32` | `best_practices.md:10-43` |
| Role structure | `best-practices.md:34-44` | `best_practices.md:45-71` |
| Task naming | `best-practices.md:61-64` | `best_practices.md:73-132` |
| Variable naming | `best-practices.md:54-59` | `best_practices.md:136-150` |
| Variable precedence | `best-practices.md:138-148` | `best_practices.md:152-176` |
| defaults vs vars | `best-practices.md:42-43` | `best_practices.md:178-192` |
| `default()` / required values | `best-practices.md:152-156` | `best_practices.md:194-209` |
| Idempotency | `best-practices.md:99-118` | `best_practices.md:211-292` |
| Module selection priority | `best-practices.md:890-895` | `best_practices.md:294-332` |
| Error handling (block/rescue, `failed_when`, `changed_when`) | `best-practices.md:119-135` | `best_practices.md:334-416` |
| Conditionals and loops | `best-practices.md:164-205` | `best_practices.md:417-500` |
| Templates and Jinja filters | `best-practices.md:232-641` | `best_practices.md:502-569` |
| Tags | `best-practices.md:675-701` | `best_practices.md:571-626` |
| Handlers | `best-practices.md:206-231` | `best_practices.md:628-670` |
| Documentation | `best-practices.md:832-875` | `best_practices.md:708-798` |
| Performance | `best-practices.md:779-830` | `best_practices.md:804-841` |

Two subjects are **not** duplicated: the generator's 377-line Jinja/lookup deep dive
(`best-practices.md:265-641`) has no counterpart, and the validator's check-mode section
(`best_practices.md:672-706`) has only a two-line stub on the generator side
(`best-practices.md:744-757`).

**And on one duplicated subject the two files disagree.** Variable precedence:
`ansible-generator/references/best-practices.md:138-148` gives a nine-item high-to-low list that places
`set_fact`/registered vars *below* task, block and role vars;
`ansible-validator/references/best_practices.md:152-176` gives the correct 21-item low-to-high list in
which `set_fact` outranks all three. An agent that reads both gets contradictory guidance on the single
question most likely to produce a wrong-value bug. There is a second, softer contradiction:
`ansible-generator/assets/templates/project/ansible.cfg:27` sets `fact_caching_timeout = 3600` while
`ansible-validator/references/best_practices.md:818` and `references/common_errors.md:692` both prescribe
`86400`.

**The `.ansible-lint` pair is the sharpest overlap.** Both ship one; they are not variants of a shared
baseline; they configure the same tool for the same repository with different, mutually exclusive
settings; and — measured — the generator's is **rejected by ansible-lint (exit 3)** while the
validator's **silently disables the two rules it explicitly enables**. Whichever config an agent
happens to pick determines the result. That is not two skills disagreeing about scope; it is one
artefact forked and both forks broken.

**Two source maps.** Seven of thirteen content lines are identical. The generator's adds the playbook,
inventory and vault guides; the validator's adds the ansible-playbook CLI page, ansible-test and
Checkov. Both carry the same two stale readthedocs hostnames.

#### Two skills or one?

**Recommendation: keep two skills, and move the shared ground into the validator.**

I came to this the long way, because the merge case is real and this repository has already made it
once. `alaa-k8s-helm/SKILL.md:8` says in its own words: "The skill is designed to replace separate
generator, validator, and debugger skills while keeping context usage low through routing-first
instructions." That is the same domain shape — author, validate and debug one declarative
infrastructure artefact — resolved by merger, with task lanes (`alaa-k8s-helm/SKILL.md:13-19`,
`:41-70`) doing the discrimination that separate skills would otherwise do. If a merge were ever
justified, this pair is where.

Three things decide it the other way.

**First, the discrimination that exists here actually works, and a merge destroys a working asset to
solve a problem that is not a triggering problem.** Each description states the other as its negative
clause — `ansible-generator/SKILL.md:3` "Do not use to validate or debug existing Ansible with no
generation need (use `ansible-validator`)", `ansible-validator/SKILL.md:3` "Do not use to author new
Ansible from scratch (use `ansible-generator`)". Those two sentences are, on the evidence of this
batch, the best pair of reciprocal negative clauses in the repository. The defects I found are not
mis-triggering defects. Not one of the 40-odd findings above is caused by an agent picking the wrong
skill. They are caused by broken templates, an inverted grep, a bad config key and stale versions —
every one of which survives a merge unchanged. Merging would pay the full cost of destroying working
discrimination and buy nothing.

**Second, the two halves have genuinely different failure modes and different consumers.** The
generator has zero executables and its risk is that it emits plausible-looking YAML that does not
parse. The validator has 2,153 lines of executables and its risk is that a check reports clean when it
could not run. They are also invoked at different times by different actors: the generator by a human
scaffolding a new role, the validator by CI on every merge request, headless, where the only thing that
matters is the exit code. A merged skill would have to carry both an authoring workflow and an
exit-code contract in one always-loaded body, and the CI consumer would load the entire authoring
surface it never uses. The k8s-helm merge works because a chart author and a chart validator are the
same person at the same terminal; an Ansible validator is a CI job.

**Third, the merge would not survive the new-skill scrutiny the brief demands.** The question a merge
must answer is: what does the merged skill do that neither can do today? The honest answer is
"deduplicate `best_practices`" — and that is achievable without a merge, by making the validator the
owner of the shared ruleset and having the generator route to it. Deduplication is not a reason to
merge; it is a reason to assign ownership.

**The one-sentence deciding test an agent applies:**

> **Does a file that does not yet exist have to exist when you are done? If yes, `ansible-generator`
> (`/ansible-generator`, `$ansible-generator`). If every file you need already exists and your job is
> to produce a verdict about it, `ansible-validator` (`/ansible-validator`, `$ansible-validator`).**

That test resolves the awkward cases correctly. "Fix the lint errors in this role" — no new file, the
job is a verdict plus edits driven by that verdict — validator. "Add a handler to this role" — a new
block that does not exist yet — generator, which then hands to the validator. "Review this playbook for
security" — verdict — validator. "Convert these shell scripts into a role" — new files — generator.
Both skills must state this sentence, in the same words, in their `## When NOT to use` section, because
a discrimination test written on one side only is a test the other side has not agreed to.

**Ownership of the duplicated subjects.** For each subject taught twice, exactly one owner, and the
other routes:

| Subject | Owner | The other side |
|---|---|---|
| The best-practice **ruleset** (structure, naming, idempotency, module selection, error handling, conditionals, tags, handlers, documentation, performance) | **`ansible-validator` `references/best_practices.md`** — because a rule is only real if something reports its violation, and only the validator ships checkers | `ansible-generator` deletes its overlapping sections and routes: "The ruleset this skill generates against is `ansible-validator references/best_practices.md`. Do not restate it here." |
| **Variable precedence** | **`ansible-validator` `references/best_practices.md:152-176`** (it is the correct table) | generator deletes `best-practices.md:138-148` and routes |
| **Jinja filters, lookups, template control structures, macros** | **`ansible-generator`**, moved to a new `references/jinja-and-lookups.md` — this is authoring knowledge, and the validator has no rule that depends on it | validator routes on a `jinja[...]` finding |
| **Module patterns — how to call a module correctly** | **`ansible-generator` `references/module-patterns.md`** | validator routes on an `args`/`no-free-form` finding |
| **Module alternatives — what a name maps to now** | **`ansible-validator` `references/module_alternatives.md`** | generator routes when it needs a current FQCN, instead of maintaining a second mapping inside `module-patterns.md` |
| **`.ansible-lint` and `.yamllint`** | **`ansible-validator` `assets/`** — one config, one owner | generator retires `assets/templates/project/.ansible-lint` and its project scaffold *copies* the validator's, with a one-line comment naming the owner |
| **Molecule** | **`ansible-validator`**, new `references/molecule.md` | generator routes; it should not scaffold a `molecule/` directory it cannot test |
| **Security predicates and Ansible Vault mechanics** | **`ansible-validator` `references/security_checklist.md`** | generator routes; it keeps only the authoring-time rules (`no_log` placement, `mode` values at write time) and cites the checklist line |
| **Source map** | **shared, but not forked** — one `references/source-map.md` owned by `ansible-validator`, with the generator's three extra URLs merged in | generator's copy retires; it routes |
| **Check mode** | **`ansible-validator`** | generator states only "generate tasks that support check mode" and routes for the procedure |
| **The `test/` corpus** | **`ansible-validator`** | generator gains its own small fixture: one scaffolded role that must pass `validate_role.sh` |

Net effect: `ansible-generator/references/best-practices.md` shrinks from 895 lines to roughly 200 (the
authoring-only rules) plus a new ~380-line `jinja-and-lookups.md`; `ansible-validator` gains nothing in
size but gains sole ownership of every rule it can test. Total bytes across the pair fall; every
instruction is stated once; and the trigger discrimination that works today is untouched.

#### Defects in a live repository

None. Neither `service-ci-kit` nor `service-runtime-kit` is touched by this lane; no Ansible content
appears in either.

#### Out-of-batch findings

- `ansible-validator/references/playbook.md:1107` names **`terraform-validator`**, which does not exist
  anywhere in `/mnt/user-data/uploads/skills/skills/sohrab/`. Recorded for the in-batch fix (deleting
  the pointer is in scope for Appendix H §10); no out-of-batch skill needs changing.
- `alaa-k8s-helm/SKILL.md:8` is the repository's own precedent for merging a generator/validator/debugger
  triple, and `alaa-k8s-helm/SKILL.md:153` already uses the settled dual trigger form
  `/alaa-prompting-guide` (`$alaa-prompting-guide`) that this pair lacks entirely. Both are cited above
  as evidence. **No edit to `alaa-k8s-helm` is proposed.**
- Neither `alaa-docker-production`, `alaa-k8s-helm`, `alaa-gitlab-ci-cd`, `alaa-haproxy`,
  `alaa-makefile`, `caas-arvan-kuber` nor `alaa-frontend-devops` contains the string "ansible". The
  reciprocal seam is unstated on both sides. Those six are in batch 7 and their halves are other lanes'
  work; I record the gap without proposing text for them beyond what my own two skills should say.

#### Is a new skill required?

**No.**

I looked hardest at two candidate gaps. The first is the stack-versus-platform hole: decision D8 covers
CI, containers and the proxy, and Ansible is a fourth delivery mechanism the ruling does not name. That
is a real gap, but it is a *ruling* gap, not a skill gap — it is closed by these two skills stating the
seam sentences in §4 of each appendix and the three platform skills stating their halves, which batch 7
is already committed to doing. No new owner is needed for a boundary that four existing owners can each
describe from their own side.

The second is secrets. Ansible Vault is a substantial, Ansible-specific body of mechanics — vault IDs,
`encrypt_string`, rekeying, `no_log` interaction with `--diff`, the difference between an encrypted file
and an encrypted variable — and none of it is properly covered today
(`references/security_checklist.md:64-85` is five bullets). But it fits inside `ansible-validator`
without straining that skill's boundary: verifying that a secret is vaulted is a validation act, and the
authoring counterpart is one section in the generator. The fail-closed *decision* already has an owner
in `alaa-security-review`. Splitting Vault into its own skill would create a third member of a pair that
already has a trigger-discrimination story working, and would have to justify itself against the same
scrutiny a merge would face. It cannot.

Everything else I found is a repair, not a missing capability: broken templates, an inverted grep, an
invalid config key, a BRE/ERE bug, four stale version floors, three fabricated commands, and a fixture
tree nobody runs. Not one of them describes work that no existing skill can own.
